THE WILLIAMS TYPEWRITER An Unsolicited Testimonial. 

SIR,

I see on your front page "This space is reserved for the announcement of the famous Williams Typewriter. Visible Writing, Simplicity, Pad Inking.

Some few months since I studied every typewriter on the market: 

I bought one of Williams. It is a beautiful little instrument, and has written hundreds of thousands of words, and goes lighter and better than ever. 

For cheapness, facility and easy terms of payment, let me commend it. A voluntary and unsought testimonial is sometimes pleasing to the recipient. 

You know my handwriting so well that I need only subscribe myself.

NORTHUMBRIAN.

CHURCH OF ENGLAND PULPIT." LONDON.

THE SHAKESPEARE PROBLEM RESTATED. 

GREENWOOD, M.P. Demy 8vo. (9 by 5 inches). 21s. net. 

Scholars admit that a large portion of the dramas contained in the First Folio of 1623 did not emanate from the pen of Shakespeare. the author of these plays really William Shakespeare, "the Stratford rustic," or did the name conceal the identity of some spirit of a different sort"? In the present work the author restates the arguments for the negative case, viz., that the Stratford player was not the real Shakespeare. He claims that such a proposition is worthy of serious consideration from open-minded and unprejudiced thinkers. 

THE DAUGHTER OF LOUIS XVI.: Marie-Thérèse-Charlotte of France, Duchesse D'Angoulême. By G. LENOTRE. With thirteen full page Illustrations. Demy 8vo. (9 by 5 inches). IOS. 6d. net.

M. G. Lenotre is perhaps the most widely read of a group of modern 

French writers who have succeeded in treating history from a point of view at once scientific, dramatic, and popular. He has made the Revolution his particular field of research, and deals not only with the most prominent figures of that period, but with many minor characters whose life-stories are quite as thrilling as anything in fiction.

THE KING'S GENERAL IN THE WEST, being the Life of Sir Richard Grenville, Baronet (1600-1659). By ROGER GRANVILLE, M.A., Sub-Dean of Exeter Cathedral. With Illustrations. Demy 8vo (9 by 

5 inches). IOS. 6d. net.

THE PATH TO PARIS. The Record of a Riverside Journey from Le Havre to Paris. By FRANK RUTTER. With sixty-two Illustrations by HANSLIP FLETCHER. Demy 8vo. (9 by 5 inches). IOS. 6d. net. 

Here the author records a journey along the banks of the Seine from Le Havre to Paris, his impressions of the people he meets and the places he visits, his adventures on the road, and the historic and artistic associations in which the localities abound.

Cheques should be made payable to the British Institute of Social Service.

OBJECTS OF THE INSTITUTE. 

To collect, register, and disseminate information relating to all forms o Social Service in order :- (1) To make such information available to all concerned in the improvement and elevation of our national life; (2) To promote the initiation and development in this country of the most beneficial and successful forms of Social Service; (3) To give assistance to all organisations that have social aims, and to facilitate cooperation between them; and, as far as possible, to prevent overlapping. 

SCOPE OF THE INSTITUTE. 

Some idea of the forms of Social Service at home and abroad regarding which the Institute collects and disseminates the best experience may be gathered from the following list:- Infant Mortality.-Prompt Registration of Births; Maternity Instruction ; Milk 

Depôts, Crèches.

Child Life. Meals; Clothing; Country Holidays; Labour Restrictions; Juvenile Courts; 

Medical Inspection; Visiting Invalid Children. 

Education. Physical, Hygienic, Domestic; Trade Schools; Apprenticeship Com mittees; Special, Country Recovery, and Vacation Schools; Play Centres; Arts and Crafts; Libraries, Reading Circles,

Advertisements Labour Conditions. — Trade Unions; Dangerous Trades; Safety Appliances; 

Women's Work; Sweating. 

Unemployment.-Labour Bureaux ; Labour Colonies; Vacant Lot Cultivation Emigration. 

Industrial Betterment.-Sanitary Conditions; Factory Dressing Rooms; Dining Rooms, Reading and Recreation Rooms; Social Secretaries; Profit Sharing; Pension 

Systems; Thrift Societies; Sick Funds; Savings Banks.

Social Centres.-Settlements; Institutional Churches; Clubs and Brigades; Social 

Institutes. 

Temperance.—Attractions to compete with Public Houses; Disinterested Manage ment; Public House Trusts. 

Co-operation.-Credit Banks; Loan Societies; Benefit Societies; Building, Farming, Manufacturing, and Distributing; Housekeeping.

Municipal Enterprises.—Experiences in Public Ownership. 

Public Health.-Sanitation; Milk Inspection; Smoke Abatement; Health Visiting, &c. 

Land.—Allotments; Small Holdings; Rural Depopulation; Village Handicrafts. 

Housing.-Town Planning; Overcrowding; Statistics; Experiences; etc. 

Garden Cities.—Garden Villages and Suburbs ; Urban Aesthetics; Provision of Parks; 

Window Gardening, etc. 

Poor Relief. Vagrancy; Elberfeld System ; Guilds of Help ; Old Age Pensions; State

Insurance; Co-ordination of Voluntary Effort. 

Criminology.-Probationary Methods; Borstal System; Physical Development; 

Prison Experiments. 

Publication." PROGRESS: CIVIC, SOCIAL, INDUSTRIAL," the organ of the Institute, is published quarterly, price Sixpence. It is intended to appeal to all who are interested in social service and the betterment of life and labour conditions. "PROGRESS" will thus stimulate its readers to carry their inquiries beyond the limited extent to which they can be dealt with in its pages, and also to contribute the results of their own social experiences; while it will also serve as the distributor of much of the information as to betterment movements and experiments gathered in the Library of the Institute from originating points in all civilised countries. 

Membership in the Institute may be obtained by sending a subscription of not less than one guinea per annum to the Secretary, but it is hoped that those interested in the Institute will remember that this is the minimum subscription and that those who can afford to do so will make contributions in much larger amounts. Membership in the Institute is of three kinds, classified as follows:- (a) LIFE MEMBERS, being the Founders of the Society, and all who shall at any time have contributed not less than £250 in one or more sums to the Institute. (b) SUBSCRIBING MEMBERS, who shall be those who shall subscribe not less than one guinea per annum to the Institute. (c) HONORARY MEMBERS, who shall be those persons who are co-opted by the 

Executive Committee. 

The efficient working of the Institute can only be maintained either by a substantial endowment such as is enjoyed by the Musée Social of Paris, and the Humanitarian Society of Milan; or, as in America, where a sum of £3,000 a year has been guaranteed, by wealthy and philanthropic individuals or firms desiring to promote social effort.

It is estimated that at least £350 more than has been already promised will' be required to enable the Institute to pay its way for 1908.

COUNTY BOROUGH OF DUDLEY GUILD OF HELP SECRETARY. 

"HE CENTRAL BOARD invite applications from gentlemen not less than 25 years be started in and for the County Borough of Dudley. The duties will be those usually attaching to such a position. The estimated population of the Borough is 53,000 -chiefly industrial-area 3,546 acres, and the proportion of persons in receipt of relief to the population, 1 in 30. Applicants, who are requested to state salary required, must be thoroughly acquainted with the objects of "Guild of Help or kindred systems, be keenly interested in the fulfilment of such objects, and must be good organisers. 

Persons having had actual experience in such secretarial work will be preferred. Office accommodation will be provided. A form of application has been prepared which will be supplied by me upon receipt of stamped addressed foolscap envelope, and should be filled up and returned as thereon directed. 

Clee View, Grange Rd., Dudley. 

J. E. FLETCHER, Hon. Sec., pro. tem.

PROGRESS Civic-Social-Industrial. 

JULY, 1908. 

QUARTERLY. 

The Industrial Future of 

Boys and Girls.

I. SKILLED EMPLOYMENT & APPRENTICESHIP COMMITTEES IN ENGLAND. 

By J. G. GIBBON. 

AT present very large numbers of children on leaving school go to occupations, such as van-boy, errand-boy, messenger and the like, which, while they reward the boy or girl liberally, make no provision for the adult, neither provide training, with the result that very many youths, when they have outgrown these prospectless occupations, have no career but to add themselves to the already swollen ranks of casual labour.* There is no more promising attack on poverty than that which grapples with this problem, and prominent among the agencies which have set themselves say. * Sir Norman Lockyer, in his recent address as President of the Association of

Technical Institutions, told a good story of an answer by an American Professor to a question that had been put to him by the Duke of Devonshire about our English Institutions. "Well, your Grace," said the Professor, "there is one thing perhaps I ought to

I am an American. In America we have the greatest respect for you over here, because, among other things, we look upon you as the people who in all the world take more trouble to utilise waste products than any other nation under the sun. 

But, your

Grace, that only applies to your material products; you waste your mental products right and left."[ED.]

PROGRESS 

the task are the Skilled Employment and Apprenticeship Committees which have been established within recent years, largely under the auspices of the Charity Organisation Society, the object of the committees being to assist children. on leaving school to obtain good employment, and to exercise kindly oversight over the children during the critical years of youth.

It seemed to the British Institute of Social Service that a knowledge of the work done by the committees would be most valuable, and they therefore addressed a number of questions to the committees. They are much indebted for the replies received. What follows is mainly based on those replies and on reports received from committees, which have been placed at my disposal. A large amount of similar work is being done by private persons and by clubs, and, at a later date, it is hoped that information will be collected respecting this. The Institute will be glad to receive information respecting such work done by private persons, by committees, by clubs, or others, and will be pleased to furnish any person or body, willing and able to give information, with particulars of the matters on which information seems desirable. We give below† a full list of existing Skilled Employment and Apprenticeship Committees as far as at present known to the Institute. Information as to any additional Committees will be welcome. 

†THE APPRENTICESHIP AND SKILLED EMPLOYMENT ASSOCIATION : 

SEC., Miss Daglish, 55, Denison House, Vauxhall Bridge Road, S.W. 

The London Committees and corresponding Committees in the Provinces, affiliated with this, are marked with an asterisk.

LONDON. 

S.E.C. denotes "Skilled Employment Committee." *EAST.-Bethnal Green and Shoreditch S.E.C., St. Hilda's East, Old Nichol St., Bethna 

Green, E. HON. SECS., Miss Anson, F. Mount, and Captain Morse. 

*EAST.-Stepney S.E.C., 52, High St., Whitechapel, E. 

Miss H. S. Weaver. 

HON. SECS., Miss Jevons andl EAST.-Jewish Board of Guardians, Industrial Sub-Committee for Girls, Butler St. Club, Spitalfields. SEC., Miss Rosenthal. 

EAST CENTRAL.-Industrial Improvement Committee, 38, King Square, Goswell Rd., E.C. HON. SECS., A. H. Paterson and Miss Elliott. 

*NORTH.-Holloway S.E.C., 457, Holloway Rd., N. HON. SEC., Miss Bray. Began work 

Jan., 1907. 

*NORTH. Trades Registry and Apprenticeship Committee (Girls), Maurice Hostel, 51, Herbert St., Hoxton, N. HON. SEC., Miss Morley.

The need of such assistance as is rendered by a Skilled Employment and Apprenticeship Committee arises from a variety of causes-defect in parent or child, stress of circumstances, or a want of social organisation adequate to industrial conditions. The pressure of poverty, the immediate need for the highest earnings which the child can bring, is one obvious cause, and ignorance, indifference, and greed also contribute-ignorance, failure to realise the grave consequences to a youth of not acquiring skilled employment, and, where such consequences are realised,

*SOUTH-EAST.-North Lambeth Apprenticeship and S.E.C., 131, Kennington Rd., S.E. 

HON. SEC., Miss Sheepshanks. Began work Mar., 1907. 

SOUTH-EAST.-Bermondsey Apprenticeship Committee, Farncombe St., S.E. HON. SEC., Miss Britton.

ignorance of the means by which they may be averted from son or daughter; indifference to the future of a child, which often but mirrors the parents' indifference to their own future; sometimes greed for the shillings which the child brings in, too often in such cases to be consumed, or to make good the money already consumed, in dissipation. 

The reports of many committees make reference to the parents' position. Thus, to give a few instances:- 

Marylebone. "The absolute necessity, in some cases through pressure at home, that the children should earn high wages, and the greed and indifference of parents in others, often make it impossible to place suitable and promising applicants in skilled industries where the earnings are small at first." Sometimes, however, there is great self-denial on the part of parents.

Bethnal Green. "In a good many instances the case is withdrawn owing to the parents not being able or willing to take small wages with good training; but it is noticeable that sometimes the poorest will make a real sacrifice to enable their girl [this section deals with girls only] to be well trained." 

Hove." We find most parents are in urgent need of the boys' wages, and that consequently a boy is required to take an errand boy's place."

Mr. Parsons, a member of the Whitechapel (now Stepney) Committee states that, while some parents are neglectful, the general attitude is hopeful. 

The children are often not willing vessels of grace. They may prefer the freer, less restrained life of the van-boy or errand-boy to the closer discipline of the apprentice or learner, to say nothing of the higher wages. Even when good work has been found by a committee, instances occur where it has recklessly been thrown away, cases such as those mentioned by one committee of a youth well placed to become a skilled instrument-maker but who could not resist the call of the milk-cans and turned milk-boy, and a girl who threw away a place as apprentice because she coveted the higher dignity of improver and because she had been asked to dust some boxes, an indignity which she resisted, with her mother's approval. Children just entering on their new course of life often have little notion of what they will have to meet, and few have not resented, perhaps in secret, some task which seemed to cast a slur upon their dignity, a dignity which to others seemed exaggerated. In these little crises, the kindly counsel of a wise elder is invaluable. 

Improvement in education may remove some of these defects, but it may well be that, in the interest of the future

development of the child, it is not wise to expect too great immediate adaptation or too severe regard for future contingencies of life: the higher the adult, the longer the period of dependence. But if this be so, the more the need, during the years of growth, of gentle supervision, that false steps may not be taken which would wreck the later life. 

Present social organisation is in some respects inadequate for industrial needs. Parents, and not poor parents only, are often ignorant of a boy's or girl's capabilities, of prospects in occupations, of openings, and of facilities for training. 

They cannot generally be expected to have full knowledge of these matters, and the schoolmaster is often a weak reed. 

There is obvious need of new organisation, a need which, in part, is being supplied by the Skilled Employment and 

Apprenticeship Committees among others.

There are also difficulties of adaptation to industrial needs. Booth has shown how London is parasitic on the provinces for its skill, and what danger exists that London boys will become the hewers of wood and drawers of water of the industrial army of the metropolis, the higher posts being taken by the skill and calibre of the provinces. Significant in this respect is the statement in the report of the L.C.C. Committee on Apprenticeship with regard to the building trade that "the better positions go inevitably to the country-bred man with his all-round training." Whereas the interests of a person may tie him to a certain town or district, that town or district may not give the best opening for his son or daughter. A Londoner may find it desirable that his son should go to some workshops at Sheffield. But to do this very much increases the family expenses, so much as in most cases to be prohibitive. Moreover, there is the difficulty of providing the judicious supervision which is so essential, a difficulty which, as regards Poor Law children, has in part been met by the splendid services of the " Metropolitan Association for Befriending Young Servants," and the "Association for Befriending Boys."

It does not appear that at present there is much interchanging of cases between Skilled Employment and

PROGRESS 

Apprenticeship Committees. Doubtless this will come in time, especially when more provincial committees have been formed. Co-operation will be facilitated by the affiliation of all local committees with the Central Association at Denison House; and it is most necessary that all local committees should be so affiliated. The work of the Central Association should prove most valuable.*

The committees seek to place children in occupations in which there is an assured future. Though there is not much mention, in the reports which have been received, of difficulty in finding suitable occupations, serious difficulties exist in some instances. The committees also seek to suit the work to the boy or girl. Inquiries are made of parents, schoolmaster or mistress, and, of course, most important of all, of the boy (or girl) himself. In some cases, children are medically examined before being placed, to make sure of their physical fitness. Despite the decay of apprenticeship, a decay not peculiar to England, large numbers of children have been placed as apprentices. In the report for 19071908 of the Central Association, particulars are given of the apprenticeship of 156 boys and girls during 1907 by 10 societies affiliated to the Association. 

The general tendency seems to be against the payment of premiums in ordinary cases. Thus Bethnal Green questions the advisability of paying premiums, and Whitechapel, who had previously paid premiums, say in their report for 1906 that no premiums were paid during that year, as also do Marylebone. Hampstead, however, during 1907, paid premiums in ten cases out of a total of forty-three children. placed. The L.C.C. Report on Apprenticeship contains the significant statement that it was frequently found that of a number of children in a workshop the only persons in respect of whom premiums had been paid were Poor Law children. 

But it may be contended that there is special difficulty in placing such children, and that the paying of premiums is It is interesting to note that the Association has formed a Special Committee "to investigate industries followed by men and boys, the trade at present in course of investigation being that of Portmanteau, Bag, and Trunk making." The Association issues a most useful list of suggestions for committees just starting work.

not without justification. The same would apply to children defective in any way-cripples, for instance-and it seems the practice of the Southwark Committee to help such by the payment of premiums. 

When it is decided to grant premiums, as much use as possible is made of existing apprenticeship charities, of which there are many. Thus Whitechapel work in co-operation with the East London Apprenticeship Fund, Paddington with the Paddington Apprenticeship Fund, Hampstead with the Wells and Campden Trustees. A full list of endowed apprenticeship charities in London is given in the L.C.C. 

Report on Apprenticeship. There are many similar provincial charities. Only a comparatively small part, however, of these charities, which are generally not restricted to apprenticeship, appears to be used for this purpose, and the suggestion that they should be converted, in part at least, to the assistance of technical instruction seems worthy of practical effect. When premiums are granted, and also when payments are made for other purposes-buying tools, for instance the committees commendably endeavour to get parents to pay and to repay as much as is reasonable. 

Paddington report that parents can seldom pay more than a small share, and this is probably true of other places also. 

A very large proportion of children are placed as learners. 

Under proper conditions these have as good prospects as apprentices, sometimes better; but caution has to be taken lest the young persons are confined to some petty function, instead of being properly taught the trade, a danger not removed by merely calling the young person a learner or improver, or yet apprentice.*

The need of instruction to supplement the training of the workshop, and this applies to apprentices as well, is deeply felt. The hope of proper technical instruction for the working classes seems to lie in the division of the working hours of youth between the workshop and the properly * A monograph by Prof. Jas. M. Motley, Ph.D. (John Hopkins University) gives much valuable information concerning Trades' Union regulations concerning apprentices in the 

United States. 

[ED.]

PROGRESS 

graded class-room, and popular opinion is gradually crystallising in favour of this system, which already obtains to some extent.

A number of children were placed in unskilled employ- In many cases, however, provision has been made for the future advancement of the boy or girl. Committees rightly feel that, with proper security, unskilled employment may be the gateway to a position of fair competence. But in some cases a boy or girl has been placed in unskilled employment because not capable of better, by reason of some defect, this measure of help being considered better than none at all. In some cases, no help can be given. More will be said of these later.

The need of kindly supervision during adolescence is urgent, for then the child enters on a new world without and within-without, where, set to earn its own livelihood, it is subjected to new demands, requiring new habits; within, where new feelings surge up which it is imperative that youth and maiden should learn to understand and control. 

There is no more splendid service than the patient oversight of a few young people, none which, well performed, rewards with richer harvest. For the quiet service, rendered during the sensitive years of youth, fertilises the very roots of social health. The necessary supervision is generally provided by the Skilled Employment and Apprenticeship Committees by assigning one or a few young persons to a guardianfriend, who helps his wards with kindly counsel and encourages them to good works.

The committees pay great attention to attendance at day and evening technical classes. Significant is the advocacy of day technical classes and the endeavour to arrange with employers for the attendance of young persons at such classes. The Whitechapel Committee "would welcome the formation, by the L.C.C., of day technical classes for boys and girls already at work at a trade. . . . A clause is inserted in our indentures securing permission for the apprenticed to attend at such, if formed "—a very com mendable provision. The Paddington Committee also

advocate attendance at trade-classes during working hours, and say that some employers allowed boys to attend at such time. In an article in the Bayswater Chronicle of April 29th, 

1906, respecting the work of the Paddington Committee, it is stated that, "so far as the Association's short experience goes, the difficulty on this score seems not to arise from the employers so much as from the fact that there are very few day technical classes available. This deficiency the Association is pressing very urgently on the attention of the local 

Education Authorities." Marylebone relate an amusing instance of a youth, whose allegiance to evening classes was being broken by the warmer pleasures of "walking-out," who was reclaimed to good ways by securing the co-operation of the maiden-a good instance of the strategy which may be demanded of the guardian-friend.

The committees strongly encourage thrift, as is to be expected of bodies so closely connected with the Charity Organisation Society, and try to bring home the need of foresight, and to lay a sound foundation by getting the young person to join a good benefit society. In other ways also the committees stand by their boys and girls as good fairies. Thus the Southwark and Whitechapel Committees provided, or were the means of getting provided, convalescent treatment for some, and the former committee also assisted a number to obtain holidays in the country, the young persons paying part of the cost, the rest being paid out of a special fund. Some may urge that the committees take the place of parents and weaken the latter's responsibility. The committees, on the contrary, may urge that they enforce responsibility, that they make the blind to see-see duties and responsibilities which were hitherto unseenand that by gentle pressure they get these duties and responsibilities better performed.

The committees have gained a large measure of success. 

Their work will extend, and extension is desirable. At present, the movement is young, and not only are the committees comparatively not very numerous, but cases helped have to be selected, so that the confidence of employers may

PROGRESS 

be gained. Will the extension of this movement provide a sufficient remedy for the ills with which it deals, or needs it to be supplemented by other action? As long as boys and girls are allowed to take a course which leads to casual labour, one of the chief sources of poverty remains unstopped. 

Some cases are practically unhelpable other than by institutional treatment, or can only be helped with great difficulty, owing to serious defects of mind or body. The 

Southwark Committee, owing to the unsatisfactory results, gave up the placing of defective children, and handed the work over to a special association. As regards at least some of the most serious cases of defective children, it is earnestly hoped that the Feeble-minded Commission will result in some wiser treatment than at present prevails-prevails with very disastrous consequences. Then again in some cases, owing to defect of parent or child, help will not be sought or taken. Will the community continue to endure this, with the harmful results which follow? 

Some urge that work such as is now done by the Skilled Employment and Apprenticeship Committees should be done by the 

Educational Authorities. This cannot be discussed now, and it must suffice to point out that the problem is not so simple as it seems, and that it is not a question of merely finding employment, but of much else besides. 

Even the cursory observer must be impressed with the urgency of the problem. The years of adolescence are most critical. And in these critical years, the boy or girl is turned to face a new world, with new demands and new responsibilities, turned often with little knowledge and little preparation. In these years are laid the foundations of the future life, foundations which, if well and truly laid, will repay a hundred-fold in prosperity and happiness, to the individual and to the community, but, if ill-laid, will bring distress and woe to both.

In very self-protection, if for no other reason, the community must grapple with the problem, for as long as these roots of evil flourish, so long will poverty prevail. And there is another reason and a nobler faith. For that spirit of

humanity which refused to see the child sacrificed in the whirl of the factory, in due season of growth will refuse to acquiesce in the sacrifice of maiden and youth, but in travail of hope will bring forth some better organisation.

II. APPRENTICESHIP SCHOOLS IN WÜRTTEMBURG. 

BY THE EDITOR. 

THAT " need of instruction to supplement the training of the workshop" which Mr. Gibbon emphasies in a previous article has been largely supplied in Germany, and notably in 

Württemburg. The fact that "Trade follows the School"

a fact we have too long overlooked-has received remarkable demonstration in that country. Less than sixty years ago its condition was deplorable. With scarcely any manufactures, and relying almost wholly on its agriculture, it was unable to support its population; now it is one of the most thriving hives of industry on the Continent, and though its population has considerably increased, it was officially reported, a short time ago, that "there is not a pauper in the land." It has ceased to be a purely agricultural, and has become even more largely an industrial community. Some idea of this may be gathered from the following report of the British minister at Stuttgart:- 

England now buys from Württemburg, blankets, carpets, flannels, hosiery, linens, tissues, instruments, types, drugs, chemicals, paper, ivory goods, wood-carving, toys, furniture, hats, pianos, gunpowder, clocks, and stays. The manufacture of gunpowder, once preeminently English, is now a speciality of Württemburg, and the Rotteweil Mills have attained such celebrity that they supply powder for artillery and blasting to Bavaria, Russia, Holland, Servia, and England. A manufacture of small arms has also obtained a footing, the Mauser factory being now famous all over the world for its repeating rifles. 

The reason of this remarkable change is not far to seek, and it may furnish us with an example and a warning. Since the year 1851 Württemburg has been patiently and laboriously building up and perfecting an elaborate system of Continuation Schools, which even now are not surpassed, but which, next April, when the Act of July 1906 comes into force, will place her quite in the forefront.

PROGRESS 

That Act, to which I will refer more fully later, applies only to Württemburg; but these schools have existed in nearly every industrial centre in Germany, and also in 

Switzerland, for many years. Württemburg, indeed, was not the first to take the lead, but followed in the wake of Baden, and even now Baden and parts of Bavaria are at present ahead of her in their compulsory regulations. The record of what has been done in these States is full of interest, and a brief account of what I saw and learnt in 

Munich, Basel, Freiburg, and Mannheim, when visiting their schools last May, may serve as the best introduction to what I shall have to say further regarding the great forward movement in Württemburg.

No city in Germany stands higher than-perhaps none so high as--Munich in the provision that has been made for 

Apprenticeship and general technical Continuation Schools. 

This is largely one might almost say entirely-due to the enthusiasm of Dr. Kerchensteiner, its Education Minister. 

Dr. Kerchensteiner recognised that the Trade (Gewerbe*)

Schools established in 1877 altogether "lacked the breath of life." As he says, "the bond of union between the school and the workshop was still wanting; the boy got, it is true, training in the requirements of his trade, but no one laid emphasis on the most important side-the practical work." Accordingly Gewerbe (or Trade Continuation) 

Schools were established in four centres in the city of Munich in which practical work was made the centre of interest. In these schools there were about 7,200 boys, between the ages of 14 and 18 years in 1907, of whom 6,000 were apprentices. The Director of the School in 

Liebherrstrasse told me that 80 per cent. of the boys leaving the elementary schools in Munich are apprenticed to some trade or other. Every apprentice is obliged to attend one *The German word Gewerbe is difficult to translate so as to convey its true significance to English readers. Literally it is an "Industrial School," but with us that means a Reformatory, which this is not in any sense; it is a "Technical School," yet not exactly as we understand the term, nor is it exactly what we understand by a "Trade School." I have therefore retained the German word throughout in the hope that the text will explain it. † See Dr. Kerchensteiner's article in School, June, 1908.

of these schools for eight or nine hours a week during the whole period of his apprenticeship, and in no case must it be less than three years. An endeavour is being made to provide only day classes for apprentices, but at present Munich has got no further than to insist that no class shall be held after 7 p.m., when the workshops close, though many are held from 4 till 7, and some during the mornings. 

In addition to these schools I visited others-two in Baden (at Freiburg and Mannheim) one in Basel; and one in 

HALL FOR EXHIBITIONS AND EXAMINATION-GEWERBE SCHOOL LIEBHERR STRASSE, MUNICH. 

Frankfurt-on-Main. In each place I found large, handsome buildings, well equipped and well staffed. That in Mannheim had 1,940 pupils, representing sixty-four different trades, among which may be mentioned Bakers, Bookbinders, Barbers, Electrical engineers, Optical Instrument makers, Cooks, Mechanics, Saddlers, Masons, Cabinet makers,

Smiths, Photographers. These schools are entirely supported by the municipalities, with the exception of that in Basel, which receives a subsidy from the Federal Government. Tuition is free. In Baden and Prussia, as in Bavaria, the attendance

PROGRESS 

of apprentices is compulsory, but in Baden it is by a State law, in Bavaria and Prussia (except in the Eastern provinces) it is only made so by Local Bye-law; Basel as yet has no compulsory law in force, but it will be secured by a recent

Act which does not, however, come into force till 1910. 

Comparing these conditions, however, with those in England we see how much leeway we have to make up; and yet it is only when we come to Württemburg that we fully realise what can be done by a country which gives a first place

to technical education. At present, and until the Act already referred to comes into force, Württemburg has no State law to compel attendance at its Gewerbe Schools, and the success that they have hitherto attained is mainly owing to the freewill of the pupils and the goodwill of the employers. And that success has been remarkable, as will be seen from the following extract from Mr. Snowden's report* : 

Württemburg, with a population comparable to New Jersey (considerably less than that of the West Riding of Yorkshire), had in 1905, 243 industrial and commercial improvement schools, public drawing schools, and "women's work" schools scattered throughout the kingdom (see diagram p. 178), with a total of 28,574 students. 150 of these institutions were Gewerbe schools for young men, of which 128 were non-compulsory, though attended by 18,535 students ; 4 were commercial improvement schools, with a total enrolment of 1,245; 42 were industrial drawing schools, with 894 pupils; 15 were 

Gewerbe schools for girls, with 1,042 in attendance; and 32 were "trades schools" for girls and women, with 6,858 on the lists. Industrial drawing was taught by 654 specialists, and the remaining subjects by 952 instructors. The State gave aid to the schools to the extent of £14,874, of which 13,300 was for the industrial and commercial improvement schools alone.

This success has incited to fresh and bolder measuresmeasures which have been taken with the hearty and unanimous approval of a people who have learnt to appreciate the Gewerbe School. It was felt that the present system, excellent as its results have been, might be considerably improved. There must be more schools, and all apprentices must be obliged to attend; the time during which that attendance is obligatory must be considerably increased, and all classes must be held during the day, the employers being compelled to give the necessary time. All this is provided for in the Act which will come into force next April. A copy of that Act was given to me in Stuttgart by Dr. Hartmann, an enthusiastic educationalist, from which I take the following extract :- (1) Each community in which there are at least 40 male workers under 18 years of age, must establish either a Gewerbe or Commercial Continuation School. Small communities may combine. (2) All boys on leaving the Primary School must attend one of these schools for a period of 3 years, unless attending some other recognised Technical School, but youths engaged in certain unskilled occupations may be allowed to attend the ordinary Continua tion School (Allgemaine Fortbildungs Schule) instead of the Gewerbe. 

(3) The teaching must be given during the day, and there shall be no evening classes for apprentices. 

* Report on the Industrial Improvement Schools in Württemburg, by Albert A. 

Snowden. Teachers' Record. 8opp. Columbia Press (U.S.A.)

PROGRESS 

(4) The school course shall be not less than 3 years, with a requisite attendance of 280 hours in each year. (5) Girls engaged in Industrial or Commercial pursuits shall either be provided with a separate school, or allowed to attend with the boys. (6) Employers are required to give the necessary time during working hours for all under 18 years to attend the school, and see to their regular and punctual attendance. (7) A small fee (about 58. per annum) may be charged, but it may be remitted when advisable, and in all cases the employers must advance the amount. The State pays half the sum required for school maintenance after deducting the amount raised by fees. (8) No pupil under 17 years may enter a public-house unless with parent or guardian, or when on a journey.

It is in the provision of compulsory day instruction and the requirement that each community with at least 40 young people at work shall establish its school that Württemburg stands in advance of neighbouring States. The day instruction was considered the most important. All the school authorities with whom I spoke, not only in Württemburg, but also in the other States, told me how impossible it was to get really satisfactory results from evening classes. 

The pupils came to these classes wearied with their full day's work and were utterly unable to give their minds to the teaching. In England we are struggling to get compulsory attendance in the evening, in Württemburg they are rejoicing in having it during the day. 

Professor Sadler* gives the result of a special inquiry from teachers and others as to the desirability of making attendance at Evening Continuation Schools compulsory in this country, and among the answers received we find the following objections :-(1) The difficulty of getting teachers; (2) the difficulty of enforcing attendance, or, when enforced, of getting the pupils to work; (3) compulsion would only bring the stupid ones, the rest will come without; (4) the increased cost. In another part of the same book 

Professor Sadler thus sums up the results of a similar inquiry in Germany : 

Business people and employers in general no longer complain in any way of the compulsory continuation school system. Many regard it as a burden, it is true, but those who are liberal-minded feel that it is a burden which ought to be borne. Speaking generally, public opinion among employers, workpeople and apprentices alike, is entirely *"Continuation Schools in England and Elsewhere." Edited by M. E. Sadler, M.A. 

Manchester University Press.

favourable to the compulsory continuation schools. The apprentices especially approve the system because the schools are grouped according to trades. Their appreciation of the system is proved by the fact that a large number of them continue to attend the schools voluntarily when their period of compulsory attendance is over. 

It is in this direction that we must look if we are to retain our commercial supremacy. In But the character of the schools, and of the instruction imparted, is quite as noteworthy as the regulations for their establishment and attendance. The goal aimed at is to thoroughly equip each apprentice* for the particular vocation he has chosen to follow. To secure this, separate classes are formed, as far as possible, for each separate vocation-" the butcher, the baker, the candlestick-maker," etc. This is the "practical work," of which Dr. Kerchensteiner speaks, which is made "the centre of interest." 

It is in this way that the training of the workshop is supplemented, and this supplement is needed in Germany, as in England, though not perhaps to the same extent. 

Germany, as with us, many of those employed in engineering, and, indeed, in many other industries, would—but for these schools-never get beyond isolated parts of the work in which they were engaged. "Thousands of boys," writes Dr. Kerchensteiner, " have only a one-sided apprenticeship, and thousands more lack even this. We must inspire them with the delight that springs from thoroughness of work. They must learn to appreciate the moral effect of good work on their own personalities. If we put the Craft School and practical training, instead of text books and mere words, in the forefront of instruction, we gain the goodwill of the pupils at once.” 

But mere manual training, excellent as it may be for rousing interest, is in too narrow a groove for the attainment of the far broader purposes of the Gewerbe School. 

Dr. Hartmann, the Director of the Gewerbe Schools in 

Stuttgart, in an interesting article in the Gewerbeblatt (June 15th, 1907), defines the two main objects of these Each Gewerbe School has also evening-and indeed, some day-classes for journeymen, foremen, and master-workmen, with longer or shorter courses, and at which attendance is voluntary. I leave this without further mention, as I am now merely concerned with what is being done for the apprentices.

PROGRESS schools as "Berufskunde" and "Geschäftskunde." The first of these relates to production, the second to distribution. What they mean is this:-First, that every apprentice must have a competent knowledge of any particular work in which he is engaged, thus a pupil from the engineering trade must understand his machine, how it is constructed, how it works, etc.; and so with other industries, work must no longer be mere mechanical drudgery, but an intelligent co-operation with the means of production. 

Drawing (freehand, geometric) and painting take a first place in the teaching of every Gewerbe School; the training of the eye is regarded as essential to the proper working of the hand. The second main object is Geschäftskunde, which relates to distribution, and the pupils are taught how to judge the state of the market, the cost of raw material, tariff, transport, correspondence, book-keeping, exchange, and general business management, etc. But these schools aim at something more than mere vocational training. These apprentices, whatever vocation they may have chosen, must all become citizens, and so they must receive such an allround education as will fit them to undertake intelligently the duties and responsibilities of their citizenship. The man is greater than the craftsman, and so in addition to the Berufs and Geschäftskunde there must be also Bürgerkunde, or a knowledge of civics. Professor Sadler thus refers to the importance of this wider training :- 

It is an

The forces behind the movement are more complex than at first appears. expression of social tendencies which are still undefined in words even by many of those who feel most strongly the push of the tendencies in their mind and work. To a great extent, perhaps to an undue degree, the desire for technical skill and for increased wageearning capacity is the motive power to-day in the system of continuation schools of Germany, as in the less perfectly organised systems of other countries, including our own. 

But from a social, as well as from an economic, point of view, the continuation school is an important factor in national life. It prevents the waste, through neglect, of much that has been learnt in the elementary school. It gives help to self-discipline during the critical years of adolescence. It aims at making every youth realise that increased skill in his trade and deeper knowledge of the scientific principles which underlie it, will not only enable him to hold his own in the economic struggle, but will also enhance his personal dignity as a member of the community. Therefore, if the continuation school is to do its complete work, it must afford a training in citizenship as well as impart purely technical skill.

One of the difficulties raised against compulsory attendance at Evening Continuation Schools in England was "the

difficulty of getting teachers." This difficulty has not been ignored in Württemburg, and great pains have been taken to overcome it. The intention is that ultimately each vocation shall have a teacher with practical experience in that vocation, who will give his whole time to the work; but for the present-though it is hoped not for long-this is impossible. In the smaller communities-though not in the larger-it has been found necessary to appoint as teachers, foremen or masters, who, having their own business or 

GEWERBE (INDUSTRIAL) MUSEUM-STUTTGART. 

workshops to attend to, can only give a portion of their time. to the School. But whether whole- or half-timers, they must be specially trained to teach. The whole-time teachers (Hauptamt) get this training at present at the Normal Training School at Karlsruhe. They are required to take a 3 years' course-of which six months must be spent in practical work—and obtain a diploma of efficiency. They are drawn mainly from the best teachers in the Elementary Schools. The cost in fees is nominal (only 12s. a year), with is. 3d. for matriculation. The State makes a maintenance grant. In Prussia three or four months' training has

PROGRESS 

sufficed, but the Württemburg employers of skilled labour would not agree to this. In providing the time for day instruction, they are called upon to make sacrifices, and they insisted that the instruction should be thorough. For those only partially employed the course of training is much shorter. 

In several of the more important centres of industry the 

Gewerbe (Industrial) Museum forms an important part of the school equipment. Of those which I saw (Freiburg, Basel, Munich, Stuttgart, Mannheim), that in Stuttgart is incomparably the finest building. Mr. Snowden, writing from America, says "it is worth going round the world to see."

As regards the building this may be true, but after what I had seen in Basel I was somewhat disappointed with its contents. There is a very fine collection of plaster casts, models, mouldings, medallions, etc.; a fine library-sumptuously housed-containing all the best books of reference in all languages on the various subjects of study, besides thousands of drawings, paintings, prints, and photographs taken from the most artistic designs the world can produce. 

In these sections it is little, if at all, inferior to Basel. It is, however, in the general exhibits that the inferiority is seen. Basel has ransacked the workshops of the world to find the finest models; Stuttgart is content to exhibit what various engineers, manufacturers, architects, etc., choose to send for the purpose of advertisement. No doubt they are excellent specimens in their way, but they cannot have the educational value of those in Basel, which represent the finest and most artistic workmanship of all ages and all countries. Here are to be seen the finest specimens of decorative iron, marble and wood work, furniture, tapestry, mouldings, cornices, pottery, stained glass, etc., all of which can be copied by the student either in the Museum, or in the home or workshop.

Such are the opportunities which Germany and Switzerland offer their boys and girls on leaving school, and the advantages of the Gewerbe school are so great and so easily obtained that an ever increasing number of their young people are being drawn into the fields of skilled employment.

Rural Housing. 

BY ANNETTE CHURTON. 

THE question of the housing of the working classes in London and other large towns has now occupied public attention for many years, and efforts, both private and municipal, have been made to meet the demand of an enormously increased urban population. This increase, as is well known, has been largely augmented by the migration of the villagers into the towns. While admitting that the towns need the reviving influence of a certain amount of fresh country life to counteract the enervating effect of the town, the astonishing rapidity with which the numbers annually moving from the rural into the urban districts. have increased during the last thirty years can scarcely be looked upon with equanimity. During the ten years between 

1891 and 1901 no less than 500,654 persons left the country villages for the towns, at the rate, that is, of 50,000 annually.. 

The effect of this upon the towns is to complicate the problems already existing there, overcrowding and consumption, with which is connected the drink problem, want of work, casual employment, and poverty.

While efforts are being made to cope with these problems in the towns, it is at last being recognised that the root of the difficulty lies in the country. If, therefore, we are to do more than palliate the evil, we must look beyond the town and study the conditions existing in villages. Here we find the most vigorous and enterprising of the young labourers leaving for the towns, with the result that energy and skill are less and less applied to the land, and old methods, which are no longer useful, are not replaced by better ones. It is hopeless to try and replace the country labourer by the unemployed from the towns. 

They have not had the requisite training, and are unable to adapt themselves to the conditions of country life. 

The only way out of the difficulty would seem to be to retain the agricultural labourer on the land, and this can only be done

PROGRESS 

by making the conditions such that it will be worth his while to remain. At present the conditions are certainly not such as to make the life attractive; a man of the upper and middle class has often not completed his education at the age when the young farm labourer is earning as much as he can ever hope to earn, and has no further "rise in life" to expect. Though the wage varies in different counties, even at the best he can hardly hope to provide for his old age, the most that he can do being to join a sick benefit 

By courtesy of the Garden City Association. 

GROUP OF FOUR COTTAGES, HEATHFIELD. 

and burial club. He has no prospect to look forward to when past work except parish relief to supplement his relations' help.

Next to the wages difficulty, and connected with it, is the one which forms the special subject of this article, namely, the housing difficulty. In spite of a diminished country population, there exists in many districts a considerable scarcity of cottage accommodation, leading to such overcrowding as to render a decent healthy life almost an impossibility. Besides the difficulty of overcrowding, the shortage of cottages leads, to the continuance in occupation of

RURAL HOUSING 

187 cottages unfit for human habitation, the sanitary authorities frequently considering that to condemn them would only increase the overcrowding. In those districts where the action of these authorities is more vigorous, and cottages are condemned and closed, the tenants hitherto occupying them have had no choice but to leave the district, finding it impossible to secure another cottage. Cases have not been unknown of families having to take refuge in the workhouse for want of cottages. On visiting a workhouse in a rural district within forty miles of London a few weeks ago, the writer of this article was informed by the Master that at that time five families were inmates of the workhousethirty-nine persons in all-who were sure of work, and would go out at once if cottages could be found for them.

An inquiry held at Evesham last year by the Worcestershire County Council, elicited facts which were summarised. 

as follows: 

:- "That there is a present shortage of cottages in all but one of the parishes in the Evesham Rural District, and in that one it is anticipated that there will soon be a shortage. 

That this shortage seems likely to increase.

That from the evidence before us it appears that in this district the shortage has the following results:- (1) It drives the young people into the towns, as they are unable to stay in the country for lack of houses. (2) It prevents the land being fully developed and cultivated in small farms or holdings, and compels the system of farms as against gardening to continue. (3) That if more cottages were provided the migration of the people from the country to the town would be to some extent stayed in the district. (4) That if the population of this Rural District is to be maintained dwe lings must be provided to enable it to be done."

The same conclusion has been arrived at in many other districts. One Rural District Council writes :- 

"Some of the houses were structurally unsafe. Many were so constructed and so placed that sanitary conditions were impossible." And another:-"The Council have long felt the want of suitable cottages within their district. Many of the worst properties have ceased to be occupied and are now going to ruin-they are rarely pulled down, and still more rarely are they replaced by better houses. 

Cheaper cottages must be forthcoming or the process of depopulation will continue."

PROGRESS 

We have abundant evidence then from all parts of the country that in many villages there is an actual scarcity of cottages, which can only be met by the building of new ones. Here we come to the difficulty of cost. 

The wages in the building trade, and the increased cost of materials, make it practically impossible to build cottages to pay, if the rent is to be as low as is customary in agricultural districts. 

We must, however, remember that at this rent the cottages have never yielded a commercial profit. It is purely a customary rent, and, as was pointed out by the Royal Commission on Labour, "has generally no relation to the size of the cottage, the cost of its construction, the accommodation which it affords, its condition as regards repair or sanitary arrangements, or to the earnings of the occupier." This custom of providing cottages at nominal rents forms part of a system of the payment of wages in kind, and has had the result of checking cottage building, both private and municipal, in country districts. 

A suggestion has been made by the Editor of the Rural World that this difficulty might be met by means of the following readjustment :-The labourer should rent his cottage direct from the owner at a commercial rent. The farmer should pay to the labourer the extra wage to allow of this, the owner deducting the same amount from the rent of the farm. In this way, though the owner would receive less rent from his farm, the equivalent would be paid by the cottagers.

If some such alteration were made enabling the rural labourer to receive a higher wage, the £150 cottage might be brought within his reach. At the time of the First 

Cottage Exhibition at Letchworth, a paper was read by 

Miss Cochrane, giving the result of an inquiry she had made among the wives of rural labourers, as to what they considered essential requirements in a cottage. The first of these was the provision of 3 bedrooms, with a fireplace in one at least. Many reasons were given for the demand. "All the women," Miss Cochrane says, "were unanimous in objecting to putting their growing boys and girls in the

RURAL HOUSING 

189 same bedroom, and to having nowhere to accommodate relations, married children, those in service, and perhaps a sick nurse. Also in illness they consider a third room essential, and some women spoke of there being nowhere to place the dead in the cottage with two bedrooms and one sitting-room. . . . In every instance the women asked for a sitting-room and a kitchen-living-room on the ground floor. The large majority would like the copper and sink in a shed outside." A great point was made of sashwindows which could be open at the top without creating a draught, also of the great desirability of possessing a parlour. "All wished for a larder or pantry facing north or east, and most preferred the shape to be long and narrow, 10 ft. × 4ft. or 8ft. × 4ft. to 6ft. Several writers have recently urged that larders need not be larger than 3ft. square. On mentioning this to some of the women they entirely dissented, and pointed out how space was required for bread pans, meat in pickle, flour bins, drinking water, vegetables, apples, home-made jams, etc., besides ordinary food. In country villages tradesmen do not often call, and certain stores have to be kept." 

It will be 'seen that most of these essentials are to be found in the plans given in this article.

The third, known as the Brandsby Model Cottage, has been used by Mr. Fairfax-Cholmeley as a model for labourers' cottages, at Brandsby in the North Riding of Yorkshire, and has been copied in other parts of the country. In a paper read before the Rural Housing Association, Mr. 

Fairfax-Cholmeley states:- 

I am quite satisfied from my own experience that a country builder who works on the job with his men, can build these cottages in pairs for a price varying from £140 to £160 each, according to circumstances, though, no doubt, a contractor who did not work himself would require a larger margin of profit. . . . . So far as external appearance is concerned 

Bad ornament is one must trust to simplicity and proportion rather than to ornament. worse than none, and you cannot have good ornament without adding materially to the cost. . . . . In the Brandsby Cottage you see a perfectly straight building with a line of slightly projecting bricks running the entire length on the level of the bedroom floor. 

That line, called a string-course, by making the cottage look lower, improves the proportion, and the extra cost of it over a plain wall might be estimated at about £1 or under.

ALTERNATIVE GROUND: TRAN 

As to the internal arrangements, emphasis is laid by Mr. Fairfax-Cholmeley on the undesirability of entering the living-room directly from the outside. 

In the Brandsby Cottage I have contrived a lobby at the entrance, from which the staircase leads to the bedrooms. The living-room itself should be the best room on the ground floor, but should not be too large to be easily warmed by a moderate sized fire, and it should not have more than two doors into it. A good size is 15 ft. by 12 ft., a greater length makes a cold end to the room. If possible the doors should be kept to the end of the room furthest from the fire. 

In the Brandsby model the kitchen is 8 ft. high .... to the floor boards above, and I have saved 7 in. in the height of the building by leaving the floor joists unceiled. A greater height than this is unnecessary in the country, where there is abundance of good air coming into the house through open doors all day, and · where the majority spend their life out of doors. . . . . The money saved by the economies I have mentioned is far better spent in other comforts, such as larger area, better bedrooms. . . . . A little more real knowledge and sympathy would soon dispose of the bourgeois prejudice that sums up the claims of the parlour as merely a snobbish

RURAL HOUSING 

191 desire for a place to store fine furniture. If it is useful for no other purpose, it is at all events a place in which a coffin may be placed; perhaps the mention of such an eventuality may bring home to those who have never thought of it, the difficulties of those who have to live in a cottage with only one living room where there is any aspiration to preserve the decencies of life.

The cost of building may be diminished in some districts by using materials to be found on the spot, where this is permitted by the bye-laws. As an instance of this, we may quote the following description by Miss Cochrane of the cottages at Winterslow, near Salisbury: 

Between thirty and forty cottages have been built by the freeholders themselves, during the last eleven years, out of the ordinary chalk of the country, which is dug out, moistened, mixed with straw, and trodden into a kind of paste. The outer walls are built up gradually by a local builder, without any frame work, and 18 in. thick. 

The walls are plastered outside and have a smooth appearance. The rooms are large and high, and the cottages in every way excellent. . . . . The cost of building the cottages varies from £110 

By courtesy of the Editor of "World's Work." 

2 

LABOURERS' COTTAGES AT WINTERSLOW. 

to 130, according to whether there are three or four bedrooms. A good cottage with three bedrooms costs about £115. Every cottage stands on its own land, and I in no instance saw two together. The cost of digging the tank is reduced by the utilisation of the chalk for the walls of the houses, and this also gives them rough cellars. The tanks are 12 ft. deep, and 6 ft. across, are lined with tiles and cement, and cost from £6 to £7. 

The method by which the cost of building is met by those who build their own houses is thus described :- "Some of the people have saved money to build their houses, some borrow from the Land Court at 3 per cent. and pay off gradually, whilst others prefer to borrow permanently from the Rechabites and other clubs at 3 per cent. or 4 per cent., which comes to the same thing as paying a rent. 

---

PROGRESS 

Great hopes have been placed on the Housing Bill now in Committee as a means of facilitating the building of cottages in rural districts. Unfortunately the rural part of the Bill is scarcely calculated to meet the real difficulties of the case. The reforms most urgently needed are in connection with the existing administration. The result of the experience of the last few years would seem to point clearly to the fact that the carrying out of the Health and Housing Acts should be in the hands of a body dealing with a wider area than that now administered by the Rural District Council. Owing to the poverty of many of the smaller rural districts, they can hardly be expected to undertake expensive schemes of housing and water supply; within the limits of one county there are several districts in which Id. rate would not raise more than 100. Then there is the difficulty of local interests and prejudices. 

Everyone who lives in a country neighbourhood knows how difficult it is to make complaints which will give offence to some near neighbour, and to endeavour to remedy abuses against the weight of an opinion in favour of letting things alone. The position of the local Medical Officer of Health is peculiarly difficult he is the servant of the Rural District Council from whom he holds his appointment, which is usually made subject to annual renewal. In the conscientious discharge of his duties he may frequently have to report unfavourably on property held by some member of the Council. This is apt to rouse the antagonism of the member concerned, and cases are not unknown in which the consequence has been the refusal of re-appointment by the Council. The salary of these officers is frequently quite inadequate, and many of them have to depend largely on private practice for their living. This is objectionable for two reasons: it increases their dependence on the goodwill of their patients-frequently owners of cottage propertyand leaves too little time for the proper performance of public health duties. 

The difficulty with regard to position is equally great in the case of the Inspectors of Nuisances. For them also it

RURAL HOUSING 

193 is almost impossible at times to fulfil their duties satisfactorily, owing to their dependence on a Council, whose members are often unwilling to have the defects in their own property brought to light. The training of Rural Inspectors is often very imperfect, less than half their number at the present time possess any certificate of sanitary knowledge. The custom also prevails in some districts of combining the office of Surveyor with that of Inspector. This is undesirable because it results in the sanitary work taking the second place, also in causing the Inspector to be less directly under the supervision of the Medical Officer. In order to improve matters in these respects, two changes in the existing system are specially needed :- :- (1) The enlargement of the area for the election of members to the administrative body, by which means the force of local interests would be weakened, and personal considerations would be a less important factor. (2) The Medical Officers and Inspectors, who would hold their appointments from this wider authority, should in every case be duly qualified for their posts by a specific course of training in matters relating to public health and sanitation.

Neither of these alterations finds any place in the Housing Bill. It is hoped that before it becomes law this very serious omission will be rectified.

Again, the financial question cannot be said to be fairly faced in the Bill as introduced into Parliament. The recommendations on this subject made by the Select Committee on Rural Housing were, (a) that the Treasury should lend money for housing purposes at the lowest rate at which the Treasury can themselves borrow; (b) that the period of redemption of loans should be lengthened; (c) that the system of increasing rates of interest in proportion to the length of the period of the loan should be abolished; and (d) that grants from the Exchequer should be made for housing purposes. Of these recommendations the only two embodied in the Bill are the lengthening of the period of redemption and the abolition of increasing the rate of

PROGRESS 

interest in proportion to the length of the period of the loan. Though this would be of some use, it would not in any way adequately meet the difficulty. If the retaining of the agricultural population is a question of importance, not only to those directly engaged in agriculture, but to the welfare of the whole nation, it is not too much to expect that the nation as a whole should be prepared to make some sacrifice to attain the desired end.

It must be recognised that the reinstatement of the rural population cannot be accomplished by means of one remedy alone. The improvement of the present conditions must be gradually brought about by many different means. 

Improved legislation is one of these, co-operation in its various forms is another. The greater facilities given under the recent Act for obtaining small holdings, provide the agricultural labourer with an opportunity for improving his position. Education in rural districts is now increasingly directed towards awakening an interest in subjects connected with country life. The provision of new cottages, even though the rent cannot be immediately within the means of the agricultural labourer, eases the pressure upon him, by providing sufficient accommodation for those just above him in financial position.

All these movements are of use in a greater or less degree, but the value of them all depends upon the existence of an awakened public conscience, and a stronger feeling of responsibility in the matter, without which no reform can be effectually carried out.

Chips from Social Workshops. 

OUR OWN WORKSHOP. 

ONE of the most important departments of the 

Institute's work deals with "Industrial Betterment," or or "Welfare Management," terms used broadly to include all efforts made by employers for the physical, mental, and moral wellbeing of their employees altogether apart from their functions as mere wage-payers. A great deal of such work is being done in England, but though we have sent out repeated inquiries we have still much difficulty in discovering just what phases of it have been undertaken by different employers. We have been able to compile a list of rather more than eighty employers in the United Kingdom who are engaged in this sort of effort in one form or another, but it must be woefully incomplete. 

We give it, however, as it stands, in the hope that readers of "PROGRESS" may be able and willing to supplement by sending other names to us. It is not unnatural or unbecoming that those who are doing work of this kind should be modest and reticent about it, but if they once realise that they can serve a useful public purpose by allowing others to profit by their example and experience, we feel sure that there would be less difficulty in obtaining the desired information :-

Industrial Betterment

MISS ROSA BARRETT is a real children's friend;

COTTAGE HOME the knowledge of need at once awakens her 

FOR LITTLE CHILDREN. 

sympathy, and she is not one who is content to "love with the tongue." Her untiring efforts to secure the establishment of Children's Courts and Remand Homes are well known, but the quieter work she has been carrying on-in conjunction with an influential committee of which Lord Meath is President-for more than a quarter of a century, in providing a Home for Little Children at Kingston, near Dublin, though probably less generally known, is in its way equally useful. 

By courtesy of the Editor of "Social Service." COTTAGE HOME FOR LITTLE CHILDREN. 

The object of the Home (as stated in the Deed of Constitution) is "to teach the lesson of self-help to the industrious poor by assisting in providing a Home for legitimate children of both sexes." Children are received from birth up to the age of 6 years, but not older, the object being the care of those whose helplessness or tender years prevent their being received into Industrial or other schools which, as a rule, do not admit under that age. Though it is a Home for 

Little Children, it is intended to benefit the parent as much as the child. A father who has been left a widower can go to his daily work much more contentedly if he knows that his little one is in safe keeping; a mother left with quite young children dependent on her is too apt to drift into a workhouse unless kind hands will

PROGRESS 

care for them while she works for their support. 

In almost every case either the parents or friends contribute to the cost, according to their means, though there is no fixed charge, and payment is not made a condition of admittance. Last year's Statement of 

Accounts shows a total expenditure of £833 19s. 2d., of which £238 1s. 10d. came from this source. During that year 29 children were received, 13 of whom were 2 or under 2 years of age; 20, of the total of 49 now in the Home, are so small and helpless that they have to be fed. Everything is done to make the lives of these little ones bright and cheerful; all their rooms face to the South; they have a dining-room, schoolroom—" and they love their lessons, Kindergarten work, and action songs,"-playroom, and 4 large dormitories. "Sickness," says Miss Barrett, "is a rare event. At the average rate of mortality of children of this age we ought to have had some 4 deaths every year; we have only had 4 in 10 years, and we knew that 3 of these could hardly live when they were admitted.” 

This has been secured by good sanitary arrangements, wholesome food, plenty of fresh air, and loving care. 

GARDEN SUBURBS. 

THE creation of these Garden Suburbs and the promotion of Tenants' Housing Schemes is greatly assisted by the fact that the Public Works Loan Commissioners are empowered to lend money at 3 per cent. to Societies of Public Utility on certain conditions, one being that not more than 5 per cent. is paid on capital. The amount at their disposal for this purpose is £4,000,000, and though all do not take advantage of it, it is there for those who need it. 

Harborne is one of the more recent of our Garden Suburbs.

There an estate of 53 acres has been purchased by the Housing Tenants (Ltd.) This estate lies on the outskirts of Birmingham and is within a 2d. tram fare from the centre of the town. It is now being laid out on the principle of allowing rather less than 10 houses to the acre. As no main roads are required for through traffic it is not necessary to have more than 16 ft. roadway in any case, though in all cases 72 ft. will be allowed between the houses. 

The road diagram on page 199 shows the use that is made of the extra space. 

This lessening of cost in road making naturally reduces rents. Ten of the 53 acres are reserved for parks, open spaces, playgrounds, and allotments which as far as possible have been located on back-land in order not to take from the more valuable frontage building sites. In the laying out of the estate great care has been

taken to preserve all existing trees, and a small ornamental sheet of water is left as it was when the estate was purchased. All the houses are being built on the Co-partnership principle.

FRENCH SOCIETY FOR DESTITUTE CHILDREN. 

IN the department of the Gironde a Society, mainly supported by private subscriptions, but with a small State subsidy, has been doing excellent work during the last 20 years among the destitute and friendless children. 

It receives the children of unworthy parents, the young street arabs, vagrants or thieves. They are taken when quite young, and those too young to have been corrupted are at once boarded out with carefully selected families under regular inspection. The foster-parents are paid for their maintenance up to the age of 12. About 200 are thus placed yearly.

But supplementary to this are 3 colonies at which the children are received who cannot be thus dealt with. The one at Lecocq takes between 40 and 50, between the ages of 8-13, gives them schooling, but no manual work; the St. Louis colony gives thorough agricultural training, both theoretical and practical, to about 100 boys from 13-18 years, with 1 hrs. school work, when regular employment is found for them. This colony is a Reformatory. To complete the work, however, a third colony has been started at Château de Gensac, which only receives those who have been reformed at St. Louis and carries their training still further, giving special attention to wheat growing and stock breeding. Marks of merit are awarded weekly, each being worth 5 centimes; one-third is given to the pupil, the remainder deposited to his account in a savings bank. A pupil who gains several of these marks a week and gains also prizes for special services may earn as much as 15 frs. a month. When one reads the past record of some of these children —with criminal parents, often themselves becoming hardened in crime—it is most satisfactory to learn that out of 3,000 children thus dealt with 97 per cent. have turned out well.

Not only are prizes offeredthis year to the amount of £400 (10,000 fr.)-but as will be seen from the illustration the whole staff of employees are called together and publicly invited to come forward and make any suggestion they please. This scheme has worked so well that during the first three months in which it was in operation the cash value to the firm of these suggestions was more than double the prize money distributed. 

Messrs. Brunner & Co. (Ltd.) are setting a good example in the efforts they are making to promote the educational and physical welfare of their employees. No young person is employed unless he has passed Standard IV. in a Day School, and during his employment until he reaches the age of 19 he is required to attend Evening Classes at least three times out of four that the school is open. Apprentices are allowed to attend classes in certain subjects during work hours. Sick pay at the rate of 8/- a week for all over 

18 years, and 5/- for those under, is given by the firm for 13 weeks, and in the case of the death of a workman over 18 years the firm grants £8, £6 on the death of his wife, and £2 of a child under 14 years, and £4 on the death of an employee under 18 years. A work's doctor may be consulted free of charge, and medicines are given as ordered. Every workman receives a week's holiday with full pay, and every foreman a fortnight.

(Ltd.), Dublin.—We gave a short account of the Welfare work of this firm in April last year, since then that work has been greatly extended by the erection of a Recreation Hall generously provided by the Company, at a cost of £1,600, for the benefit of their more than 2,000 employees. A VILLAGE UTOPIA. 

AN agricultural village in which half the houses are on the telephone, where the cottagers can ring up the shopkeepers in the neighbouring towns and order their stores without taking the journey, where the village school has been transformed into a paradise which is to the children a perpetual joy, where there are motor cars for the teachers and a motor launch for the youngsters-it sounds like "News from Nowhere," and yet it really is in the very heart of England. It is a fine instance of what can be done when wealth is held as a trust.

This Village Utopia is Bredon's Norton, in Worcestershire, 5 miles from Tewksbury; the wavers of the magic wand are Mrs. 

Woodhull Martin and her daughter Miss Zula Woodhull. Before they came upon the scene, says a writer in the Cheltenham Looker-on, "the village was in the tumble-down condition which is typical of so many decaying agricultural villages in England to-day. The village school was managed in the bad, old, inefficient style, and the villagers were as much cut off from communication from the outside world as if they had been in the centre of the African Continent.” 

Americans by birth, Mrs. Woodhull Martin and her daughter determined to make the village which had come into their possession, not only tolerable, but so attractive that none would wish to leave it for

PROGRESS 

the towns. The first step was to cut up the estate into small holdings of from 5 to 25 acres; the next to restore and beautify the Old Manor House and establish there a Woman's Agricultural Club, where now about 30 women are studying agriculture under competent teachers. This Club is now self supporting. poker D 

By courtesy of Miss Woodhull.

A FAVOURITE RETREAT OF THE VILLAGE SCHOOL CHILDREN. 

They next attacked the village school, and after a hard fight with the Education Authorities they persuaded them to allow the whole school to be run on true Froebel principles. Miss Woodhull does not use the word "Kindergarten"; she regards Froebel's teaching

Locker On as something much more than the mere working of patterns in wool and making models with cardboard. It recognises that a child's mind is not a blank to be filled up, but is full of latent intelligence to be drawn out, consequently it is equally adapted to the older as to the younger children. To give this, two trained teachers from the 

Froebel Education Institute have been brought from London, and the whole teaching up to the highest standard is arranged with a y courtesy of Miss Woodhull.
