We are told that "the finest gramophone places the best music of the day at the disposal of these village children, and they are taught to march to the strains of an auto-piano in order that the teacher may be able to give them her whole attention." 

We are not surprised to learn that children from neighbouring villages wish to attend indeed, the school accommodation is already so overtaxed

Such an example should do much to transform village life, and help to solve the problem of how to stop the rural exodus. 

FOLK SONGS AND DANCES. 

THE revival of the Old Folk Songs in order to counteract the baneful influence of the Music Halls by cultivating a taste for something purer and more ennobling is much to be desired. We are glad to note that strenuous efforts in this direction are now being made in more than one quarter.

The London Schools Musical and Dramatic Association recently founded by Dr. Frederick Rose, Educational Adviser to the L.C.C., is one of these. It deals with the children; but not confining itself to song, it seeks also to cultivate a taste for good instrumental music and simple, healthy drama. Dr. Rose thinks that "grown-up people are past praying for," and that "after twenty-five their tastes are formed;" his hope lies with the children. in the elementary school. Mainly through his efforts this Association has now been formed, having for its objects :-(1) To collect suitable songs, music and plays, and render them accessible to its members; (2) To assist elementary schools in arranging and interchanging performances; (3) To obtain for its members music, musical instruments, etc., at reduced prices. Every possible assistance will be given in advice, money, choice of plays and music, loan of materials, etc., to all who desire to arrange for performances by school children, and the Association will itself take in hand the interchange of such performances between different schools. One of its main objects will be the compilation of an Elementary School Song Book with music, in order to rescue from oblivion the beautiful 

Folk Songs of our own and other nations. Members are asked to visit the schools in order to discover such talent as is available for singing, instrumental or dramatic performances. Lists of suitable plays, songs, and instrumental music have already been drawn up and circulated. This is but the beginning of a movement which admits of large extension. Dr. Rose would have a stage connected with every school, so that by the cultivation of a purer taste in the young, the taste for the vulgar, debasing songs and plays now too much in vogue, would be destroyed. 

The Association for the revival and practice of Folk Music, of which Lord Lytton is the President, and Miss Mary Neal's

Espérance Club the Missionary Centre, is working for the revival of Folk Songs, Games, and Morris Dances in all parts of the country, rural as well as urban. The Espérance Club had long made music, dancing, and play-acting a feature of its activities, but had come to the end of its tether, when an article on Folk Songs by Mr. Cecil 

Sharp set them on a new tack.

The songs were first of all introduced to the Club girls who, says Miss Neal, went "perfectly mad" over them.

The next thing was to find the dances and dance music to fit in with the spirit of the Folk Song. To this again Mr. Sharp helped by putting them in the way of getting the old Morris Dance tunes. A modest public performance was then given in the Small 

Queen's Hall, which was so popular that hundreds of letters poured in from people interested in village life -teachers, club leaders and others—and now the Espérance members are teaching these dances in counties North, South, East and West of England. They are awakening quite a new interest in village life. Miss Neal (50, Cumberland Market, N.W.) kindly offers to answer any question or to send teachers to spread the movement.

BRADFORD LAND SETTLEMENT SCHEME. 

HOW THE LAND IS LAID OUT. 

MENS GARDENS

The Land Settlement Scheme (Bradford).—That which now goes by this name owes its origin to a hopeful experiment to provide work on the land for the unemployed which was made some two years ago by the City Guild of Help. The results were sufficiently satisfactory to lead to the formation of a Company to carry the experiment still further, and on a larger scale. The Share Capital is £1,000, of which £500 has been called up. 

With this 10 acres of land have been leased from the Bradford Corporation on their Esholt estate. Work began in February last year with 5 men who at once started to break up 5 acres and submit it to intensive spade culture. That they worked with a will is evident from the fact that in the first summer they had been able to raise large crops of beans, peas, cauliflowers, and other vegetables, and in the winter there was a crop of 10,000 savoys and a great quantity of young winter cabbage. In addition to these they had a patch of from 4,000 to 

5,000 wallflowers and some 1,500 eyes of rhubard. Roughly speaking, the sale of produce up to the end of last year realised £50. 

The settlers, who at first slept in a large caravan, using a rough wooden building knocked together by one of their number, are now comfortably housed in a substantially-built cottage containing a large living-room with large square window, scullery in which is a bath, 2 bedrooms and an attic. An adjoining and similar cottage is occupied by the Superintendent, an experienced market gardener. 

Each cottage has a good-sized garden.. An acre and a half is to be reserved for a pony, fowls, and pigs-the pony with a cart will take the produce to the Bradford market. The Settlement Company limits its dividends to 4 per cent. on share capital. Any surplus will be divided among the settlers.

The Marple Dale Farm Colony.-This Manchester experiment is a year younger than that of Bradford. Each has the same object -to train unemployed men to work on the land, so that ultimately they may be able to earn their own living either in service as skilled agriculturists or on their own small holdings. Up to last month 12 men were employed and trained, under an experienced manager, in general farm work, horticulture, poultry rearing, etc., but in consequence of lack of funds it has been found necessary to reduce that number by one-half. In addition to the general farm on which the full working day is spent, each man has a quarter-acre allotment, on which he grows his potatoes. Every man receives Is. a week as pocket-money and 4s. a week is sent to the wife and family of each married man, and the same amount put to the credit of the

PROGRESS 

unmarried, in addition to which they have board and lodging, free boots and some clothing-altogether equivalent to about 17s. a week each. This is reckoned a fair maintenance wage during the period of training. Mr. W. Price-Heywood (Hon. Secretary and Treasurer) reports :-"We have had some difficulty in getting men who will work steadily, but we hope to succeed in showing a few how to maintain themselves on the land." This does not seem to have been the experience at Bradford, where all but one-who had to leave on account of ill-health-have continued cheerfully and steadily at their work.

CATHOLIC SOCIAL WORK. 

have that of— REPORTS of quite a number of social activities, carried on by Catholics in various parts, have reached us recently. Among them we 

St. Hugh's House (Clapham Park) with its Cripples' Home, in which boys-crippled mentally as well as physically—are trained for such occupations as they are capable of following, and thus made more fit for the battle of life; the workshops at ST. CRISPIN'S, where, under a competent and certificated instructor, they are taught high-class hand-sewn bootmaking, and at ST. JOSEPH's, where they make brushes and brooms. Free lectures and lantern talks on 

Social and Rescue Work are organised by the St. Hugh's House 

Committee, and given at various Convents and Colleges. Another interesting branch of this work is that of the FISHER'S CLUB for boys, providing a Labour Bureau, a chip-carving class, lectures, and social evenings with games and musical and dramatic entertainments. 

The Catholic Women's League, by uniting women in "a bond of common fellowship," aims at their higher education and the promotion of Social Work. For the furtherance of this object it issues as its organ The Crucible, an interesting and ably written quarterly, and is also offering to any member of the League, between the ages of 20 and 30, a scholarship of the value of £60 per annum for 2 years, to be held at the School of Economics. It has established Registers for voluntary and paid workers available for Social Work, seeking at the same time to find them suitable openings; it collects and gives all information likely to be of use to Catholic women in all matters relating to education, training for public and professional work, sick nursing, skilled trades, etc.; it collects and classifies every sort of information on Social and Charitable Work, and establishes courses of lectures and discussions on social, industrial,

and economic questions. Special courses of 6 months each are given in ST. MARY'S COLLEGE to train educated girls as children's nurses. The practical course includes the care and feeding of children from a fortnight to 5 years of age, duties and management of a nursery, cooking, laundry, needlework, etc. The theoretical course includes hygiene, a child's physiology, First aid, and Kindergarten methods. 

PROBATION OFFICERS. 

In the last number of "PROGRESS " attention was called to Mrs. Barnett's appeal for lady volunteers to act as Probation Officers under the recent Act. Lord Lytton, as Chairman of the State Children's 

Association, has followed that up with an appeal to the Chairmen of Petty Sessions to call meetings of residents in their several divisions "at which the great possibilities for child-reformation provided under this Act might be made known and the assistance of suitable persons obtained." Lord Lytton's appeal is especially directed to organised effort in country districts where the cases to be dealt with will be few, and he thus summarises its advantages:- (1) Voluntary effort would be focussed on the reclamation of the nation's most important asset, its children, a large number of whom only need a helping hand at a critical period to become law-respecting citizens. (2) Having a choice of helpers it would be possible to place each child under the care of an officer specially qualified to deal with its particular case. (3) When it is understood that the Probation Officer is, above all, the friend of child offenders, the public will no longer hesitate to bring these for correction to the kindly discrimination of the Court, hitherto unable to deal with them save by discharge or conviction.

It is a fine opportunity for Social Service of the highest kind, and all who are interested in the welfare of these children should make sure that prompt action is taken on this appeal. 

VACATION PLAYING FIELDS. 

THIS institution, though older in years, is really the complement of the Vacation

School, and like that, was started in America. It was the outcome of a conviction that "the boy without a playground is father to the man without a job," and that it is during play that children learn many of the lessons that fit them for the sterner duties of life. 

It began in a small way in 1886. Sandheaps were placed in two open spaces in the City of Boston (Mass.), which were soon taken advantage of by the children. A year later the daily attendance reached 400, and teachers trained in Kindergarten methods were appointed to * See article by Mrs. Humphry Ward, "PROGRESS," Jan., 1906; also April, 1908.

PROGRESS 

supervise and organise the play. The popularity of the Playing 

Field grew so rapidly that in 1900 there were no fewer than 21 in the City of Boston with an average attendance of 4,000 children. 

The example of Boston has been widely followed throughout the States, and the movement is spreading in all directions. 

The one essential to these playgrounds is the sandpit; where space permits there may be also see-saws, swings, and Maypoles; but a sandpit the children must have, or they will still continue to make mud pies in the gutter with an old tin. The need for supervision and organisation is most evident where swings, see-saws, etc., are provided. The "ape and tiger" instinct is still strong in many of them, and they have yet to learn that "might" and "right" are not always synonymous. Without this supervision the small child would often be turned out of a swing by a bigger one, or a girl rudely pushed aside by a boy. But the playground has been found an excellent school for teaching fairness, unselfishness, and chivalry, and teachers testify to a marked improvement in these playground children, both in discipline and moral tone, when they resume the ordinary school routine. There has been also a notable decrease in juvenile crime in the neighbourhood of these playing fields; youthful energies which must find expression and which often find it in acts of turbulence or even crime, have here been provided with a healthy outlet. That they are popular with the children, notwithstanding the supervision, is proved by the long distances they will walk to get the opportunity to play amid pleasant surroundings. 

Miss Hutchinson (Organising Secretary to the York Health and 

Housing Reform Association), to whom we are indebted for the above account of the Boston experiment, gives also some particulars of the cost of the 2 Playing Fields recently started by her Association in her own city. The fields were used for 4 weeks daily from 9 a.m. till noon.

In one case where everything had to be paid for-rent of field, salary of lady to organise and of 2 men to take charge, and such requisites as sandpit, swings, see-saw, bats, spades, skipping ropes, etc.—the cost amounted to less than £20. In the other case the field was lent, and for two weeks the games were organised by voluntary helpers; this reduced the cost to just over £15. Nearly half of that, however, was spent in material (swings, etc.), much of which will be available another year. As Miss Hutchinson says: "This is surely a small price to pay to secure for the children of our Elementary Schools a privilege enjoyed in all Secondary."

Old Age Pensions in Oldham.-Last Easter Mr. Butterworth, a large employer of labour in Oldham and a firm believer in a national non-contributory and non-discriminatory scheme of Old Age Pensions, decided to anticipate the Government by giving out of his own pocket 5s. to every person in the town-rich or poorwho had reached the age of 65. The town is divided into 10 wards, and to each of these wards a Biblewoman had been appointed. 

The distribution was made through this agency; each Biblewoman undertaking her own ward, and practically visiting the house of each applicant to test the genuineness of the case. Altogether 6,176 applied for the gift, many of whom did not stand in need of it, but as Mr. Butterworth had invited "rich and poor," many took it to give away to cases of poverty in which they took a special interest. 

500 inmates of the Workhouse received the 5s., but it has not yet been possible to find out how many are prepared to leave if they could receive the 5s. regularly. It is most satisfactory to learn that though every effort was made to learn to what extent the money was spent in drink only 6 such cases were discovered. 

In most cases the gift has been a great boon and gratefully received.

The Land Club.-Mention was made of this in a short notice of Mr. Montague Fordham's "Mother Earth" which appeared in the last number of "PROGRESS." Since then Mr. Fordham has contributed an article to the Daily News in which he states that already a dozen of these clubs have been started and are in working order. 

As a rule they meet monthly and tackle the various problems that arise under the Small Holdings Act. One club ran a housing candidate for the District Council. At present they are only feeling their way to fuller developments, though they have already begun to set about organising co-operation.

PROGRESS 

Prohibition Areas in England and Wales.—Mr. Chas. Roberts,

M.P., in his book on "The Time Limit and Local Option" brings out the fact, revealed by the Home Office returns, that of the 12,995 civil parishes in rural districts in England and Wales, there are 3,903 (more than 30 per cent.) in which there is no on-license. 

In the county of Lincolnshire 45 per cent. are no-license parishes. 

The Books that are read.-An "Investigator” on behalf of the Westminster Gazette gives in that journal (April 7) some interesting facts.

He states that there has been a remarkable growth of serious reading during the last few years; the borrowing of novels is declining all over the country, not more than 15 per cent. of the work done by our public libraries is in that direction. In the public libraries of the United Kingdom there are 4,000,000 reference, and 8,000,000 lending books; 11,000,000 reference books are consulted every year according to the records, and at least an equal number are taken from the shelves and consulted without being recorded. 

Every year 60,000,000 books are lent for home reading. Giving further detail the "Investigator" states that "the taste for history, biography and travel is on the wane ; readers are all for Science and 

Sociology, and new books on Socialism are always in demand." 

The L.C.C. Open-Air Schools. The result of the experiment in this direction at Bostal Woods (described in an article contributed to our last number by Dr. Frederick Rose) was so satisfactory that the Education Authority of the L.C.C. has decided to increase last year's grant of £400 to £2,000 for 1908, in order to provide three open-air Schools in different parts of London. The places selected are 

Shooter's Hill (which takes the place of Bostal Woods), Forest Hill, and Kentish Town. The latter we visited a few days before the 

School was opened. The wooden buildings are placed in the beautiful and spacious grounds of a mansion long untenanted. 

One was amazed to find so much beauty in close proximity to so much squalor. 

It was delightful to think of slum children spending their school hours amid such healthy and pleasant surroundings. The Schools are to be open from June 1st to October 31st, each will provide for 75 children, divided into three classes of 25 each. The staff will consist of one head and three assistant teachers, a nurse, a cook and helper, and school keeper. It is expected that with Government grants the £2,000 will cover all expenses, except the absolutely necessary school-feeding. school-feeding. For this private funds are relied on.

The Housing Question; Distribution of Population. Mr. 

Nettlefold in his "Practical Housing" gives some striking figures to show on what an insignificant proportion of England's acreage the vast majority of her population is housed. The total area is 

20,000,000 acres, of which only 4,000,000 is comprised within the Boroughs and Urban Districts; the total population is 32,500,000, of whom 25,000,000 live in those Boroughs and Urban Districts, that is to say, nearly 77 per cent. are living on 20 per cent. of the land. But this is by no means all. Of the 4,000,000 acres comprised in Boroughs and Urban Districts, 500,000 belong to the former and 3,500,000 to the latter; but when considering the amount occupied by dwelling houses, at least three-fourths must be deducted from the total Borough acreage for business premises, railways, undeveloped land, etc., while from the total Urban 

District area must be deducted (according to carefully prepared statistics) all but one-fiftieth. One-fourth of 500,000 is 125,000; one-fiftieth of 3,500,000 is 70,000; together equal to 195,000, or say 200,000 acres. That is to say, two-fifths of the population of 

England and Wales are actually housed on sooth part of the land of the country; another two-fifths on 26th part, and the remaining one-fifth are scattered over the rest of the land; in other words, 26 millions live on about 5th and 6½ millions on 18th of the land. 

London's Homeless Poor. A recent report' of the Medical 

Officer of the L.C.C. gives some suggestive statistics concerning the number of persons in certain areas who one night last February either would not or could not obtain any shelter but the street. In these areas the council's officers counted 1,998 men and 402 women -a total of 2,400. Of these, 1,315 were found on the Embankment, Westminster, Soho, St. James's Park, and the Marble Arch. 

The report states further that on that same night there were 7,000 vacant beds in common lodging-houses which could have been had at about 4d. the night, and in addition there were 700 vacant beds which could have been had free in the casual wards. This last fact suggests the inquiry: Why did 700 persons on a cold February night choose to walk about the streets rather than accept the hospitality of the Poor Law Guardians?

Books on Social Subjects. 

Continuation Schools in England and Elsewhere.

-Edited by 

M. E. SADLER, M.A. 779pp. Manchester University Press. 

8s. 6d. 

The Manchester University has rendered valuable service by the publication of these admirable reports on the Continuation Schools in this and other countries. Few subjects are of greater importance at the present time, and if we are to hold our own in the industrial competition of the world we must act promptly and energetically with a view to a very decided improvement in our present position. 

This book should do much to stimulate action. At present we lag far behind, as will be seen from the valuable chapters on the 

Continuation Schools in Denmark, Germany, Switzerland, and the

United States. In dealing with Germany it is to be regretted that so little mention is made of the remarkable developments in Württenburg, which by the law coming into force next April, has taken a great step in advance.* Munich was probably singled out for a special chapter on account of the leading position taken by Dr. 

Kerchensteiner in the development of these schools, andfor excellent work it stands as high as any city in Germany. In chapter XV. on "Should Attendance at Continuation Schools be made Compulsory in England?" Professor Sadler makes a valuable contribution to a much debated question. But in order to reap anything like the full benefit of such compulsory attendance it will be necessary to materially improve the preliminary training of those who attend them. 

Foreign Solutions of Poor Law Problems.-By EDITH SELLERS. 

176pp. Horace Marshall & Son. 

2s. 6d.

Those who have read the articles contributed from time to time by Miss Sellers to some of our leading reviews will welcome this reprint, and those who have not read the articles should read them in the book. Her object in writing is thus stated in the introduction : 

The articles contained in this scheme were written for the express purpose of showing that some of the very problems with which we here are still grappling have already been solved elsewhere in a fairly satisfactory fashion-the problem of the State child, for instance, of the worn-out worker, and the loafing vagrant, as well as that of obtaining money's worth for money. 

Miss Sellers does not rely on second-hand information. She herself has personally visited the various countries and made painstaking * Vide article on that subject in the present No., pp. 175-184.

investigation into all the subjects she writes about. 

Perfect accuracy of judgment on all matters is not of course to be expected, and, speaking from a personal and somewhat intimate knowledge, we think she has hardly dealt fairly with the case of Berlin. The difficulties there are enormous, but they are being met with courage and wisdom. Owing to these difficulties Berlin is the last place that should have been selected as an example of the working of the 

Elberfeld system.

This however, is the only adverse criticism we have to pass on an otherwise excellent book. The chapter on "The State Children of 

Hungary" might seem like a fairy tale, but from official documents in the Institute Library we can testify that it is not. 

West Ham: A Study in Social and Industrial Problems.- Compiled by EDWARD G. HOWARTH, M.A., and MONA WILSON. 

1907. 423pp. J. M. Dent & Co. 6s. net.

The Outer London Inquiry Committee was formed in 1905 with the object of investigating the conditions of life in the ring of smaller municipalities and urban districts which surround the County of London. They are part of London economically, but are disconnected in administration, and it is generally recognised that they have long been, from many points of view, in a deplorable condition. 

Unfortunately Mr. Charles Booth for obvious reasons had to exclude them from the masterly survey of "The Life and Labour of London" which he organised several years ago, and it has been left to the Outer London Inquiry Committee to take up the work. 

And first of all it must be said that the performance reflects the very highest credit on Miss Mona Wilson, Mr. A. Sawyer, Mr. E. G.

Howarth and Mr. J. H. Morgan, who were charged with the actual making of the survey, and placing the result before the General Committee, the subscribers, and the public. It certainly would justify the General Committee in going to their supporters for further contributions to enable it to take up the survey of another area. In this connection it is to be regretted that the accounts of the undertaking are not given in the volume-it is a very valuable opportunity lost of bringing before the public a practical demonstration of the need for work of this nature and the relatively small cost when men of the experience and capacity of those named can be found willing to give their services voluntarily. 

The report itself reveals a state of things which must make even the most optimistic of us feel disheartened. The three main

PROGRESS 

divisions of the report deal with (a) Housing; (b) Employment and 

Wages; and (c) Local Government. 380 pages contain well written descriptions of the district, the accuracy of which is ascertainable by anyone who is doubtful from the tables of figures appended to chapters and from the references to the authorities quoted. It is significant that eleven pages are more than sufficient to contain an account of the various ameliorative agencies that are at work trying to keep within bounds the flood of poverty, misery and quite unnecessary suffering that follow from unregulated or its regulated industrial growth. cc at the 

The problem of West Ham is the problem of Casual Labour. 

Here are some of the conclusions of the report :best, the casual worker is continually in search of fresh employment. 

Above all men therefore, he requires mobility, and this is exactly what he does not possess.

There is a tendency for industries employing cheap women's and children's labour to arise in casual labour districts, and the casual labourer is often kept in a district by his family's occupation." The end of this is that the women and children support the man, who degenerates into a loafer. "Whatever justification or excuse there may be for it, the inadequate and temporary assistance afforded by able-bodied outdoor relief, by two or three days' work in the Labour Ward, or under the Distress Committee, or the Church Army, or by gifts of food or money from charitable sources, must tend to perpetuate the present state of things. 

Employers of casual labour openly welcome such relief as a rate in aid of wages.

It is comfortable to them to know that the man whom they have employed on Monday may be employed on relief work on Tuesday. They are freed from any sense of responsibility.. The remedy to some extent lies in the hands of the employers of labour. If they would open their eyes to the fact that the system of casual employment produces the workmen whose defects they are always eager to point out-men who are unable to maintain themselves in good physical condition and whose habits of regular work have been undermined by periods of enforced idlenessmanufacturers and others would be far less ready to make a convenience of casual labour." 

But we must not rest content with merely leaving the responsibility there. It is neither the labourer nor his employer who is to blame. 

It is the community as a whole, and we must each take our share of the responsibility, and of the stupendous task of putting it right. J. McK.

Land and the Landless.-By GEO. CADBURY, JUNR. and TOM BRYAN, M.A. 18opp. 

18opp. Headley Bros. 

Is. net.

This book-one of the series of Social Service Handbooks, edited by Mr. Percy Alden, M.P.-deals with "the revival of English rural life," which the authors describe-a description which no one will dispute as "one of the most pressing problems of the day." 

Many books dealing with the same subject have appeared in the last few years, but there is plenty of room for this-indeed, we know of no other that places the problem before us so clearly and concisely. 

The authors have wisely recognised that the first step towards getting out of our present difficulty is "to understand how we got into it"; accordingly, they have devoted considerable attention to the causes that have resulted in a change from land-use to land-ownership and the consequent "degradation of the labourer” and the “ decay of the yeoman." In pointing the way out "The Example of 

Denmark" is given an interesting chapter to itself, a careful study of which should be made by all who desire the revival of English rural life. The final chapter on "The Conditions of Success" puts before the reader, in about twenty pages, all the salient points of the problem, referring specially to the need of Co-operation—not only for purchase and production, but also for the establishment of Banks on the Raiffeisen principle—the revival of subsidiary industries, and the awakening of a social life in our villages. After all" say the writers, "the rural and agricultural problem is as much a problem of the man, as it is a problem of the land; . . . . the supreme need of the moment is not Acts of Parliament, nor even millionaire philanthropists, to work miracles in the way of re-peopling the waste places of England; the need is rather for some English Bishop 

Grundtvig' who should call into activity the highest and best in English manhood, both in town and country." 

Essays in Municipal Administration.—By JOHN, A. FAIrlie, Ph.D. 374pp. The Macmillan Co. (New York). 

Americans are doing well to study the Municipal Institutions of other countries as well as their own, and to make known to their own countrymen the results of their investigations. In the book before us Dr. Fairlie does both. The arrangement of the book is admirable; the several essays are classified under three main groupsin the first are those relating to problems of organisation and the legal relation of the cities to the State; in the second, those dealing

PROGRESS 

with municipal functions and activities; while the third deals specially with municipal government in Europe. The first two groups are occupied almost entirely with the municipal affairs of the United States, but in the second we find an interesting chapter comparing the revenue systems of America with those of foreign countries, in which are several valuable tables both of income and expenditure in many of the larger cities in the two Continents. 

The section dealing specially with Municipal Government in Europe has four chapters devoted severally to Great Britain, some European cities, Vienna and Italy. Dr. Fairlie's inquiries have been thorough and his judgments are impartial and appreciative. 

British Socialism.-By J. ELLIS BARKER. 522pp. Smith, Elder 

&Co. IOS. 6d.

A Critical Examination of Socialism.-By W. H. MALLock. 

302pp. John Murray. 6s.

New Worlds for Old. By H. G. WELLS. 353pp. Constable. 6s. 

If the number of books on Socialism which are now appearing is any indication of the interest taken in the subject it must be considerable. And indeed it is. Go where one will it is sure to be a prominent topic of conversation, and often with those who show the most amusing ignorance of what they are talking about. Mr.

Barker in "British Socialism" has done good service by showing the folly of judging Socialism by the wild statements either of its friends or foes. After a careful examination of almost all the party literature on this subject, he deals with it not as a scientific abstraction, but as a business proposition. Though writing as an Anti-Socialist, he nevertheless recognises that "Socialism is not only an elaborate economic doctrine, but at the same time it is a complete system of practical politics;" accordingly he sets himself to inquire, “What are the views of the Socialist with regard to the Banks, the National 

Currency, Education, Foreign Policy, Agriculture, Taxation, etc." 

Mr. Barker, however, looks for salvation to Tariff Reform. 

Mr. Mallock is well known as a lusty opponent of Socialism he devotes a considerable part of his "Critical Examination of Socialism" to an examination of the Marxian theory. Admitting that it may be true "as applied to primitive communities," he contends that it utterly fails to account for the increased wealth of the modern world. That increase he attributes to the development of what he calls "the directing class; he holds that the increase in wealth during the last 150 years is mainly due to the ability of 

---

the few, and that the ability of these few is necessary to a continuance of wealth production. Mr. Mallock recognises that the more intelligent Socialists of to-day—especially those in America—would · admit this, but contends that under any system of Socialism whatever it would be impossible to offer rewards sufficient to induce these men of ability" systematically to develop, and be willing to exercise, their exceptional faculties. (c Mr. Wells appears as a doughty champion on the other side. 

Writing with breezy optimism he has a firm faith that sooner or later his "New World" will have replaced the "Old." He believes that this industrial "peace on earth" will be brought about through the working of industrial "good will amongst men." That he is no revolutionary Socialist is plain from the following passages taken from different chapters :- 

I have tried to let it become apparent that while I do firmly believe, not only in the splendour and nobility of the Socialist dream, but in its ultimate practicability, I do also recognise quite clearly that with people just as they are now, with their prejudices, their ignorances, their misapprehensions, their unchecked vanities and greeds and jealousies, their crude and misguided instincts, their irrational traditions, no Socialist State can exist, no better State can exist, than the one we have now with all its squalor and cruelty. . . : 

Unless you can change men's minds you cannot effect Socialism, and when you have made clear and universal certain broad understandings, Socialism becomes a mere matter of science and devices and applied intelligence. That is the constructive Socialist's position. 

It is difficult to attempt anything like an analysis of this interesting book or indeed to single out any particular chapter, but the one on "The Spirit of Gain and the Spirit of Service" should be read by all who wish to understand the position of the Constructive 

Socialist. Mr. Wells emphatically denies that a Socialist State would "abolish competition, on the contrary it looks to competition for the 

Service of the World."

Diseases of Occupation. THOMAS OLIVER, M.D., F.R.C.P. 

427pp. Methuen. IOS. 6d. net.

One of a series of useful books issued by the new Library of Medicine, all dealing with the all important question of national health. Dr. Oliver is Professor of Physiology in the Durham 

University, and Physician to the Royal Victoria Infirmary, Newcastle-on-Tyne. His work lies in the midst of a great industrial centre, and the diseases about which he writes will constantly come under his observation.

Dr. Oliver justly says that "no matter what Parliamentary 

Legislation may enact, industrial hygiene will never be secured until

PROGRESS 

the workers themselves are educated in regard to the dangers incidental to particular trades." It is to be feared that not many of the workers will be able to read through a volume of more than 400 pages, though every effort has been made to give the information in as popular a form as possible and avoid technicalities, bnt if the book is found in all the public libraries of our industrial towns, many might read the particular chapters devoted to their own special occupation. Among the subjects dealt with are Work and Fatigue ; 

Women's Work; Diseases due to impure air in the Factory and 

Workshop; also to dust (organic and inorganic); to working in compressed air; to micro-organisms and parasites; Diseases the result of work in high temperatures, etc. There is also a valuable chapter on the Aims of Factory Legislation and what it has accomplished.

Work and Wages, in continuation of Lord Brassey's “Work and Wages” and “Foreign Work and Wages.' "Part II., Wages and Employment.-By SIDNEY J. CHAPMAN, M.A., 

Professor of Political Economy in the University of Manchester.

With an Introduction by Lord Brassey. Longmans, Green 

Co. 1908. pp. 22 and 494. IOS. 6d. net.

It would be a thankless task to criticise this volume as a contribution to economic literature, since its author himself would not expect it to be regarded in that light. It will suffice here to indicate its contents which, if they were brought more nearly up to date, would be a very useful storehouse of facts and figures for the economic student. The first chapter entitled analytical groundwork, in accordance with the advice of the author, may be omitted-only professional economists are interested in the abstractions it deals with-and the second chapter in its treatment of English Trade Unions concerns chiefly those who are studying for legal examinations, since the obscurities of the Taff Vale case were swept out of practical consequence, to the great content of all concerned, by the 

Trades Disputes Act, 1906. An interesting account of American, 

French and German Trade Organisations follows, together with very elaborate and useful statistics. Chapter 3 treats of the policy of Trade Unions in respect of such subjects as machinery and apprenticeship. Chapter 4 deals with Sliding Scales (with statistics to the year 1900), Arbitration and Wages Boards. Chapter 5 

explains Unemployment and its remedies, and Chapter 6 Workmen's Insurance and Pensions. On all these subjects the authorities,

American and Continental as well as English, from whom Professor Chapman has collected his facts or borrowed his opinions, are adequately described, so that any student can easily ascertain where to go for first-hand information. The description of the American 

Trade Union Label and the discussion of the vexed question of restriction of output by Unions are specially useful as things not always easy to find when wanted. 

Administration of Charity.-By ARTHUR PATERSON. 

City Council for the Organisation of Charity. IS.

E. R. P. 115pp. 

Reprints of a series of articles written for the Times. Starting with the fact (given on the authority of the Charities Register and Digest) that "the total income of bona fide charitable agencies in, or available for, London was, in 1906, £10,402,232," and with the other fact that certain classes (children, cripples, the aged, etc.) need much more adequate assistance than they now receive, the writer endeavours to show that by a less wasteful administration of existing funds this could be accomplished without resort to State aid. Mr. 

Paterson finds the solution of the problem in the formation of an "Association of Subscribers to Charitable Funds,"* with the following objects :- (a) The promotion of systematized co-operation between charities with kindred objects, and the co-ordination of their work where, in the opinion of their subscribers, better results could be obtained by such action. (b) The establishment of uniform principles and methods among charities in their distribution of relief, especially with regard to their relation with the workmen's friendly societies and trade societies on the one hand and the Poor Law and other public authorities granting aid in distress on the other hand. (c) The promotion of facilities of a systematic kind for useful interchange of information among all charities for the purpose of preventing overlapping and imposture. (d) The consideration of ways and means to extend the work and strengthen the financial resources of all bona fide charities subscribing to the main objects of the "Association."

The objects are admirable, but there is no one of them which is not covered by the aims of the British Institute of Social Service, indeed this was the very work which the Chairman of its Council (Lord Lytton), at the recent Annual Meeting advised the Institute to take in hand so soon as it received sufficient financial support to warrant it. * In an important Report on the "Organisation of Charity," presented to the Swiss Federal Government, by Dr. Zollinger, a recommendation is made that "from time to time temporary classes should be held for those who are engaged in the administration of Charity, and in large communities one Central Institution should direct, but not administer, local effort."

National League for Physical Education and Improvement. 

ITS AIMS, ACTIVITIES, AND INTERESTS. 

Chairman of Executive Council- THE LORD BISHOP OF RIPON. 

Chairman of London Branch- THE LORD CHIEF JUSTICE. 

EARLY in the year the Executive Council of the League appointed a Sub-Committee to draught a circular on the subject of Health Visiting, a subject on which the Council has lately concentrated much of its attention. The Sub-Committee drew up the following 

Report which was submitted to a meeting of the Executive Council (held on the 12th ult.) when it was approved, with instructions that it be published.

EXCESSIVE INFANT MORTALITY. 

THE high rate of Infant Mortality prevalent in many districts arises from causes which are preventible. If these causes are not adequately grappled with there is good reason to 

Any rate exceeding 80 anticipate that racial degeneration will result. per 1,000 should be regarded as unduly high.

Statistics show that the average rate during the period 1901-1905 for 

England and Wales was 138 per 1,000; and for London 141 per 1,000.

The districts of the Metropolis in which the rate is highest are:- 

SHOREDITCH FINSBURY 

... 173 160 

BERMONDSEY POPLAR 

... 159 

154 It should here be noted (a) that one-third of the total deaths of infants occur in the first month of life; (b) that three-fourths of the infants born in the country are healthy at birth. 

Causes and Preventives.-The influences which contribute to this most serious state of things are extremely well and succintly discussed by Dr. Newman,* who points out as the prime causes of the evil :— (a) The condition of the maternal physique. (b) Unsatisfactory feeding. (c) General mismanagement and exposure to cold. 

Directly personal factors in the physical well-being of infants are : breast feeding; access to a pure milk supply in cases in which artificial feeding must be resorted to; warmth; fresh air; cleanliness; sleep. 

Other and important contributory factors to be reckoned with are -Housing conditions; poverty; conditions of work; drink; ante-natal conditions. 

"Health of the State," chap. VIII. (Social Service Series, No. 2), 1907. Headley 

Bros. Is, net.

Instruction of Mothers. There is urgent necessity that mothers and prospective mothers should be efficiently cared for and instructed as to the management of themselves before and after child-birth, and as to the proper feeding and management of their offspring, especially during the first 12 months of life.

NEED FOR 

HEALTH VISITORS. 

These should be officially appointed.- 

In the case of girls and women past school age, the necessary care and instruction can only be properly secured by the appointment of efficient Health Visitors, who must be adequately remunerated. 

Personally," says Dr. Newman, "I am satisfied that this is one of the most useful methods of preventive work." cc Instances of what has already been done in this direction are the 

For the last 10 years the City of Birmingham has employed 

17 Health Visitors, all paid out of the Borough Rate. Their services are greatly valued by the Medical Officer of Health and the community generally.

Of The Sheffield Corporation have appointed a Chief Female Health 

Visitor with a number of subordinate salaried visitors under her. 

13,000 infants born in 1907, 9,458 were thus visited. 

Huddersfield, Glasgow, Liverpool, and Wakefield ought also to be specially mentioned in connection with the adoption of this progressive and enlightened policy.*

Of the Metropolitan Boroughs particular mention should be made of the official and unofficial action taken in St. Pancras—where a 

Mothers' and Infants' Society has been formed-and in Finsbury, where the Public Welfare Association has for some time past, by voluntary subscriptions, retained the services of a former female Health Visitor with most encouragingly successful results.† 

Duty of Authorities.-Local Authorities should :- (1) Appoint Female Health Visitors according to the needs of the population in each case. (2) In order to derive the full benefit of their services it is necessary that Local Authorities also adopt the 

Notification of Births Act, 1907. 

* In addition to the above may be mentioned the following municipalities, all of which have appointed Health Visitors, with Committees more or less fully organised :-Bradford, Dundee, Ilkeston, Keighley, Leeds, Leicester, Manchester, Nottingham, Reading, and 

Rotherham. We shall be glad to receive the names of others. [ED.] †The conditions now obtaining in all the large towns in England and Wales as to the appointment and work of Women Health Visitors and Inspectors are well described in a pamphlet, entitled "Women as Inspectors," price 3d., recently issued by the Central 

Bureau for the Employment of Women, 9, Southampton Street, W.C.

PROGRESS THE VISITORS' FUNCTIONS. 

THE principal functions of Women Health Visitors are:- 

(a) To advise expectant Mothers on the management of their health, and as to the influence of ante-natal conditions on their infants. (b) To follow up the notification of births, and to visit the homes when Doctor and Midwife have ceased to attend. (c) To note conditions of ignorant feeding or of non-supply of sufficient and nourishing food, and to take judicious steps to correct these evils. (d) To give advice on the proper feeding of infants; to warn 

Mothers against premature weaning; to inculcate in particular the importance of breast feeding until the teeth appear; and, above all, of the avoidance of the use of foul feeding bottles. (e) To promote and encourage the practice of weighing infants regularly, and to instruct or remind Mothers on points of general hygiene. (ƒ) To instruct and guide in their work the Voluntary District 

Visitors who assist in Health Visiting. 

(g) To note any insanitary or foul conditions in any of the homes. visited and in any of the inmates of the same, and to report these points to the Medical Officer of Health under whom they work. (h) To attend at factories during the dinner hour, at Mothers' Meetings, and on other suitable occasions for the purpose of giving talks on the subject to which their qualifications and the objects of their appointment relate.

The duties of Health Visitors should be clearly distinguished from those of School Visitors or Nurses, although the requisite qualifications are in most respects similar. School Nurses are employed directly by the Education Authority of a district. Health Visitors are attached to the staff of the Medical Officer of Health. The degree of differentiation must, however, be left to the decision of Local Authorities in accordance with the conditions confronting them. 

It is important to remember that the qualifica- 

THE VISITORS' tions of Health Visitors differ in many respects QUALIFICA

from those of Female Sanitary Inspectors.

The Health Visitors' qualifications should TIONS. 

comprise :- (a) Special theoretical and practical training in the care of infants and young children. (b) A Certificate of Proficiency in Hygiene granted by some recognised examining body.

(c) Some practical as well as theoretical knowledge of the elements of home nursing.* 

(d) It is also essential that Health Visitors be thoroughly tactful. 

It is a necessary incident of their employment that they should make themselves the real friends of the people whom they visit, and whom they are employed to instruct and assist. To make it clear, in fact, that they are there to help the mother, and not to find fault with her. 

THE fact should be strongly emphasised that 

Health Visitors must be properly remunerated according to the conditions of the locality of their employment, and to the pressure of the work devolving upon them. As the qualifications usually required of a competent Health 

Visitor presuppose five years' training, her remuneration should be calculated on an adequate scale, and must be sufficient to enable an educated woman to live with a reasonable amount of comfort. 

THE VISITORS' REMUNERATION. 

Opposition to the appointment of trained Health Visitors is experienced by some Local Authorities because the remuneration of the Visitor, as such, has not yet been made the subject of contribution at the hands of Central Authorities, who have only power to contribute to the cost of employing Female Sanitary Inspectors. To meet this objection, the London County Council are, by Section 19 of the General Powers Bill now before Parliament, seeking power to pay half the salaries of Health Visitors whenever they are officially appointed in the Council's area. Further, it is proposed by the Public Health Bill, which, however, is not a Government measure, to enable Local Authorities outside the Metropolitan Area to appoint Health Visitors subject to the approval of the County Council, which Council, it is proposed, shall make a contribution to the extent of two-thirds of the officers' salaries. 

VOLUNTARY HEALTH 

As a result of this difficulty of salary some 

Local Authorities are disposed to rely on the services of voluntary Health Visitors.† Zeal and goodwill, however, without the necessary knowledge, training, and experience do not suffice, neither can 

VISITORS.

systematic and regular service be required from Voluntary Workers. 

In a matter of such vital moment to the community, trained experts, working under the discipline which official employment for remuneration involves, are essential. In every populous place one expert 

Superintendent Health Visitor, at least, should be appointed. Bands of Voluntary Health Visitors may, however, very usefully act under the direction of such a Superintendent. A scheme of this kind is explained in the last Annual Report of the Cambridge Branch of the League. 

A similar scheme, with considerable elaboration, is being worked at Huddersfield.

SECONDARY EDUCATION ON INFANT MORTALITY. 

In conclusion, the Sub-Committee are of opinion that more attention should be given to the possibility of educational work in connection with Infant Mortality by those who are responsible, not only for the Elementary 

Education of children, but also for the Secondary Education of lads and girls. At the conclusion of their school lives such lads and girls arrive at an age when their sense of responsibility towards themselves and the future population of the country is ripe for wise cultivation, and when they themselves are old enough to profit by judiciously imparted, straightforward information.

THE CARE OF THE BABY. 

THE Council of the League, fully recognising that the physical improvement of the race must begin with the babies, has prepared a four-page LEAFLET, entitled "How to bring up a Baby.” This leaflet sets forth in concise form (avoiding all verbiage or unnecessary matter) certain practical directions under the following heads :- 

Natural and Artificial Feeding; Directions for Suckling, for Weaning and Feeding afterwards, and for Hand Feeding; Preparation and Quantities of Food to be given (giving a detailed table of quantities from the first fortnight to the end of the first year); 

Things to be Avoided, with certain General Directions to be followed. The leaflet has been drawn up in consultation with some of our best known medical experts, and was approved by them before being printed. It is hoped that it will prove of great service to 

Health Visitors, Guild of Help Workers, and others. It may be had on application to the British Institute of Social Service. FEEDING

THE MOTHER. 

THE provision of good wholesome food for mothers, or expectant mothers, is a movement which deserves every encouragement from those who are working for physical improvement. Now that breast-

feeding is recognised as of first importance if the excessive death-rate among infants is to be reduced, common sense suggests that in very many cases proper food must be provided for the mothers before they can give proper food to their babes. The report on this work which reaches us from Dundee, where the Dundee Social Union. has established two restaurants for this purpose, tells of a movement which might be profitably extended. The first was established 

Their object is threefold : 

May, 1906, the second March, 1907. (1) To encourage the breast-feeding of infants; (2) to discourage married women's work; and (3) to provide a centre for educational. work among women. (1) Breast-feeding is encouraged by the provision of good, nourishing dinners, consisting of soup, meat, potatoes, milk pudding, and bread, at a cost of 2d. each (or free in necessitous cases) for mothers who are nursing their own babies and who are unable to provide a proper meal for themselves. (2) To discourage married women's work a Maternity Club provides 1/6 to 3/- weekly for three months-varying according to the mother's previous payments-to mothers who stay from work to nurse their babies. During this time the mothers receive also free dinners. (3) The Educative work is at present limited to the weekly weighing of the infants, with advice as to feeding and management, and simple, practical lectures by a qualified Queen's Nurse, with long experience, on how to feed and clothe the baby. This part of the work combined with the home visiting of birth cases by two Lady Health Visitors specially appointed for this duty, has been found of great value, but the hope is that at no distant date a complete "School for Mothers" may be developed.

Any mother may bring her baby to be weighed, and during last year 293 availed themselves of the privilege. The results in the lessening of the rate of Infant Mortality are most encouraging. Among the 

130 entered on the books for the year preceding August, 1907, the death-rate was only 6 per cent. as compared with 20 per cent. for the district. 84 per cent. of these mothers fed their babies from the breast until they returned to work. 

The Maternity Hospital at the Royal Infirmary is rendering great assistance by reporting suitable cases in the districts surrounding the Restaurants. Last year 354 cases were thus reported; all of them were visited, and 76 being found unable to pay were given free dinners. Committees have been organised to work in the poorer districts of the City on the lines of the well-known Huddersfield 

Health Union.

PROGRESS A HANDBOOK FOR HEALTH VISITORS. 

THE increased attention which is now being given to Health Visiting is creating a considerable body of literature on the subject. Many leaflets and handbooks have recently issued from the Press with a view to helping the amateur lady-visitor. 

One of these, "PRACTICAL HINTS TO HEALTH VISITORS," by Greta 

Allen, just published by the Scientific Press, fully justifies its name. 

It is distinctly practical, and deals in a simple and easy manner with the principal subjects the Health Visitor requires to know. Given first in Dublin as lectures, they are now published in book form especially for the use of workers among the poor. The subjects dealt with are the Infant, Infant Feeding, the School Life, the Dwelling, Food, and Beverages. Want of explicitness in one or two cases is the only serious fault we find in an otherwise useful book; e.g., we read (p. 22) "on the whole I should prefer them" (children of a year old!) "not having beer," and then follows a story which had been far better left out. Of course, the writer means that they must on no account have such things; but why not say so straight out?-jokes so subtly expressed are not always understood. Apart from these blemishes, and they are rare, the Voluntary Health Visitor will find here many helpful hints.

A CO-OPERATIVE CITY AT ABERDARON 

ED JU entral 

A NEW Healthy, Well-built Homes for Free Workers.

TRIUMPH FOR CO-OPERATION. 

GOOD SECURITY FOR ALL WHO WILL HELP. 

The Aberdaron Housing Society. 

HE ABERDARON HOUSING SOCIETY 

has acquired the valuable Estate of 

BODERMID-ISAF, consisting of 73 acres.

On this Estate it is erecting cottages for the workmen employed at the CO-OPERATIVE GRAN- ITE QUARRIES of Aberdaron. The work of erecting the cottages is already proceeding apace. The quarrymen are receiving wages far in excess of those hitherto paid in the district, and would gladly pay for good accommodation. But at present the housing conditions at 

Aberdaron are like those too often prevailing in rural districts intolerably inadequate. The Aberdaron :: 

Housing Society proposes to remedy this evil by supplying well-built houses for well-paid men. 

There is no doubt that the cottages can be let as soon as they are built.

When the land required for the workmen's cottages has been taken, there will remain many acres of the best agricultural land for small holdings and allotments, while the present Tenant is eager to take all that can be spared for his farm.

The estate affords ample security for the money needed for the project.

The Board of Directors includes MR. G. H. ROBERTS, Labour M.P. for Norwich, MR. C. SHERIDAN JONES, 

Managing Director, whose services in connection with the Penrhyn Quarrymen's Cause and with many Cooperative enterprises in North Wales are well known, Mr. W. W. CROTCH, author of "The Cottage Homes of Rural England," and MR. SIDNEY STRANKS of the 

Stonemasons' Union. will apply to T. Rhodes, Secretary, 59, Great Ormonde Street, W.C.

It is hoped that all who are willing to support so good a cause

Cheques should be made payable to the British Institute of Social Service.

OBJECTS OF THE INSTITUTE. 

To collect, register, and disseminate information relating to all forms o Social Service in order :- (1) To make such information available to all concerned in the improvement and elevation of our national life; (2) To promote the initiation and development in this country of the most beneficial and successful forms of Social Service; (3) To give assistance to all organisations that have social aims, and to facilitate cooperation between them; and, as far as possible, to prevent overlapping. 

SCOPE OF THE INSTITUTE. 

Some idea of the forms of Social Service at home and abroad regarding which the Institute collects and disseminates the best experience may be gathered from the following list:- Infant Mortality.-Prompt Registration of Births; Maternity Instruction; Milk 

Depôts, Crèches.

Child Life. Meals; Clothing; Country Holidays; Labour Restrictions; Juvenile Courts; 

Medical Inspection; Visiting Invalid Children. 

Education. Physical, Hygienic, Domestic; Trade Schools; Apprenticeship Com mittees; Special, Country Recovery, and Vacation Schools; Play Centres; Arts and Crafts; Libraries, Reading Circles.

Advertisements Labour Conditions. - Trade Unions; Dangerous Trades ; Safety Appliances; 

Women's Work; Sweating. 

Unemployment.-Labour Bureaux; Labour Colonies; Vacant Lot Cultivation; Emigration.

Industrial Betterment.-Sanitary Conditions; Factory Dressing Rooms; Dining Rooms, Reading and Recreation Rooms; Social Secretaries; Profit Sharing; Pension 

Systems; Thrift Societies; Sick Funds; Savings Banks.

Social Centres.-Settlements; Institutional Churches; Clubs and Brigades; Social 

Institutes. 

Temperance.-Attractions to compete with Public Houses; Disinterested Manage ment; Public House Trusts. 

Co-operation.—Credit Banks; Loan Societies; Benefit Societies; Building, Farming, Manufacturing, and Distributing; Housekeeping.

Municipal Enterprises.—Experiences in Public Ownership. 

Public Health.-Sanitation; Milk Inspection; Smoke Abatement; Health Visiting, &c Land.—Allotments; Small Holdings; Rural Depopulation; Village Handicrafts. 

Housing.-Town Planning; Overcrowding; Statistics; Experiences; etc.

Garden Cities.-Garden Villages and Suburbs; Urban Aesthetics; Provision of Parks; 

Window Gardening, etc. 

Poor Relief.-Vagrancy; Elberfeld System; Guilds of Help; Old Age Pensions; State

Insurance; Co-ordination of Voluntary Effort. 

Criminology.-Probationary Methods; Borstal System; Physical Development; 

Prison Experiments. 

Publication.-"PROGRESS: CIVIC, SOCIAL, INDUSTRIAL," the organ of the Institute, is published quarterly, price Sixpence. It is intended to appeal to all who are interested in social service and the betterment of life and labour conditions. "PROGRESS will thus stimulate its readers to carry their inquiries beyond the limited extent to which they can be dealt with in its pages, and also to contribute the results of their own social experiences; while it will also serve as the distributor of much of the information as to betterment movements and experiments gathered in the Library of the Institute from originating points in all civilised countries. 

Membership in the Institute may be obtained by sending a subscription of not less than one guinea per annum to the Secretary, but it is hoped that those interested in the Institute will remember that this is the minimum subscription and that those who can afford to do so will make contributions in much larger amounts. Membership in the Institute is of three kinds, classified as follows:- (a) LIFE MEMBERS, being the Founders of the Society, and all who shall at any time have contributed not less than £250 in one or more sums to the Institute. (b) SUBSCRIBING MEMBERS, who shall be those who shall subscribe not less than one guinea per annum to the Institute. (c) HONORARY MEMBERS, who shall be those persons who are co-opted by the 

Executive Committee.

The efficient working of the Institute can only be maintained either by a substantial endowment such as is enjoyed by the Musée Social of Paris, and the Humanitarian Society of Milan; or, as in America, where a sum of £3,000 a year has been guaranteed, by wealthy and philanthropic individuals or firms desiring to promote social effort.

It is estimated that at least £250 more than has been already promised will be required to enable the Institute to pay its way for 1908.

vi 

Advertisements Will be Published at an Early Date The L 

Mothers' Own Paper

Points Re"The 

"Mothers' Own

Paper." 

IT will actively work for the reduction of infantile mortality, by organising Branches where none exist and assisting all established Societies working for this object, by apportioning a part of the profits to the organisation of meetings, expenses of lecturers, &c., to further this greatest of national movements. 

No quack nostrums, female pills, or questionable preparations, whose advertisements degrade so many of the existing women's papers and render them a dangerous pestilential influence in a home, will be allowed to appear in the "The Mothers' Own Paper."

The price of the paper will be id. weekly, and it will deal with home management, foods and cookery, infants' foods and feeding, home dressmaking and millinery, paying hobbies, poultry-keeping, gardening, thrift, insurance, education for trades, etc., etc., just what the health visitor needs and can recommend with confidence as a guide to the young mother. 

At the present time the papers and books published for women's reading are mostly either trashy novelettes, or the advice given is absurd for mothers who have to keep a home and bring up children upon 16s. to 25s. or 30s. weekly. The schooling of such mothers stopped at the "Three R's," and articles upon carbohydrates, leguminous or farinaceous foods are quite meaningless to them. That there is a pressing need and a wide sphere of usefulness for a good woman's paper is obvious, and "The Mother's Own Paper" is intended to meet this great want. Its founders are practical journalists and experts who have expended years and a considerable amount of money in acquiring special knowledge of every article of food offered to the public, and in producing the paper and organising its sale, etc. 

A limited company is being formed to produce the paper, and some of the foremost scientists and social workers have taken shares in the undertaking, which should prove highly profitable, and, in addition, yield a handsome yearly sum to the Movement for Reducing Infantile Mortality.

The Shares are not being offered to the general investor, and prior allotments will be made to those directly interested in the movement before other applications will be entertained.
