PROGRESS Civic-Social-Industrial. 

OCTOBER, 1908. QUARTERLY. 

Suggestions for Poor Law Reform. 

I.-CLASSIFICATION. 

BY GENERAL BOOTH. 

It will be generally acknowledged that the methods of our present Poor Law have largely failed to restore to a state of manly efficiency the lowest grades of our social life. In order to remedy this many of the punitive arrangements for dealing with paupers, vagrants, drunkards, incorrigibles, and criminals should give place to more rational and reformatory methods, but in order that this may be effective there is need for a common-sense and more scientific classification. 

The principle of classification is admitted in the dividing of the sexes; the segregation of the sick, the feeble-minded, and the insane; and the separate treatment of the children, but I would insist upon a much wider extension of this principle, partly to secure greater efficiency and economy in the management of these classes, but still more for the restoration and reformation of those whose physical or moral conditions have compelled them to seek the aid of the State. 

It is only by this means that the qualities likely to help towards rehabiliment can be ascertained. 

After a somewhat long experience in dealing with these problems I have been led to certain definite convictions with

· 

PROGRESS 

regard to the lines to be followed. The classes to be dealt with fall under four main divisions, but each of these requires to be again sub-divided for distinct and separate treatment. 

The main divisions are these: I. The Incapacitated; II. 

The Confirmed Vagrant or Criminal; III. The Unemployed; 

IV. The Children. 

I. THE INCAPACITATED. Under this division I would include all who are incapable of doing remunerative work on account of (1) Old Age. It is to be expected that the Old Age Pensions now granted by the State will draw off the best type of the aged from seeking Poor Law relief, but there will probably always be left what may be termed, for want of a better name, an inferior residuum of the aged poor to be thus dealt with, but I would not on that account deprive them of the facilities for spending the remainder of their days in comfort and happiness. At the same time I am convinced that those who can do some work, however light, would be better and happier for doing it; idleness, even with the aged, is not conducive either to health or the enjoyment of life. For such some work suited to their capacity could and ought to be devised to a much larger extent than is done at present. (2) Physi al Unfitness, including the blind, the deaf and dumb, cripples, and all persons afflicted with such bodily ailments as render it impossible for them to earn a livelihood in the open market. But here again I would give them work suited to their condition, in order that they may reap the benefits which accrue from employment, however slight it may be. (3) Mental and Moral Infirmity, a class which includes the feeble-minded, epileptics, imbeciles, and insane. My experience convinces me that these classes should be segregated as completely as possible-some may need to be permanently segregated but none should be allowed to return to the outside world until it is clearly proved that not only have they been cured, but that there is reasonable ground for believing that they are capable of permanently earning a

living. Suitable work under healthy conditions—such as the lighter branches of agriculture-—must be found for them, and until they are well on their feet they should remain under the supervision of inspectors appointed for that purpose. Impoverishment and unhealthy environment have much to do with the creation of a large proportion of these classes; change those conditions and often it is only a question of time, patience, and skilful treatment for them to return to the normal physical and mental state. But their replacement in Society must be made conditional on their entering upon entirely different conditions from those which were so largely responsible for their affliction. 

Feeble-minded women should, I think, be treated separately and on the lines recommended by Mrs. Bramwell 

Booth in giving evidence before the Royal Commission on the Feeble-minded. Those lines were as follows:- (a) I would make it compulsory to register the feeble-minded. (6) Every feeble-minded woman registered and duly certified by medical men to be made a ward and placed under the care of some legal guardian, such guardian to be, by preference, a relative, where relatives are found to be suitable, but otherwise under the care of any person or society willing to accept the responsibility; and where no suitable person or society can be found to undertake this, I would make the judge of the county court, or some magistrate, the guardian, with power to place them out suitably. (c) The guardian to be invested by law with full power and control over the feebleminded, irrespective of her age, precisely as if she were a child of sixteen, and be made responsible for her safety and proper treatment. (4) Every feeble-minded woman to be placed under the direction of the laws now in force for the protection of girls under sixteen years of age. (e) The feeble-minded should not be removed into institutions from the care of guardians who can provide for them under the normal conditions of their class; but where such provision cannot properly be made, then I favour their being brought into institutions especially fitted for them, where they should be taught useful occupations. (f) I recommend that such institutions as are here outlined should be voluntary institutions, and while the financial arrangements should be subject to careful audit, and the homes open to proper inspection, the State should have nothing further to do with them. They should, in short, be as far as possible like homes, and as little as possible like workhouses, asylums, or prisons.

In dealing with these classes it will be observed that I attach great importance to the two principles of detention and labour. The first requires no argument for its support. 

Where the liberty of the person, aged or otherwise, becomes a menace or a nuisance to Society there must be resort to compulsion. Society must be protected, and the individual, in his own best interests, must be either segregated or placed

PROGRESS 

under restraint. On the second point-the organised employment of these classes-objections, I know, have been raised. On this matter I have had considerable experience. 

Thousands of destitute men and women are now engaged in various occupations, who, without such a system, would either have to be maintained in enforced idleness or would have to eke out an existence on the crumbs and doles of the charitable public. But their labour saves them from being pauperised or demoralised on the one hand, while on the other they produce a large number of articles which they and others of the State-aided classes require. (4) Sickness. I contend that the sick poor should be the first charge upon the nation, whether the sickness is brought on by their own ignorance or folly, or by poverty, contagion, or natural causes. There should be one Health Authority in the community directing and controlling the necessary duties; Health Visitors should be appointed, and sufficient infirmary or hospital provision should be made for all patients requiring treatment in such institutions. At present the outdoor medical relief afforded by the State is limited to the service of a Parish Doctor. Voluntary agencies have certainly sprung up in the form of free dispensaries and kindred charities which render valuable service, but they are not organised or co-ordinated so as to cope with the sickness in the parishes in which they are situated, and there is, I fear, some overlapping.

In the system which I advocate the Health Authority should take the initiative. It should not wait till the sick poor come to its doors. It should find them out and should give immediate aid without any circumlocution. It is better that mistakes should be made than that the sick, especially in serious cases, should suffer or spread disease.† District * It is urged that any surplus produce, if sold in the open market would be detrimental to trade. Not so, if, as in my workshops, there is no underselling. When this produce is sold at market price the only charge that can be made against the system is that a new competitor has entered the field. But surely it is as legitimate to use commerce to uplift and renovate mankind as it is to increase its material wealth. 

Power should be given to the Authority to recover the cost in certain cases from the parties responsible, but in all cases medical aid should be given without the stigma of pauperisation.

Surveyors, under the London Building Act, are empowered to enter on their own initiative any house or building in their district which they suspect to be unsafe; the Public 

Health Committees of the Councils have the power to act in a like summary manner with regard to persons or places suspected of harbouring infectious disease; I would apply the same principle to the appointment of doctors and nurses for a systematic visitation of the homes of the poor. In a word, I would organise a body of medical police for the detection and treatment of diseases. It should be their duty to render immediate relief to such sick persons as they may find and to report all cases which, in their judgment, should be removed to a public hospital. 

II. THE CONFIRMED VAGRANT OR CRIMINAL. Under this class I include all those who are well able to support themselves, but have become a nuisance, or expense, or danger to Society by idleness or misconduct. They may be classed as follows: (1) The Vagrant and Unemployable; (2) The Habitual Drunkard; (3) The Confirmed Criminal. 

These classes are the despair of every State official who has to deal with them. The vagrants show no sign of diminishing in number; the habitual drunkard is still with us; and the professional and confirmed criminal still preys on Society and constitutes a big percentage of our prison population. 

It is generally agreed that our present system, both in its punitive and reformatory character, has practically failed, and that the moment is ripe for introducing complete reform. 

Punishment alone is no remedy and the prison is not a 

Reformatory. 

It appears to me, therefore, that the present time is most opportune for reviving the consideration of the plan for establishing Compulsory Labour Colonies which I ventured to embody in the Bill which was introduced in the House of Commons in 1904 by Sir John Gorst, with the support of a number of members on both sides of the House, and received with wide approval so far as concerned its main principles. *These are set forth in my pamphlet (q.v.), "Vagrants: A New Proposal." 1904.

London: Headquarters of the Salvation Army, 122, Queen Victoria Street, E.C.

PROGRESS 

The economic aspect of such Colonies has occupied much of my time. During frequent visits to America, Canada, and other parts of the British Empire, as well as to the Continent of Europe, I have been able to discuss the question with experts and others who have had official and practical experience in the direction of Beggar and Labour Colonies. I may also claim to have acquired a unique acquaintance with every phase of the economical working of this class of Colony by experience gained in connection with the City, Land, and Industrial Colonies of the social work of the Salvation Army, and I am satisfied that if the State will provide the facilities for the establishment of such Colonies in this country the results will more than justify the experiment.* 

My proposal is therefore that Compulsory Labour Colonies should be established in different zones of the country in which all belonging to this confirmed vagrant or criminal class should be confined. They should be received into these 

Colonies under one or other of the following conditions: (a) Convicted Vagrants on a magistrate's order; (b) Voluntary entry, as in the case of Inebriates Act, 1879; 1899; (c) Prisoners who before the expiration of their sentence seem likely to benefit by such treatment; (d) Habitual criminals, drunkards, or incorrigibles, who at the discretion of a judge or magistrate may be sentenced to a period of imprisonment to be followed by a period of committal to the Colony. 

The aim of these Colonies would be the reclamation of as large a percentage of this class as possible and their restoration to the ordinary occupations and privileges of Society. 

The means I would adopt are, in the main, as follows:- (1) Detention for such a period as the necessity of each individual case suggests. (2) I would not abrogate the penal principle in the discipline of such a colony. Provision should be made for compelling the idle to work and the drunkard to undergo the treatment The Witzwyl Colony may be taken as an example of successful economic working with forced labour. (See Report presented to the Right Hon. Walter Long, M.P., by 

H. Preston Thomas, Esq., L.G.B.; see also Sir Walter Besant, "The Farm and the City," on his visit to the Hadleigh Colony.)

prescribed for him.

Insubordination should also be subject to discipline, but I would not rely upon it. Hence, my next method would be (3) Kindness.

Colonists should not be treated as convicts, but as men desiring and deserving to be reformed in their disposition and habits. (4) Instruction. I would set before the inmates of a Compulsory Labour Colony the physical, moral, and material benefits arising from honest, industrious, and sober living. Many of this class are what they are to-day by reason of superficial and false education. I would rectify that, not merely by instruction in morals, but still more by (5) Actual formation of habits favourable to such living.

They should be taken in hand as incorrigible children and compelled to eat, drink, labour, walk, talk, and act on very much the same principles as one would train children. 

III.—THE UNEMPLOYED. By these I mean those who are able and willing to work, but who are unable to procure remunerative employment. The causes of this are manifold, and I frankly confess that this social problem is so complex that it baffles the wit of man to find an all-round and complete remedy, but I am disposed to believe that by some such plans as I here outline the degree of distress caused by unemployment can be considerably lessened. (1) There should be Compulsory Registration both on the part of those who are seeking workers and those who are seeking work. Employers should be compelled to make a weekly return to the municipal bureau of the number of their present employees and of the number of additional workers, if any, needed immediately. They should also state whether they require the aid of the Bureau to fill these vacancies. Able-bodied men, professional or industrial, who are out of work for more than a week should be compelled to register their age, occupation, and where last employed; also whether willing to travel to where their labour is in demand if facilities are granted. This should be done personally on a form obtainable at any Post Office or Labour Bureau.

PROGRESS 

(2) All local Bureaus should be affiliated, as in Germany, with a National Labour Exchange, so that local authorities may be kept constantly informed as to the conditions and requirements of the labour market in all parts. At present, in the absence of such a National Labour Exchange, it is almost impossible to ascertain in any given week or month the number of skilled and unskilled persons out of work, or the extent to which labour is in demand, while for lack of compulsory registration we simply muddle through both normal and abnormal periods of distress. But by some such scheme as this it would be possible to bring the requirements of capital and labour much closer together. 

But when all this has been done there will still remain, I fear, a serious amount of unemployment. No doubt here and there will be found those of the idle, loafing class who prefer to live on the relief provided by the Guardians or the doles of the charitable rather than earn their own bread by the sweat of their brows. But the brand deserved by the few ought not to be indiscriminately stamped on the many who remain unemployed only on account of lack of employment. The problem is to provide for this unfortunate surplus, a surplus which will be found mainly among the unskilled, for the skilled workman-except in the case of prolonged strikes—can always rely on the support of his 

Trade Union. 

Should extended powers be conferred on municipal. authorities to enable them to make temporary provision in the shape of work? It seems to me that if we are to grapple with this problem in dead earnest we must ultimately look to statutory authority for setting in motion the requisite machinery. I believe that an opportunity to work for his own maintenance should be afforded every man, and that if he fails to find that work by his own energy it is the duty of the community to step in and provide it for him. I would submit the following as the principles which should guide any such action:- (1) That any work thus provided must be of a character likely to prove remunerative to the community.

(2) That the rate of remuneration should be below that ordinarily paid for the same labour under other conditions. 

The responsibility for fixing this rate must rest with the Local Authorities, they alone being competent to decide the economic conditions of the areas within their own jurisdiction. (3) That the rate thus fixed should be paid to the workers according to the value of the work done, and the value only. 

Where, however, this value is inadequate to the needs of the worker, and it is thought expedient to pay more, this surplus should be debited to the worker either as a grant in aid or as a loan; if the latter, it should be recoverable by the Local Authority within twelve months, provided the worker is in a position to repay. (4) That the selection of the kind of work-whether it be the manufacture of goods, or the planting of trees, or the creation of parks, etc.-be left entirely to the Local 

Authority. If, however, goods are manufactured, either by machinery or by hand labour, they must not be put upon the market at lower than trade prices. In other words, there must be no underselling.

I believe that a wider application of these measures will set in motion a vast body of unskilled labour, which, after all, contributes two-thirds to the unemployed problem. But even then, I sorrowfully admit, there will still be a surplus. of people to be provided for in other ways. 

To what extent and in what way can this be met by 

EMIGRATION? By Emigration I do not mean the imperfect and unsystematic thing which in the past has led to much disappointment and no little misery. No one can condemn more strongly than I do the indiscriminate transfer of people to another country where, on landing, they are left in their ignorance to shift for themselves, thereby becoming too often a sure prey to the land-sharks and ultimately a source of perplexity to the new community, whether they be the vicious, the criminal, or the wastrel. Again, the recent experience of some public authorities has shown the unwisdom of sending out large parties of one class together, and then practically leaving them without any after-care in the way

PROGRESS 

of advice or temporary assistance to meet the contingencies which so often arise when the emigrant is settling down to the new conditions which confront him. The value of this after-care has been fully proved by our experience in emigration in connection with the work of the Salvation Army. 

This need we have been able to meet by our unique and world-wide organisation, with the result that, though during the last three years we have selected and transferred over 30,000 persons from the British Isles, the number of disappointments is not more than one per cent. 

But the actual surplus population of the Old Countrywilling to emigrate-is greater than the ability of our Colonies to absorb in the ordinary way. It is, therefore, necessary to supplement Emigration by COLONISATION. 

By this I 

mean the settlement of suitable people in communities on areas of land selected for the purpose, thus enabling the settlers to cultivate their holdings and support themselves and their families. This would involve the erection of buildings, provision of necessary capital, direction, and efficient oversight. It might be carried out either at home or abroad, but the chances of success all point to settlements beyond the British Isles, but within the British Empire. It is the neglect of our opportunities of colonising which is, I believe, one of the most fruitful sources of the social evils by which. we are surrounded. I regard this remedy of so great importance that I would make the following recommendations:- (1) The creation of a Central Board to consider plans for the relief of congested districts in Great Britain by the transfer of the surplus population to the unoccupied lands of the British Empire at home and abroad. (2) That such Board be empowered to assist by way of grant or loan (with or without interest) any scheme submitted to them by any Colonial Government, Local Authority, Corporation, Company, or responsible person or persons, and which they may approve. (3) That a sum of Ten Million Pounds sterling (£10,000,000) be placed at the disposal of the Central Board for the purpose of thus assisting in the better permanent distribution of the people of Great Britain.

IV. THE CHILDREN. 

pleasure in recent years than to note the steady progress which public opinion-and the authorities concerned-have made in the direction of a more humane and careful treatment of the children who, for some reason or other, have been compelled to come under the sheltering arm of the State. I need only cite the special schools now provided, in most districts, for the physically and mentally defective. 

Nothing has given me greater

There still remains much to be done in order that all these children may be treated without any odious brand, and under such conditions that there may be a reasonable assurance that they will pass out into the world without any fear that they will again become a charge upon the community. The principle of adoption should be more freely used, and it should also be made possible for the responsible Local Authority to arrange for the emigration and maintenance of suitable young children in our Colonies. The expenditure would be much less than the cost at home, and the children growing up under the simpler conditions of life that prevail abroad would greatly benefit.

It will be observed that in outlining the above recommendations I have left untouched the whole question of the administrative Authority. This omission is deliberate and prompted by two considerations. One is the fact that several influential Corporations are urging a transfer of the powers now vested in the Guardians to County and Borough Councils, while the President of the Local Government Board is generally credited with the intention of presenting to Parliament a measure which will probably involve some rearrangement of the duties belonging to various local bodies. 

I do not profess to be an expert in civic administration, and therefore leave these details to those who are. 

But, finally, I would not disguise the fact that I attach far more importance to the reform of the man than to the reform of the law. The problem of problems lies in a nutshell; the great mass of human misery among all classes has its chief source in the individual, as distinct from his circumstances, antecedents, or environment; and unless,

PROGRESS 

among other means adopted, efforts are made to bring about the social reclamation and moral regeneration of the individual, then, frankly, I despair of accomplishing any radical improvement in the condition of these classes. 

II. 

ADMINISTRATION. 

By J. B. PATON, D.D. 

In the suggestions which I am about to propose with regard to certain modifications in our present methods of Poor relief, I am concerned mainly with our cities, because it is with the city and its problems that I am most intimately acquainted; but I am told by those who are acquainted with the needs of the country districts that the same principles and methods can be easily applied there also. 

Throughout all the past, cities have been the centres of civilisation, hence the problem which confronts us. How can these cities become the homes of a healthy, happy, industrious and noble people? We look around us now and see that the problem of city life menaces not only the civilisation to which they have given birth, but the very existence of the multitudes that inhabit them. 

It is mere ostrich-like cowardice and blindness that will not face the truth and see the swift deterioration that falls upon masses of the people in the insanitary homes and the sordid squalor of the slums of our great cities. There may be a fuller and freer life in our cities; but for vast numbers it is life which burns with a fierce fever-heat, and we witness all around the wastage and wreckage it entails. Macaulay had a prophet's vision when he warned us that, if no great reforms are made, the Huns and Vandals who will destroy the Christian States of Europe are being bred, not in the wilds of Asia, but in the slums of our great cities.

This problem, then, of our city life must be solved, or our national well-being, if not our existence, is at stake; and it only can be solved by the awakening of a new spirit—the spirit of "collectivism" as it has been called-which, touched with the fine enthusiasm of humanity and a sense of the brotherhood that links man to man, can be organised and applied with wise, civic statesmanship to comprehend the intertwining elements of that problem. A municipal conscience must be roused that shall impress and possess every member of the City's Commonwealth. Citizens of every grade and of every class must unite to support the civic authorities whom they have chosen for the government of the city, and take part with them in the furtherance of its well-being. Happily there are signs of an awakening at the present time. There is a growing movement for civic collective action-a growing sense of communal responsibility for the well-being of the borough or district. 

In Germany, more than in any other country, this new spirit has possessed the whole community, and civic patriotism has become the finest moral power. Not only in the housing of the people and the planning of their cities so as to secure the health of the citizens and the beauty of the city, as Mr. Horsfall has shown us, is this patriotism. seen, but also, and quite as effectively in their efforts to deal with the stupendous problems of Poverty. It is here that the German cities have done their best and their grandest work. The Elberfeld system-now so widely known-has spread throughout the Empire, and commands the approval of the wisest men, and the enthusiasm of the people. In this system, citizens of every rank take upon themselves the charge and care of the poor, and their duty is fulfilled. with such completeness of organisation and such fidelity of service, that there is no poor man, woman, or child who does not receive with discriminating sympathy the succour that is needed. I cannot but hope that the promised revision of our poor-law system will ensure in some effective way the adoption of that humane and, I may say, perfect system of poor-law relief.

PROGRESS 

After much thought, and conference with those who have studied the working of the Elberfeld system on the spot, I am convinced that by some such modified scheme as I now suggest, it may be applied to England without any violent change in our present methods. My proposals are as 

(1) That the duty of assessing the rating values of property and of making contracts for expenditure connected. with the relief and help of the poor, now entrusted to the Guardians, be entrusted in future to the Municipal Council which controls the financial interests of the community. 

The importance of this will be understood when it is remembered that the Guardians are elected not merely to relieve the destitute, but for the special duties of guarding the poor from destitution and assisting them by all possible means to maintain their personal independence. This service requires special qualifications, and they should be chosen only for their fitness for it. They should not be tempted to seek the office from any other considerations; their office should be magnified as one that gives no place or opportunity for any business but the diligent care of the poor; and an office thus magnified would attract the best men and women of the community because of its freedom from business transactions of an alien nature. (2) That the town or city should be divided into a sufficient number of districts to secure the same effective individual care of the poor which obtains in Elberfeld and other German towns. Guardians should be elected, as at present, to act as Chairmen of these districts, but their number should be increased so as to correspond with the number of districts required. (3) That 12 Associate Guardians be appointed in each district to act with the Chairman; each district should be sub-divided into 12 sub-districts, and an Associate Guardian placed over each and made responsible for the care of its poor. The appointment of the Associate Guardians should rest with the Municipal Council of the borough, which shall select from persons nominated for that office by the

Churches, the Charitable Societies, the Chamber of Commerce, the Trade Unions, and other united and responsible bodies, without distinction of church, class, or party. (4) The Chairman, with his 12 Associates, should meet fortnightly. Their duties should be similar to those of the 

German Armenpfleger, and they should not only relieve the actually destitute by the giving of alms, but in all possible ways, should prevent prevent the needy from becoming destitute by encouraging them to recover their independence. This should be done (a) By enlisting the support of relatives and friends and the help of neighbours; (b) By friendly sympathy and counsel; (c) By endeavouring to find work. for the unemployed; (d) By seeking in every way to awaken hope and energy and to strengthen character in those whom they would help. This, of course, should be done under careful supervision, and with all the safeguards which have made the Elberfeld system so successful, and which, whilst undertaking this larger responsibility and care of the poor, has greatly decreased the amount spent in such Poor Relief.* 

(5) There should be a Central Board composed of Guardians and members of the Municipal Council in equal numbers. The Central Board shall register and preserve in its offices the records of all cases dealt with in each district or division, and the decisions of each district and divisional Committee shall be revised and approved by it. 

Further, this Board, after receiving reports from the Guardians, shall prepare the annual budget of Poor Relief, and shall appoint from the Guardians and from members of the Council, Sub-committees for the management of institutions. such as the Poor House; the Infirmary for the infirm and sick; Elementary Schools for children; and Training 

Industrial Colonies, if they should be established to provide training for the unemployable, and to find work for the unemployed.

The budget so prepared by this Central * Mainz may be quoted as an example. Before adopting the Elberfeld system the city spent £6,500 annually on the relief of its poor, and the average amount given to each case was only £3 138. od.; after adoption its total expenditure fell to £4,400, while the amount given to each case rose to £9 11s. od. [ED.]

PROGRESS 

Board to be presented to, and adopted by, the Municipal Council.

Two objections to this scheme may possibly arise in some minds. First it may be said that it will not be possible to find a sufficient number of Associate Guardians to administer it properly, and, secondly, that if found they would not easily be trained, and if untrained they would be worse than useless.

With regard to the first objection I would say that it is hard to believe that England-always famous for voluntary service rendered to the State and the Municipality-has not as much public spirit as is shown in Germany. In Elberfeld it is deemed an honour to be a helper. There are often more candidates for the office than places to be filled. “If you want to know" (remarked the President of the Administrative Council to some English visitors) "how this service is regarded in Elberfeld, you have only to ask a citizen if he is a Visitor of the Poor (Armenpfleger)—he will answer either that he is, or has been, or will blush with the consciousness that he has not had the honour conferred upon him." The voluntary service readily obtained by the 

Civic Guilds of Help already established in this countrynow numbering 35-should be a sufficient assurance that when the time comes we shall not be behindhand, and that service would be obtained even more readily than by our Guilds of Help when those who render it are recognised by the Civic Community. For this reason I have proposed that they be appointed by the Borough Council, and thus obtain an honourable civil status ; and I would further suggest that, as in Elberfeld, those who serve the poor in this way for a certain number of years should have their names inscribed on a permanent Roll of Honour exhibited in the Town Hall. 

With regard to the second objection, which concerns the training, it should be remembered that not only will the most careful detailed instructions be given to each helper as to how to deal with different types of cases, but trained officials will be appointed to advise in cases of special difficulty. 

Then again, each case will be discussed fortnightly at a

meeting of the District Committee and the Visitor will simply carry out its decisions. Occasionally Conferences should be held of all the Associate Guardians with the Central Administrative Council, at which the principles and methods of Poor Relief and the care of the poor should be discussed, explained, and enforced, so that there may be a thorough understanding of the work in hand. There is no reason why our voluntary Helpers should not be as amenable to training as those in Germany; indeed, the experience of those at work in our Guilds of Help proves that they are. "Given a good organisation and trained workers to guide and advise all District Committees" (writes one of the leaders of this movement in Bradford) "and it is remarkable how few of those who render this voluntary service will prove unsuitable."

There are certain principles which underlie the Elberfeld system, and which are preserved in the modified scheme I suggest for our country, which seem not only to commend it, but to make it, or something like it, absolutely necessary in our time. (1) The conditions of labour in our country make it expedient, and even necessary, that a community should care not only for those who are absolutely destitute, but also for those who may be saved from the degradation of pauperism. The fluctuations of trade affect great multitudes of our industrial classes, so that without fault of their own they are suddenly thrown out of employment. During such a period of unemployment even the thrifty find themselves reduced to deplorable want. When we remember that such a man as Will Crooks went through this terrible experience for many months, it shows that there is a class of people in every community whose poverty makes an irresistible appeal for temporary help, and who indeed deserve and need it. The very fact that in our present industrial system we seem to require a certain number of unemployed, ready for any new industrial enterprise, shows that unemployment is not now, as it has been under former conditions, an ignominious state into which men allow

PROGRESS 

themselves to fall. This condition of things is giving rise to social evils which degrade the character of numbers of our people. It is hard to refuse those who beg for alms, when we know there are many who deserve and would be helped by our alms; but this willingness to give alms has produced a predatory class who live on charity, and charitable institutions abound to aid them. But at present the ministries of these charities overlap, and people are tempted to live by sedulously cultivating them. All this growing evil of our time would be obviated if every needy case in every district of the town were cared for in a humane and reasonable manner. (2) The development of our industries has led to a great separation between employers and employed, and also to a segregation of the wealthier and the poorer classes in separate and distant districts of our towns, so that they see little of one another, and have no friendly intercourse. This fact makes it necessary that means should be found, such as the Elberfeld System provides, for personal acquaintance and intercourse between the more educated and wealthy classes of the community and the poorer classes. It is not simply a sense of alienation, but a sense of antagonism and hostility that is growing up between these diverse classes, and each suffers because of its separation from, and its ignorance of, the other. There can be no doubt that many of our poorer families would hold themselves in higher esteem, and would be awakened to greater effort after a higher and more honourable mode of life if they were visited, and had the friendship of, men and women of higher social rank than themselves: and the men in higher social position would be better fitted for the duties of citizenship, and take greater interest in the concerns of their town or city, if they had personal acquaintance with the needs and difficulties of the poor. (3) All who have studied the problems of poverty recognise that it is only by individual and personal care that the poor can be wisely dealt with, that appropriate and needful sympathy can be given to the sick and aged, or that strong

stimulus and patient effort can be applied to restore the idle and listless. This is made possible in the Elberfeld System by its minute subdivision of labour, whereby each visitor, having charge of only one small district, is able to become acquainted with the condition of all its poor. This would be equally the case in the scheme I suggest, and the Associate Guardians would be able to give the necessary individual and personal care to each case.

In making these suggestions I am but seeking to revive the soul of our Saxon freedom which underlay and inspired our British Constitution. The "tithing" and the "hundred” of our first communities were formed in order that the persons in them might give a frank pledge for the good conduct and the welfare of one another. The old English word "borough" meant that all who lived in it should give the same warrant to the King and to the State. In asking this voluntary service in the care of the poor I am only asking that in larger and nobler ways our citizens may do what once was done in early days and in our country, and what is being done abroad. Everywhere this movement of civic, collective action for the welfare of the city is growing apace, and becoming one of the illustrious features of our time.

Fifty Years of Child Legislation. 

BY ROSA M. BARRETT. 

IT is well sometimes to tilt lances at our national shortcomings, but it is also well, by a review of the past, to find out, now and again, whether as a nation we are, on the whole, moving in the right or in the wrong direction. Where do we stand now as compared with fifty years ago? With all that there is to discourage, with the signs of misery, of poverty, of depravity so evident, is there anything at all to encourage? 

Is the future of a child-a citizen in posse-born to-day more hopeful than it was then? When we remember that less than fifty years ago an infant had next to no rights in the eyes of the law; that less than half fifty years ago (only twenty-one in fact) the offence of cruelty to, or neglect of, a child was unknown to English law, we may expect some striking change. What, as a matter of fact, has been done?

Perhaps the first Act that recognised the right of an infant to legal protection was the Infant Life Protection Act, passed in 1872 to protect boarded-out children, when more than one child under a year old (now raised to 5 years) is taken for hire. Why a single child should not equally need and receive protection is somewhat puzzling! The latest Act affecting infants-unfortunately only permissive and not compulsory-is the Notification of Births Act, passed in 1907. Where this Act is adopted by the local authorities, notification of birth must be sent within thirtysix hours after birth to the Medical Officer of Health. Its special value lies in the fact that this notice enables a Health Visitor, or other qualified person, to visit the infant's home and, where needed, to give instruction and aid to the often well-meaning but very ignorant mother. Simple as this sounds, it has proved one of the most successful of schemes for the safeguarding of infant life. 

The appalling infant mortality which exists-and in many districts even increases-in spite of modern knowledge, is now attracting widespread attention; but wider knowledge and deeper sorrow are needed to stimulate further efforts to

CHILD LEGISLATION 

263 check and remove this great reproach. In England, in some districts, infant mortality exceeds 200 per 1,000, although it certainly need not be even 100; it is less than 100 in 

Norway and Ireland, where the conditions of life are at least as hard and the people as poor as in England. The average annual rate of infantile mortality in England was, for the ten years from 1895 to 1904, 150 per 1,000—but in 1905 it fell to 

128, the lowest on record. Apathy and ignorance are no longer universal; we are awakening to our shortcomings. Legislation will provide further safeguards when Mr. Samuel's 

Bill becomes law, not only by an extension of the Infant Life 

Protection Act, but by making injury by burning—when proper precautions have not been taken-or other acts of neglect, punishable offences. Private efforts also are being made in many directions to safeguard the frail little lives, so carelessly, so ignorantly, and so thoughtlessly brought into this world without any choice on their part; "I did not ask you to born me" said one little mite to its angry mother. Among various private efforts may be mentioned the very few Homes for infants (motherless or otherwise), which ought to be far more numerous,* and the crèche. † But these are only palliatives, and what is most needed is that the mothers themselves should be enabled to take proper care of their own infants and children. Some day perhaps we shall recognise this as so important that no one will dream of employing or allowing to be employed, outside her own home, the mother of young children, while training in these duties will be considered a necessary part of a girl's curriculum. Girls, rich and poor alike, are still carefully taught much of everything except the most essential thing of all-how to live and how to help others to live. * I would also mention what is being done in Huddersfield under the inspiration of Mr. Broadbent, and in the Schools for Mothers in St. Pancras and Dumfermline, of which accounts have already been given in "PROGRESS." There are also similar movements in Birmingham, Belfast, and elsewhere.

The danger of the crèche in factory towns is that it tends to encourage the employment of married women and to allay any misgivings employers may have on this It is illegal to employ a woman in a factory or workshop within four weeks of childbirth, though the Act is often evaded. This period needs to be much extended. matter.

PROGRESS 

It seems almost incredible now, that less than fifty years ago a parent was under no legal obligation even to feed his child, to keep it clean or give it proper care or treatment, or to send it to school; there was no power to remove a child from its parent however ill-treated (unless there was property and the child could be made a ward in Chancery), nor had anyone the right to enter a private house, even though screams unceasing might be heard coming from it. Even twenty years ago to ill-treat or neglect a child was not a punishable offence. Parents may still be found who declare that they have a right to do what they like with their own. Happily, the law no longer agrees with them, if doing what they like means causing unnecessary suffering. Just as 

Lord Shaftesbury was the good genius of the oppressed in the earlier part of last century, so was

Benjamin Waugh in the later. By his exertions the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Children was formed in 1884, and in 1889 as the result of the experience gained through this Society, he secured the passing of a farreaching law for the protection of children—a law amplified in 1894, and to be further extended by Mr. Samuel's excellent Bill. So for the first time a special national Society was formed and special Statutes passed against cruelty to children.* The latter, however, would have been useless without the former, for laws, however good, cannot enforce

CHILD FOUND AND RESCUED BY THE N.S.P.C.C. 

*It is true that an Act was passed in 1868 making it a punishable offence to neglect to provide food, etc., to the danger of a child's life, or to abandon a child, but since it was no one's duty to see the law was carried out, it was almost a dead letter.

CHILD LEGISLATION 

265 of themselves, nor can tiny children, absolutely at the mercy those about them, set them in motion. When these laws were passed, the cry was almost universal that they were wholly unnecessary, yet in one year (1906) no less than 115,000 children were found to be suffering from cruel neglect. Probably even this does not represent more than one-third of the actual sufferers, for the majority must remain undiscovered until the Society is able to cover the whole country. It is almost incredible that this Society has been able to do so much during the comparatively few years that have elapsed since it was formed-especially when it is remembered that it is wholly voluntary and receives no State aid whatever-for although the cases dealt with are still sadly increasing as 

its work spreads, yet the worst forms of cruelty such as brutality and violence - are steadily decreasing. Instead of forming, as they did for five years after the Society was started, nearly half (46 per cent.) of the total cases dealt with, they were only 8 per cent. during the year ending 

March, 1907; every year fewer children need to be removed from their parents; and every year the prosecutions become fewer. At first, 16 per cent. of the cases in which the Society intervened had to be taken into court, now it is only 

5'5 per cent.; a warning, a visit from the inspector, or some other means, has sufficed to effect the end in view-the better care of the child. That

the wilful neglect or causing a child unnecessary suffering is a

PROGRESS 

punishable offence is now becoming recognised as a stern fact; perhaps the reason why it was not made so till 1889 was that to most people the idea of a parent being wilfully cruel to his own child seemed incredible. 

In 1886 a law was passed which gave every mother some legal control over her own child. Hitherto this right had only belonged to the mother of an illegitimate child, while a married-and presumably therefore a better-woman, had none. Now the Court, in cases brought before them, must consider the wishes of the mother as well as of the father. 

This seems so obvious a right that one can hardly imagine. how it was ever disputed.

Laws enabling Poor Law Guardians to remove pauper children from the workhouse stigma aud its degrading surroundings, by boarding them out or placing them in separate buildings or in Scattered Homes; and laws for the inspection and protection of these children, are all comparatively recent, and are proofs of an increasing consideration. for those least able to protect themselves. For children employed at home no protection has yet been devised, such as has happily been accomplished for those employed in factories, etc. Though one might hope for their safety and happiness at home, yet so great is the pressure of poverty (even where the parent is not cruel or drunken) that mere babies, only two or three years old, may often be found toiling at some sweated industry, while children only a little older-either evading school or working before and after school hours--will be found at work at six a.m., and again till late at night, for the sake of the few extra pennies that can thus be earned. Can we wonder that they try to get these extra pennies when we learn that the average earnings of women-old hands too-working from forty to sixty. hours a week at carding hooks and eyes, only amount to 

3s. 3d. a week? These home workers, who are nearly all women and children, are too poor and too busy to drink; but they are wretchedly housed, and the poorer they are the less can they resist the greed of employer or landlord. While the licensing of such workrooms might abolish the worst of

CHILD LEGISLATION 

267 them and oblige the landlord to keep his premises in decent order, there seems little hope of improving the condition of the children, or of stopping their work until, by a Minimum Wage Bill or by an improved moral tone among employers, the parents are better paid. When Is. or Is. 3d. a dozen is paid for making shirts, who can blame the unhappy woman who eagerly utilises her small children to save her precious moments? We tell our little ones, in accents of horror, about Herod's cruelty and his massacre of the infants; would that our preachers would sometimes tell of the far

CARDBOARD-BOX MAKING. 

more cruel and long-drawn-out massacre of infants that is going on to-day in our own country! Are we much better than Herod in our own indifference and apathy? 

More progress has been made with regard to the employment of children away from home. Within the memory of many, children, even tiny dots only five years old, were literally sold by their parents or guardians (even by the Poor Law Guardians), and bought by employers to work for ten, twelve, and even sixteen hours in factories, in mines, in fields, and even in ships. The first effective Factory Act

PROGRESS All was passed in 1844,* and inspectors were appointed to see that its provisions were carried out. The employment of children under eight years of age (!) was forbidden and other restrictions imposed. We have travelled a long way since then, but this Act first established the principle that a child is entitled to the protection of the law, and that, if necessary for the welfare of the child, the State has a right to intervene and protect it from parent or employer. At present, no child under twelve years of age may be employed in or about mines, factories, or workshops; boys under thirteen may not be employed underground, girls and women not at all, while in certain employments certificates of physical fitness are needed for those under sixteen. "children" (ie., those under 14), all "young persons (those from 14 to 18), and all females, are more or less. protected by the various Factory and Workshop Acts. The maximum hours of consecutive work have been fixed for all "protected" persons employed either in textile or nontextile factories; no overtime is allowed in the former, and fixed times for meals must be given; night work and 

Sunday work is forbidden for women and for all under 18 (with certain exceptions for boys, and for creameries, and certain Jewish workshops), and there must be six holidays a year. Inspectors have the right to enter and examine factories, workshops, etc., to enforce these laws, and to summon offenders.

In shops the hours of work are limited (!) to seventy-four a week for all under eighteen, and seats must now be provided for female assistants, though (as a rule) their use is strongly discouraged. These two are almost the only restrictions at present imposed on the employers of shop assistants, except that where a shop and workshop are owned by one person, the hours of joint employment may not exceed those permitted under the Factory Act to young persons and women. This is absolutely the only protection as to hours of work as yet afforded to women in shops, and * It is true that in 1802 and 1819 measures were introduced, but they only applied to certain mills (chiefly cotton mills), in which children's work was restricted to twelve hours a day.

In 1836, this was reduced to nine hours a day for children under thirteen. 

Inspectors were appointed to enforce it, but it remained almost a dead letter.

CHILD LEGISLATION 

269 while it is chiefly in the poorer class of shops that the hours are so excessive and that the sales women are also employed in the workroom, yet larger shops, with less excuse, often impose on their workers and assistants an unhealthy and arduous life. 

There are no longer any "climbing" or chimney boysindeed, young girls were sometimes employed in this dangerous and degrading task. Thanks to the efforts of

Lord Shaftesbury they were finally abolished by law in 

1875. Previously children (the smaller the better) were often sent up chimneys naked-sometimes forced by the lighting of fires underneath to go higher and higher. It was actually found necessary to prohibit by law the sending of children up chimneys to put out fires. From contact with the soot these children were liable to a special and loathsome disease. I hardly think such treatment would be possible to-day, though greed seems to make a man capable of almost anything. Another Act passed in 1871, also due to Lord Shaftesbury, restricted-and has now led to the abolition of the employment of children in brickfields. Formerly thousands, both of boys and girls, almost as soon as they could walk, were employed working in the cold, wet clay for fourteen or more weary hours each day. Agricultural gangs, which formerly employed young children under cruel conditions, are now happily unknown, though up to 1867 even children under eight were so employed without restriction. 

Girls and women, and boys under 13, may no longer be employed underground.

Legislation has now also intervened to protect children. from being public performers, and partial protection is afforded to those under sixteen from being trained as acrobats, circus performers, etc. This is often a painfully cruel process, and is necessarily begun at a very early age, and they can still, unfortunately, be so trained by their parents or step-parents. The same Act prohibits the performance in public by any child under eleven years of age, except by special licence and under stringent conditions. It was at the same time made a punishable offence to cause,

PROGRESS 

procure, or allow a boy under fourteen or a girl under sixteen to beg, play, perform, or sell in the streets or publichouses. Not so very long ago child-beggars used to abound, whilst deformities and a starved, ill-clad appearance were paying investments. Some attempt, but a quite insufficient one, has been made to legislate for children living on canal boats, and also to lessen the evils of children frequenting public-houses.

drink for his own consumption, and if employed to fetch drink when under fourteen he can only be supplied in closed vessels. There is happily a growing demand for the entire exclusion of young children from licensed houses.

Poor Law Guardians have power, since 

1889, to retain custody over orphans, deserted children, or those with bad parents, until they are sixteen years of age, or if girls, eighteen. Children have now, therefore, some-though still too little-protection from the greed or cruelty of parents or employers. There is as yet, however, no legislation to protect domestic slaveys from excessive labour-at least after they are fourteen years old. It is rather curious how few Acts there are

CHILD LEGISLATION 

271 for the protection of any child over the age of fourteen; hence, many small workers (apparently only nine or ten) are always "past fourteen." Up to that age the Compulsory

Education Act enforces attendance at school. Half-timers under twelve are now practically abolished, since all children under that age must now attend school for the full time. 

Probably no Act has been so fruitful, so far-reaching in its effects, as the comparatively recent Compulsory Education Act (1870) and its subsequent amendments. Children must attend school—or be otherwise educated and not only are the schools now free, but by the most recent Acts of all (one of which came into effect in January, 1908) a special rate may be levied for the feeding of hungry school children, and, with the object of checking the threatened degeneracy of the race, there are to be regular medical inspections of school children under the charge of a central medical department. We shall all agree that children cannot learn or be healthy without suitable food, but the danger of still further lessening parental obligations will need to be carefully guarded against. It is a great reproach to our own countrywomen that the children of other nationalities equally poor and living in just the same evil conditions-are more healthy, better cared for, and better treated than many of our own; for it is a fact that aliens (whether Jews or others) do not as a rule drink, or ill-treat their children. 

These privileges (?) belong to English mothers.

The various Acts distinguishing juvenile from adult offenders, the Act for the rescue of young children and girls from immoral houses, have all come into being within a far shorter time than fifty years. These Acts will, under Mr. 

Samuel's Bill, be still further extended. It may be possible to give a fuller account of these at some future time.

Chips from Social Workshops. 

OUR OWN WORKSHOP. 

FROM the summary of inquiries given below it will be seen that we passed the 1,000 mark in the first nine months of this year; last year we only reached 980 in twelve months, and the year before only 472. This rapid extension of our work is most gratifying; it not only implies a large extension of our usefulness, but indicates a growing appreciation of it on the part of the public. 

The following list of a few of the most interesting inquiries received during that period will give some slight idea of the amount of research involved in answering a thousand. They have come to us from all parts of the world, and have related to:-The employment and education of children and remedies for abolishing child labour; Froebel Societies in the United Kingdom and abroad; the 

Provision of Meals Act; the number and cost of maintenance of convicts; prison conditions; systems of punishment, etc., at home and abroad; lectures on Civics, Eugenics, and Housing; the number of profit-sharing firms in the United Kingdom and elsewhere; treatment of employees with regard to social and physical well-being in factories; protection of mothers before and after child-birth; maternity instruction; programmes for social study by clubs, settlements, etc., and for foreigners visiting London; duties and powers of Trade Union 

Secretaries; labour disputes and rates of wages in various trades; land nationalisation, afforestation, etc.; provision by Municipalities of cemeteries, public parks, and open spaces; purity of the milk supply; homes and sanatoria for cases of epilepsy, consumption, etc.; advice as to the formation of Guilds of Help in various towns; statistics on the Elberfeld System; labour conditions of married women as wage earners; sweating and proposed remedies; employment for women and girls, and preparation for it. 

When an inquiry relates to any department of social effort for which a Society already exists, we refer the inquirer to that Society to supplement such information as we are able to give. In this way we are a feeder for other organisations as well as a reservoir of information. 

It is as a Clearing House for other Societies that the Institute must find its greatest usefulness, and to achieve its full public value it must become a Societies' Society, so that it may live up to its motto: "The 

Experience of All for the Benefit of Each." Seeking to disseminate fact and not to propagate opinions, keeping in touch with all schools of thought and identifying itself with none, the Institute appeals for

more support from Societies. They are frequently among our inquirers, and constantly benefit by the stimulus which the Institute administers through answers to questions as well as in the pages of "PROGRESS." Yet at present only sixteen Societies give tangible recognition of the Institute's value. At the end of 1907 the Bolton, Bradford, Halifax, and Harrogate Guilds of Help, Carnegie Dunfermline Trust, Dunfermline Civic Guild, Social Institutes Union, Wesleyan Methodist Union for Social Service, and Working Men's Club and Institutes 

Union were subscribers, and since then the Central Bureau for the 

Employment of Women, Garden City Association, London Domestic Mission, Marylebone Health Society, Bethnal Green Public Welfare 

Association, and the Society for the Destruction of Vermin have made or promised contributions. We urge readers to encourage other Societies, of which they are members, to do likewise.

TRAINING FOR SOCIAL WORK. 

THE provision made by the School of Social 

Science in connection with the Liverpool

University for the Training of Social Workers is so valuable and sets such an excellent example to others that we gladly call attention to it once more. Social work of one kind and another is attracting so much attention; our Guilds of Help, Health 

Visiting Committees, and other agencies for Social Service are calling out such an army of volunteers that it is of the utmost importance that they should have the opportunity of learning, by theory and practice, the best and wisest methods of working. Such opportunity 

See "PROGRESS," Vol. I., F. 231.

PROGRESS 

is provided by the courses of lectures, classes, and practical demonstrations that have been arranged in connection with this University School. The courses extend over two years and embrace the following subjects :-The Nature and Growth of Society, Local Administration, Administration of Charitable Relief, History and Administration of the Poor Law, Motives to Social Service, Social 

Problems in relation to Children. This last consists of five lectures by Miss Macadam (Victoria Women's Settlement), and is specially intended for "School Managers, visitors to invalid children, Sunday School teachers, and others engaged in work among children.” 

But the chief value of the scheme lies in the fact that thorough practical work is combined with theoretic teaching. Certificates are granted to those who prove themselves efficient, and for those who are working to obtain it the following scheme of practical work has been prepared to cover one year : 

First Three Months. Ground work in charity organisation; case work method; clubs. 

Second Three Months. Skilled employment and apprenticeship; work under school managers (a) elementary, (b) special; care of invalid children; after care. 

Last Six Months. Experience in different forms of work:-country holidays; vacation schools; thrift; District Nursing Association; investigation of industrial and other subjects. 

To complete its good work the School has also provided a library and offices similar, though on a smaller scale, to that provided by our own Institute. Opportunity is here afforded for advanced study and investigation in the various branches of Social Economics, and inquirers and workers are assisted with information as to social methods adopted and experiments tried elsewhere. HON. SEC., Miss Oliver Jones, Victoria Women's Settlement, 294, Netherfield

Road, Liverpool. 

WORK AMONG THE BLIND. 

FOR 37 years the South London Institute has been carrying on a beneficent work among the blind in Southwark, a number estimated at 4,000. Its work has outgrown its accommodation, and last autumn it entered upon its new building, erected by private benevolence, though, unfortunately, not yet paid for. The following objects, as set forth in the Committee's Report, should appeal to the sympathies of all :— 

1. By means of voluntary helpers to organise the work of charity among the blind poor of London. 

2. To aid blind men and women to obtain suitable employment. 

3. To send the younger blind into schools and training shops, and obtain grants and pensions for the older ones. 

4. To provide healthy and elevating literature, a suitable library, and healthy entertainment.

5. To visit the sick and infirm, and to grant relief after careful investigation. 

6. To provide dormitory accommodation for young unmarried blind women. 

One very interesting feature of the work of this Society is the provision that has been made to secure some home-life for young unmarried and friendless blind girls. The new building has nine cubicles, cosily furnished, and when funds allow more will be added. 

This is a great boon-the wages of the blind must always be low, and the danger of going to and fro to work is considerable. 

TRADE SCHOOLS. 

RATHER 

more than two years ago the

Report of the L.C.C. on the Apprenticeship 

Question recommended the establishment of Trade Schools. Since then action has been taken, and in no direction has it been more successful than in its establishment of Trade Schools for Girls.-Of these there are now six in full operation, under the control of the L.C.C. They are to be found at the Borough and Woolwich Polytechnics, at the Paddington and 

Shoreditch Technical Institutes, at Wandsworth, and at Morley

College. The subjects taught, though varying somewhat in the different schools, comprise dressmaking, waistcoat-making, ladies' tailoring, corset-making, millinery, upholstery, and laundry. The girls enter when they have finished their elementary school course, and during six hours each day (including an hour for dinner), they receive thorough theoretical and practical instruction in one or more of the above subjects. The object of these schools, as stated in the prospectus of that at the Borough Institute, is, "to give the pupils a thorough preliminary training in a skilled trade, and at the same time to continue their general education, so as to develop their intelligence, and enable them, after two or three years of workshop experience, to become the skilled, intelligent workers for whom there is always a demand." The course of instruction, of which an interesting account is given in the Queen (March 14th, 1908), follows very much on the lines of the Ecoles Professionelle in Paris, described in an earlier number of "PROGRESS."* The usual course is for two years, for which the small fee of 9d. a week is charged. In order to bring the pupils who have completed their training into contact with employers requiring such service, exhibitions of their work are held from time to time, so that employers can judge of the work each girl can do, and engage accordingly. 

---

PROGRESS The Workshop Schools in Berne. The Lehrwerkstätte in 

Switzerland have established a well-deserved reputation, and none stand higher than those of its capital, the latest report of which has just reached us. They are municipal institutions, but carried on under the direction and control of the Canton and the State. They are established for the purpose of giving a thorough practical training in various handicrafts to youths of 15 and upwards. The pupils are classed under four main divisions :-machinists, cabinet-makers, and upholsterers, workers in iron, and workers in lead, tin, zinc, etc. 

For the first, the course extends for four years; for the others, only for three. The school hours are 9 each day, with only three 19 

ROOM FURNISHED ENTIRELY BY APPRENTICES IN THE BERNE WORKSHOP SCHOOLS. 

weeks' holiday during the year. The tuition is free, but pupils have to find their own drawing materials, mathematical instruments, etc. 

After the first year the pupils in the machine shops receive a small payment. This varies according to the proficiency of the pupil; it averages about 25 cents per day for the first year of payment, 50 cents for the second, and 75 cents for the third. Pupils in the other divisions are paid at the same rate, but in their case payments begin at the close of the first half-year. 

In addition to the above, shorter (quarterly, half-yearly, or yearly) courses are arranged for young artisans who wish to perfect themselves either in the theory or technique of their particular craft, or in some special branch of it. The pupils attending the shorter

courses are usually apprentices who find, after completing their apprenticeship, certain deficiencies which they wish to remedy. If they aspire to be foremen or master-workmen, they take the full year's course. All these classes are also free, but the school hours are somewhat shorter, ranging from thirty to fifty-two per week.

LADY ASSISTANTS' CHORAL PRACTICE. 

founder - M.

Boucicaut-is due, not merely to his own keen business faculties, but also to his recognition of the fact that his own prosperity depended largely on his employees.

At the outset, therefore, he decided to establish such conditions as would make all their work the spontaneous expression of their best impulses. He pursued this course from the time when he first took over the direction of the business; he raised their salaries and at the same time gave them a direct interest in their sales; he suppressed all fines; inaugurated Sunday and Holiday closing. For their bodily comfort he provided a large dining hall (380 ft. long), in which 6,500 luncheons are served daily and gratuitously, also reading and concert rooms. 

But the special feature of his work is the generous provision made by his Provident and Pension Funds to shield his employees from the anxieties of old age or infirmity. 

THE PROVIDENT FUND is created by the annual deduction of a certain proportion of the profits of the business.

Assistants on

PROGRESS 

completing 5 years' service with the firm are entitled to a share in this annual division of profits in proportion to their salaries. The capital is retained for a time by the Firm, and interest is paid at 4 per cent., but on the completion of 10 years' service one-third can be withdrawn; after 15 years, two-thirds can be withdrawn by male, or the whole by female, assistants, or the whole can be withdrawn on reaching the age of 45 (female) or 50 (male). The 

Fund last year amounted to £190,536 of which there were 3,280 participants.

THE PENSION FUND was founded by Madame Boucicaut to complete her husband's work. From her own fortune she gave 

Fr. 5,000,000, to which the Firm adds a percentage of its annual profits. The annual amount from both sources now available for distribution is Fr. 618,000 (£24,720). All assistants on attaining the age of 45 (female) or 50 (male) are entitled to a pension if they have completed 20 years' service, or earlier if incapable of continuing their duties. The minimum pension is a life annuity of Fr. 600 (24), the maximum Fr. 1,500 (£60). There are now 504 pensioners. There is also a Special 

Pension Fund for the widows and orphans of assistants, and another for the workpeople employed in the various workrooms of the Bon Marché. This latter provides for workpeople disabled by sickness or accident; for

OPEN-AIR SCHOOLS IN ENGLAND. 

THERE is some hope that the Educational

Authorities in this country are beginning to realise the hygienic benefit of the Open-Air 

School. We move slowly and like to see results before trying new experiments. Such results have now been supplied by Germany,* and the L.C.C., quick to appreciate their * Vide "PROGRESS," No. 10, pp. 86-98.

importance, has established the three schools already described in our 

The necessary object-lesson has thus been brought nearer home, and other authorities are following on the same lines. 

It 

We have received accounts of two open-air schools that were started in the summer; one at HALIFAX (opened July 20th), the other at BRADFORD (opened August 31st). Like those of the L.C.C. both these schools preserve the essential features of their German prototypes :—the children are selected by the Medical School Officer from the weak and anæmic of the elementary schools; they are to remain long enough to give reasonable hope of some permanent improvement; and all arrangements as to curriculum, exercise, rest, and food are so arranged that while the health of the children is the first consideration their education is not to be neglected. Halifax has taken for the purpose the long-untenanted mansion and grounds of Bermerside, formerly the residence of Mr. Edward Crossley. is admirably suited for the purpose; the mansion and outbuildings can be utilised when the weather is too inclement to be out of doors, while at other times there are the spacious grounds with their pleasant lawns and thickly-shaded paths. It is here that sixty delicate children (between the ages of 7 and 10), drawn from ten of the poorest schools, spend their days and receive their education for three months during the present year, and for a longer period afterwards. Bradford, which has always made the health of the children a foremost consideration, was not long behind its neighbour, but, as the school was not opened till late in the summer, the time during the present year will be too short to give any fair test of results. Bradford has placed its school on its Eshold Estate, on a well-sheltered croft five acres in extent and close to a great wood. It is some five miles from the city, but the children, only forty in number at present, are taken free by car to and from the school daily. For this first experiment the selected children are between the ages of 8 and 9 years. 

The school daily life and general arrangements are practically the same in each and the same as those already described in Germany and London :-the small classes with from two to two-and-a-half hours only of formal instruction; the two hours' afternoon rest; the three (Bradford) or four (Halifax) meals a day, and everything (lessons, meals, rest, and exercise) in the open air when possible. 

London at Kentish Town, and Halifax at Bermerside, have shown that the deserted mansions which now too often become the prey of the jerry-builders may be utilised to bring joy and health into the lives of the children and at the same time prevent the creation of other slums. 

Ibid. No. 11, p. 216.
