PUBLIC-HOUSE RIVALS. 

In a recent speech Lord Lytton is reported to have said "the State must become a formidable rival to the public-house in catering for the social needs of the people." This may be supplemented with a remark by Dr. Paton when addressing the Summer School at Hay field last June: "After ten hours cooped up in the atmosphere of the workshop, a boy wants to have his fling, and he ought to have it." The same is true of young men and women, and temperance reformers are recognising that these needs must be met if we are to make any effective headway.

The SOCIAL INSTITUTES UNION is rendering fine service in this direction, and now that it has united with the Federation of Working Men's Clubs and established a closer alliance with that for Working Boys its work is considerable. Its main object is "to promote the establishment, maintenance, and union of Institutes and Clubs for men and women, founded with a view to provide means of social intercourse, education, and recreation." Its conditions are that such clubs must be non-political and unsectarian, must allow no alcoholic drink or gambling on the premises, and must only admit as members those who have reached the age of 18 in the case of males and 16 females. With this object in view it is working both in London and the Provinces, seeking to establish in various centres branches of the parent society that will themselves engage in similar propaganda work. With the exception of the W. H. Smith Memorial Institute in Kingsway, the society does not run any club under its own direct management, but is ready at all times to give advice, personal service, and, when necessary, small monetary assistance to all affiliated branches. The object of the union is to bring the various clubs into touch with one another and keep them from becoming selfcentred. In London alone there are now no fewer than IOI affiliated clubs (men's and women's), with a membership of about 1,800, and in addition to these there are a large number in other parts of the country. The Women's Branch is securing the establishment of additional clubs for women. These numbers look hopeful on paper, but compared with the need they are but a drop in the bucket. If the number of public-houses is reduced more counterattractions must be found.

There are many who fully recognise this,

but are at a loss how to set to work.

They will do well to apply to 

Mr. R. G. Pearse, Gen. Sec. Social Institutes Union, Kingsway, W.C. 

In connection with this same subject it may be mentioned that a 

By courtesy of the Social Institutes Union. 

A QUIET EVENING IN ONE OF THE INSTITUTES. 

lady has written to the Institute saying, "I am perfectly convinced that if all public-houses are to be closed on Sundays, and many altogether, there must be some place provided for the people where

PROGRESS 

they can have shelter, light, brightness, beauty and entertainment."

Her suggestion is that the various societies in London working for temperance and social betterment should unite to establish one Winter Garden, similar to that established by Lady Bell in Middlesbro';* if that succeeded it would be an encouragement to start others.

She backs up her suggestion with the offer of 100 towards any suitable scheme under responsible management. 

It is a fine opportunity for our public-spirited men and women to whom wealth has been committed as a trust.

GLEBE LAND AND SMALL HOLDINGS. 

IN the January number of the Albany Review the Rev. A. E. T. Newman tells the story of the complete uplifting of the population of an entire village by the breaking up of the glebe land into allotments. Thirty years ago, Granborough, in 

Bucks, was in the miserable plight in which so many of our agricultural villages are found to-day. Its young folk were leaving it for the towns, while the labourers who remained, earning but a pittance while at work and turned off in the winter, were many of them compelled to go upon the rates. 

Now all this is changed, and changed by the wise action of a former Vicar, the present Bishop of Truro. He simply broke up his glebe into allotments large enough to allow the cultivator to grow something more than his own potatoes and cabbages, and by doing so to make a comfortable addition to his weekly income. The land was taken up at once. The village population is only 280, but among these are divided 70 acres, of which 55 are arable. The lots, which are let at the rent previously paid by the farmer, are each half an acre, but few have been found content with one lot, some hold three, one man has four. During the last 30 years the fall in the value of land has reduced the income of many livings 50 per cent. ; in 

Granborough the value of the living has increased and a healthy relationship established between the labourer and the Vicar. 

Sixteen years later the same experiment was tried in Warwickshire, and with equal success by Mr. Tuckwell, at that time the 

Vicar of the parish. The glebe consisted of 200 acres ; half of this was rented by small farmers, the remainder by 80 labourers. 

It had previously been left uncultivated, bringing no income to the Vicar and no advantage to his poor, half starving parishioners. The land had become so foul that for the first 2 years no rent was *Vide "PROGRESS," No. 9, p. 44.

charged; after that time the rent was £1 per acre, with fixity of tenure during Mr. Tuckwell's incumbency. The lots ranged from acre to 3 acres, and 5 years after the land had been brought into cultivation an expert, sent down by the Allotments Association, reported that the allotments alone had added-by consumption, not sale-4s. a week per acre to the labourer's income, and that no less than £800 had been taken out of the land and poured into the cottages. When Mr. Tuckwell left, "another Pharaoh arose,' who broke the leases and raised the rents. The men abandoned their holdings, and what was a Garden of Eden in 1893, in 1897 was a desolate wilderness. Mr. Newman, who succeeded Mr. Stubbs as Vicar of Granborough recommends all owners of glebe, in their own interests and in that of their villages, to consider the possibility of dividing the land among the labourers. His experience seems to fully justify the recommendation, but that of Mr. Tuckwell, as told in the "Reminiscences of a Radical Parson," indicate that it cannot always be done without considerable opposition from the " powers that be," and that when done it may easily be undone by an unwise successor. "IN the adolescent age" (says Dr. Stanley

BOYS' SOCIETY Hall in his monumental work on that subject), (St. Saviour's, "powers and faculties, essentially non-existent. Liverpool). before, are born; new forces and impulses are developed; new tastes formed which are necessary to the adult life. These powers and impulses, if not trained, become a source of mischief." To provide this training at this critical period this 

Society was started 3 years ago. It has taken as its basis the elementary schools of the City, its object being to lay hold of the boys as they leave school, and by enrolling them among its members to aid them to develop into Christian men. Those who have had anything to do with boys' clubs will understand the difficulty of the task; many have been called but few chosen." To succeed in this work requires a wise and tactful statesmanship, but the regulations of this Liverpool Society give strong evidence that this quality belongs to its originators. 

In many respects it is like other boys' clubs; it has the ordinary rules—though perhaps more carefully drawn than many—regarding admission, fees, etc.; it makes the usual provision for games, physical culture, summer rambles, evening classes, etc., but there is one special feature in these regulations which marks it out as

PROGRESS 

worthy of the special attention of all who are engaged in a similar work. It is best described by its own Handbook : 

During the first year, probationership simply requires obedience to rules. 

During the second, membership involves previous requirements and a regular connection with self-improvement subjects in evening schools or other definite undertaking. 

During the third year, senior membership includes previous requirements, with a recommendation to try to obtain some certificate of proficiency in self-culture subject. 

During the fourth year, fellowship requires distinction in self-culture subject, if practical and possible, and full membership of some church. 

The aim is that each member shall engage at home or elsewhere in some helpful subject or useful hobby and keep himself in touch with some means of improving his chances in life. Promotion in the status of membership depends upon years of satisfactory connection with the Society, and upon progress in self-improvement according to opportunities and abilities. In all arrangements the boys are taken into fullest confidence. The government is democratic and, though connected with an Anglican Church, it is entirely non-sectarian, the only condition being that on Sundays the members shall "attend some one Sunday School, Bible Class, or Place of 

Worship." A MINING VILLAGE AS A GARDEN CITY. 

THE mining village, as a rule, has very little in common with the Garden City, and yet one is now being laid out in Yorkshire which, according to the Municipal Journal, bids fair to be a serious rival to Letchworth, Bournville, and Port Sunlight. 

The scheme is being carried out by Mr. A. B. Markham, M.P., and his colleagues of the Brodsworth Main Colliery Co., who have leased a large estate known as Woodlands, near to Doncaster. On this it is proposed to build 1,000 Model Cottages, with an average of about 5 to the acre. These houses, which have each been given a 25 ft. frontage in order to obtain the maximum of light, air, and sunshine, will contain either large living room (18 ft. by 13 ft.), scullery and 3 bedrooms (or parlour, kitchen, scullery, 3 bedrooms), and bathroom fitted with hot and cold water. Each house will have its own w.c., and each house has cross-ventilation from the front door to a window opening on the staircase. Cupboards, dressers, and wardrobes are being fitted copper is placed in the scullery. per week, with rates additional. with wide grass margins and a double avenue of trees; radiating from this are other roads 50 ft. wide, but as each house is set back from the road 25 ft. to 30 ft., there will be at least 100 ft. space into the rooms, and a steamflue 

The rents vary from 5s. to 5s. 9d.

The main road is 120 ft. wide,

between, which with large open spaces at the rear will secure a healthy environment for all.

The large public baths, consisting of a central swimming bathround which are grouped a number of shower baths-—which Mr. Markham is presenting to the village, is one of the most pleasing features of the scheme. Here each miner will be provided with a separate wardrobe and locker where he can leave his clean clothes when going to his work, resuming them before going home after a thorough cleansing with shower and plunge. 

His working clothes will be well aired and dried in a steam heated corridor before again required. Those who understand how near cleanliness is to godliness will appreciate the moral effect of this provision on a mining population. One other feature completes the picture of this model village. On one position of the estate stands a mansion which is to be converted into a workmen's club. This is surrounded by gardens, lawns, a park of 12 acres with fine large forest trees and a fishing lake of 4 acres, all of which are to be preserved, with an additional 10 acres of meadow, for the enjoyment and recreation of the inhabitants.

HOMES FOR THE AGED POOR. 

THE erection of the charming Hampton 

Cottages by Mr. W. Brittain Jones at 

Knockhold, Sevenoaks, is the expression of a generous desire to add comfort to the declining years of the aged poor. So far as we know, the idea is unique. 

Subject to the payment of the rates and a small charge for conservancy (less than 1/6 a week), the use of the cottage is granted rent free; at the same time they are not Almshouses, but ordinary cottages that might be let in the ordinary way, and they are not intended for the destitute, careful provision being made that no tenant shall ever have received Parish relief or be in danger of having to apply for it. To secure the latter each applicant must show a reasonable certainty of being able to support himself without having recourse to the Parish. This may be done either by showing membership in a Friendly Society, or promise of help from relatives or friends; failing this, Mr. Jones requires the would-be tenant to deposit £5 in a Savings Bank, such sum to be kept intact during occupancy, interest being paid to the tenant. Subject to these and to the further conditions that the applicant belongs to the working class, is over 55 years of age (unless his power of earning has been prematurely impaired by accident or illness), is a native of, or five

PROGRESS 

years resident in, the parish, and that his family is not too large for the accommodation, the free use of the cottage is granted for life, or during good behaviour. The £5 deposit is intended to make any necessary eviction possible without hardship. Occupants are encouraged to pursue their old avocations, and may remain as tenants FFE 

By courtesy of Mr. W. Brittain Jones. 

HAMPTON COTTAGES FOR THE AGED POOR. 

unless their earnings become so large that a rent-free cottage ceases to be of importance. Mr. Jones says "that in the Hampton 

Cottages he wishes to do for such parishioners as have set examples of industry, independence and respectability, what the King does at 

Hampton Court for the great servants of the State."

SOCIAL SERVICE IN BIRMINGHAM. 

THE Civic sense has long been a powerful factor in Birmingham life, and many of her citizens have taken a front rank among England's social servants, and more than once the pages of "PROGRESS 

have contained accounts of some of her various social activities.* From others that have reached us we select two-the School for Mothers and the People's Kindergartenwhich have a special interest in the fact that they are carried on mainly by two sisters the moving spirits, if not the actual originators-who are members of a family honourably known in the city for their 

Social Service. 

*Vide "PROGRESS," Vol. II., No. 5, pp. 50, 53, and No. 8, pp. 281-2.

The School for Mothers.-Miss Bunting's experiment in St. 

Pancras has been already described; * Miss Lloyd has started another in Birmingham. The "School" was opened last January in one of the most thickly populated areas of the city. It is run on similar lines to that in St. Pancras—there are the visits to the home before and after the birth of the child; the weekly "At Homes" for the mothers; and the consultations with the doctors to which the mothers come with their infants. Classes are held also for teaching cooking and nursing. When addressing the Hayfield Summer 

School on "Motherhood and the Infant," Mr. Broadbent, of 

Huddersfield, drew attention to the fact that "civilisation has destroyed maternal instinct, and civilisation has forgotten to supply knowledge and intelligence in place of what it has taken away." It is to remove this reproach that these Schools for Mothers have been started, and the work is being admirably done in the one in Birmingham. Large illustrated posters (5 ft. by 3 ft.), giving information about the feeding of children, are hung round the "At Home" room, and the mothers are asked their opinions on the matters with which the posters deal; handbills are distributed warning against the danger of using “dummies,” and object lessons given to enforce the warning; and Health Visitors give short, 20 minutes' talks on the management of children, on consumption and precautions to be taken, with practical illustrations of the need for obtaining proper ventilation. by windows and chimneys. All lectures are accompanied by practical demonstrations, even to the bathing of the baby. consultations the babies are undressed and weighed and information obtained as to their nutrition, habits, illnesses, etc., also particulars as to home conditions, all of which are carefully recorded for future Except in certain special cases no food is given, but mothers are taught how to prepare nutritious and economical meals for themselves. Cast-off garments, given by ladies, are sold to the mothers at nominal prices and paid for in instalments. use. 

At the

When the "School" was opened on January 8th five mothers attended and two doctors; the next week an invitation was given to tea, and 30 or 40 attended. This free tea was only given once; since then the mothers have been supplied at their own request at d. a cup. At the end of June there were on the Society's register 

282 mothers, 217 of whom are members of the "At Home," and 105 consulted the doctors.

The People's Kindergarten.-When considering the Education problem one of the most hopeful signs is the revival of the Pestalozzi- 

*Vide "PROGRESS," Vol. II., No. 8, p. 268.

PROGRESS 

Froebel system for the development of child-life.

Sesame House in 

London-the prototype of which was the Pestalozzi-Froebel Haus in Berlin-supplies the teachers, and the two kindergartens recently established in Birmingham (and there is the prospect of a third) give valuable object lessons in the carrying out of Froebel's scheme of training. The first of these was established some four years ago, by 

By courtesy of Miss Julia Lloyd.

INTERIOR OF PEOPLE'S KINDERGARTEN, GREET. 

the generosity of Mr. and Mrs. Barrow Cadbury, in a squalid suburb of the city. The success of this, under the enthusiastic directorate of Miss Julia Lloyd, led to the starting of a second Kindergarten three years later at the Women's Settlement, and the Birmingham People's Kindergarten Association was formed to extend the movement.

The aims or the work are thus stated :- 

1. To give natural healthy conditions for children under school age, i.e., up to the ages of five or six years. 

2.- -To lay a foundation for life in the acquisition of habits of order and cleanlinessto build up character by opportunities for mutual helpfulness and through the fostering of life in plant and animal. 

3. To train hand-power and develop the muscles at an age when the instinct for action is strong. 

4. To give a basis for school instruction by experiences gained in connection with garden and domestic work and the care of pets. 

5.-To re-act on home life through the training of the children to habits of helpfulness and the appreciation of order and beauty. 

6. To give girls opportunity to learn practically and theoretically how to provide for the necessities of child nature. 

In order to accomplish the latter (No. 6) it is hoped that the girls in the upper standard of the Council Schools will be allowed to take the Kindergarten as their training ground for home life, and thus bridge over the existing gap. No training ground could be better for the purpose. The children are drawn from the poorest class, and no child is over 6 years of age, yet under this system it has been found possible to develop the whole child nature, and at the same time to inculcate habits of order and cleanliness and to teach them to find their happiness in mutual helpfulness. The subjects of the teaching are all taken from nature, but attention is concentrated on one subject at a time in order to deepen the impressions received. 

Thus for July it was the cow-a cow in a neighbouring field furnishing the object. With this before them these little ones were taught not only to feed the cow, but to buy and measure out the milk from the milkman, to skim it and even help to churn the cream into butter or make it into cheese. The teaching of each subject is always accompanied by correlated games and songs. The afternoons are spent in the garden helping to dig and hoe and plant in the early months, and later to gather vegetables and fruit, which they either prepare for cooking or make into jam.

At present these Kindergartens are private institutions, but it is hoped that the Education Authority may take them over and carry them on on the same principles. 

TO SAVE BABIES. 

THE 

An excellent work, very similar to that of our School for Mothers, has been carried on in the 

Säuglingsheim in Munich during the last five 

As in our "Schools," the Consultations and Goutte de Lait have a prominent place; the babies are brought to the Home, medically examined and weighed, and all needful advice given to the years.

mothers; milk tested and prepared in the Home is supplied at something under cost price to those who are unable to give breast feeding. 

The number of babies brought daily averages over forty, a number for which the present premises are all too small. When the writer visited it last May, the entrance hall and passages leading to Dr. 

Meyer's consulting rooms were crowded with waiting mothers. 

Nursing mothers, unable to supply themselves with nourishing food, are given free meals as in the Restaurant Gratuits in Paris and elsewhere. 

But the Säuglingsheim undertakes a larger work than that of the School for Mothers. It is a Home as well as a School; it provides a Hospital where delicate babies (under one year old), requiring such special treatment, are housed and nursed under medical supervision. 

During last year 231 babies (124 legitimate, 107 illegitimate) were thus cared for. The average length of their stay was sixty-three days. 

But after leaving the Hospital they continue to receive in their houses the oversight and attention of the medical staff so long as needful. 

The same gratuitous medical attention is given to all nursing mothers who need but cannot afford to pay. A Summer Home has also been provided, where from May to October thirty babies can be taken by their mothers for light, air, and sunshine; and, still further, there is a Lying-in Hospital. Every effort is made to reduce the terrible infant mortality among the children of all classes, and for this purpose special courses of instruction are given for the training of nurses, whether for the homes of the wealthy or for visitation among the poor. Dr. Meyer, the senior physician of the Säuglingsheim, stated that, while the infant mortality in the city stood at over 20 per cent., and in S. Bavaria much higher, that among those ministered to by the Home had fallen to 8 per cent.; no wonder he was enthusiastic about his work. The total annual cost of this work is rather over £2,000, a portion of which is defrayed by the municipality. 

A SWISS INSTITUTE OF SOCIAL SERVICE. 

22 UNDER the somewhat modest title of the 

"Central Depôt for Social Literature (Cen tralstelle für Soziale Literatur) a work is being carried on in Zürich on similar lines-though on a somewhat more limited scale—to those of the British Institute. 

At present the work is confined mainly to the collection of a wellclassified library of information on all subjects that pertain to social and industrial betterment. This information is placed at the disposal of all who wish to avail themselves of its advantages; a librarian has been appointed to assist inquirers, and a reading and study room

have been provided for the use of all who require an opportunity for more careful research. But the establishment of this Bureau of information has naturally led to inquiries from many who wish for information which they are unable to apply for in person. These inquiries come from many lands, and, as far as possible, are readily answered. This important branch of its work will doubtless extend rapidly, as it has done with us; but it is pleasant to learn that this development has been helped by subsidies from both the Municipal and Cantonal Governments. 

(Stepney). 

"To facilitate co-operation between all orga- FEDERATION OF nisations that have social aims, and, as far as 

SOCIAL FORCES 

possible, to prevent overlapping," is one of the first objects of the British Institute; we therefore welcome most heartily the newly-formed STEPNEY COUNCIL 

OF PUBLIC WELFARE, which seeks to do for Stepney what we are seeking to do for the whole country. It is a movement very similar to that recently started in Hampstead,* and in the main follows the same lines both as to objects and organisation. 

For some years Stepney has had not only a Council of Public Welfare, but also a Visitors' Association working independently; these two bodies have now amalgamated, and at the same time the reorganised Council has widened its scope. The Executive, now acting for both, carries on its work through the following subcommittees, each concerned with its own department :- (1) Public Bodies, which seeks to influence these bodies in favour of desirable schemes of social welfare. (2) Charity Co-operation, composed of representatives of the various local charitable agencies, and aiming at the prevention of overlapping. (3) Health, which takes over the work of the Visitors' Association in its effort to reduce the rate of infant mortality, the ravages consumption, etc. of (4) Temperance and Morality, for unifying the temperance, rescue, and preventive work in the borough. (5) Social Study and Literature, which promotes the formation of local circles for the study of social questions. 

A committee of working men to deal with industrial law, thrift, etc., is shortly to be organised. Such a society, working on such broad lines, free from all taint of sectarianism or party spirit, and having Toynbee Hall for its power-house, should be able to face with some confidence even the tangled problems of Mile End, Whitechapel, and St. George's-in-the-East.

Der Volksverein für das Katholische Deutchland, which has its centre in M. Gladbach, has numerous branches in all parts of 

Germany which give help in various ways to Catholic working men and women in both urban and rural districts. The Central Information Bureau in the town itself is a large, handsome building. containing a library of over 5,000 vols. The Bureau gives valuable information to working men and women on all matters relating to their sick, accident, infirmity or old age insurance, and also regarding Factory laws and regulations, Housing and Sanitation, etc. in 1890, it has now more than 350 affiliated branches.

The Libraries of London and Elsewhere. The following interesting statistics are given in "The Libraries of London," recently issued by the University. In 1906 the population of "Greater 

London" was 7,113,561. The number of volumes in the public and administrative libraries, and in those libraries of societies and institutions, is approximately 8,000,000. Deducting 500,000 of these as being in libraries with very restricted conditions of admission, we find that London provides a little over one volume per head; 

Berlin, with a population of about 2,500,000, possesses about 5,000,000 vols. in public and society libraries, or about two vols. per head; Dresden, with about 500,000 inhabitants, has 1,500,000 vols. in its libraries, or three vols. per head.

There is no public or semi-public library in London other than the British Museum with more than 175,000 vols.; Berlin possesses two besides the Königliche Bibliothek.

Ireland's Crusade against Tuberculosis.-The Registrar- 

General for Ireland (Sir Robert Matheson) told the British Association that already a great reform had been worked among the Irish people. 

The practice of keeping windows open, more attention to cleanliness, decrease of the habit of spitting, increased attention to the necessity for more nourishing diet, pure milk supply, efforts to provide means of segregating advanced cases of tuberculosis in inexpensive structures, provision of special district nurses to look after consumptives in their own homes, disinfection, etc. These gratifying results are largely due to the splendid efforts of the Women's National Health Association, under the presidency of Lady Aberdeen. 

Reduction of Infant Mortality; Encouraging Examples.- The President of the Local Government Board (Mr. John Burns) 

stated at the National Conference that in Huddersfield the infant mortality which, in 1891, was 18 per cent., was now only 9.7 per cent.; in 

Bristol, during the same period, it had been reduced fron 18.4 per cent. to 9.8 per cent. ; in Battersea from 17.6 per cent. to 11.5 per cent., and in some wards it stood as low as 5'1 per cent. From these facts Mr. Burns hoped that we should soon see "well-organised, well-administered, sanitarily-conducted urban centres wrestling with the small towns and rural areas for the laurels in the reduction of infant mortality." Hampstead, Battersea, and Woolwich have now almost won them.

The Larger Work of the Guild of Help.-To keep the struggling poor from sinking to the level of pauperism is one main object of the Guild of Help; it is a part of its larger work. By the generosity of one of its supporters the Halifax Guild is just now able to render a beautiful service in that direction. When it became known that no person who had received poor-relief during the preceding twelve months could receive the Old Age Pension, a sum of money was placed at the disposal of the officers of the Guild to enable them to assist deserving people who otherwise would be compelled, before January 1st, to apply to the Guardians. The investigations will be made by the Captains and Helpers of each district.

Medical Inspection in Schools (West Riding of Yorks.).— 

Ten whole-time Medical Inspectors for assigned districts are to be appointed to act under the instructions of the County Medical Officer, at a salary of £300 per annum with travelling expenses, and

PROGRESS 

an additional Medical Assistant in the County Medical Officer's Department at a salary of £300. Provisional estimates amount to £9,300 a year.

There are in the area concerned about 900 schools, If in the first year with over 200,000 children on the registers. only the children newly admitted and those leaving school are examined, this will involve about 45,000 examinations. When the work has reached its full scope the annual number of medical examinations will be not far short of 100,000.

The House Fly as Disease Carrier.-Sir Crichton Browne, when addressing the Sanitary Inspectors' Association, gave some striking facts to show the serious danger to health and life from this apparently harmless insect. Its legs are brush-like and are often found loaded with micro-organism picked up from its foul haunts. 

Such flies have been caught and made to walk over a prepared gelatine plate; this plate has then been treated as an incubator, when it has been found that in every footstep of the fly there is a colony of disease-producing bacteria. Dr. Jackson, of New York, who has been conducting experiments on this subject for several years, has found as many as 100,000 fæcal bacteria on the legs, body, and mouth of one fly. He has also taken a sort of very rough census of the fly population of the city, noting their prevalence at certain seasons and in certain districts, from which he has been able to show “that over a period of five years there was an almost precise correspondence between the temperature curve (which indicates the activity of flies), and the curves of typhoid and other intestinal diseases."

There are 650 deaths from typhoid in New York annually, and 7,000 from other intestinal diseases, and the common house fly (says Dr. Jackson) is the chief carrier of the infection. It carries it (said 

Sir Crichton) to the milk jug, the sugar, the cake, and to every article of food on which it settles; and he added, “the fight with the fly will be a stiff one. It has been calculated that one fly will lay 1,000 eggs, so that, on the snowball principle, it may have 

25,000,000 descendants in a season." As " 

As "nothing walks with aimless feet," so the fly has its use as a scavenger; the only sure way to destroy it is to attack its breeding grounds and to leave it no dirt to scavenge.

Books on Social Subjects. 

Socialists at Work.-By ROBERT HUNTER (Author of “ Poverty”). · 

374pp. New York, Macmillan. 6/6 net.

Critics and opponents of Socialism usually complain that they cannot discover what Socialism really means, because its various expounders contradict each other, and then the opponents proceed to quote all the absurdities uttered by anybody who calls himself a 

Socialist at any time within the last half century, and to assert that herein is expressed the true Socialist faith. 

To any inquirer dissatisfied with this method of controversy, and yet puzzled with the uncertainties of the Socialist creeds, the present volume should be welcome. It is a full and impartial account, by an American, of the chief European Socialist parties in Germany, Italy, France, Belgium, and England, of their leading orators and politicians, and the work they have actually accomplished. 

Mr. Hunter's remarkable study of "Poverty" in the United 

States has already made him known as one of the most promising of the younger generation of writers on Social Science. This volume will add to his reputation. He has examined his subject at first hand, visited the countries he describes, attended Conferences of the Parties, listened to the leaders, and estimated their personalities from direct observation. An appendix by another hand deals briefly with the other countries which Mr. Hunter has not studied. 

This general view of an international movement, at present adolescent, but destined by the admission of its enemies as well as its friends, to play a great part for good or for evil in the future of the world is of equal value to the politician of this generation, and to the student of the next. Those who have for long years taken part in the building up of this great movement, must be encouraged to discover how in every land, industrial and political conditions. substantially similar—the growth of the great industries employing enormous capital and innumerable workers on the one hand; and on the other, the advance of democracy and the ever increasing "class consciousness" of the common people-are resulting in the formation of Socialist-Labour Parties on substantially similar lines. 

To others Mr. Hunter presents the spectacle of this world-wide demand for a recognition of the economic claims of the people in order to carry the conviction that Socialism must be something more than the day-dream of idealists, or the nightmare of criminals, or

PROGRESS 

the delusions of false prophets, or of blind leaders of the blind proletariat. The world-wide demand for economic freedom in the twentieth century surely must be akin to the world-wide struggle for religious liberty in the sixteenth century, and the world-wide uprisings for political liberty in the nineteenth century. In the past the extravagant powers of pope, and priest, of noble, and king, have been abated for the benefit of the nations. The real rulers of the lives of the people to-day are the capitalists who own the machines by which alone most men can live. The aim of the writer is to show that the work of the twentieth century is to find out how these excessive powers may be best abated for the benefit of the world.

Modern Mansions.-By LEWIS BRADSHAW, C.C. 

duction by Philip Snowden, M.P. 

Cloth 15.; Paper 6d. 

E. R. P.

With intro- 

Northants Publishing Co.

Our interest in this book lies in the fact that it is an attempt to give better social conditions to the mothers and daughters of England. 

The author claims that by the plan he here proposes he has found a Solution of the Housing, the Servant, and the Drink Problem.”

This is a bold claim, and we can only hope that it may be realised, but whether or no, there is much in Mr. Bradshaw's housing scheme which is well worthy of serious attention. Mr. Snowden describes it as “an attempt to secure social economy by arrangements whereby the drudgery of housework is minimised by co-operation." This is true, but at the same time it must not be confounded with the various schemes for co-operative housing which have hitherto been tried with indifferent success. The use of the word "Mansions" in the title is misleading; in London the name is associated with "Flats," which is very far from the proposal of our author. He would retain the house or cottage, but would group them by an ingenious arrangement round a central building, where the main part of the ordinary housework, such as cooking, feeding, washing, etc., would be carried on on an associated plan, and in which would be also common rooms for billiards, reading, recreation, etc. It is, as Mr. Bradshaw says, "an attempt to combine the advantages of the co-operative hotel system-the whole management of which is to be in the hands of a tenants' committee-with those of the Garden City Cottage." The details of this interesting scheme are here carefully worked out and illustrated by numerous drawings and diagrams.

The Prevention of Tuberculosis.-By ARTHUR NEWsholme, 

M.D., F.R.C.P. 430pp. Methuen & Co. IOS. 6d. net.

This book is a valuable contribution to the literature on this subject which has of late been rapidly accumulating. Dr. Newsholme has long been known as a careful, scientific investigator of the causes of this terrible disease, and his former position as the Medical Officer of Health for Brighton enabled him to test many of the conclusions to which he had arrived regarding the best methods of prevention. 

He now gives the results both of his inquiries and his experience. 

The book is divided into three parts:-The Causes; the Most 

Successful Means of Prevention; and the Necessary Administrative 

Measures. The carefully-compiled statistics given in Part I., embodying as they do "historical and international experience," are rightly regarded by Dr. Newsholme as indispensable to the main argument of the book. It is by these that he estimates the influence of the various causes which lead to the existence and spread of tuberculosis, among the most important of which are infection from food and the effect of the environment in the home and workshopboth as regards direct infection and in lowering the power of resistance to infection. In the Second Part the author examines the various means that have been tried to combat this evil, and is led to the conclusion that the reduction of mortality from tuberculosis has been mainly secured by segregation. This fact has naturally an important bearing on the administrative measures discussed in the closing chapters. Dr. Newsholme expresses the hope that this book, though written "almost solely from the standpoint of the public health administrator, and intended primarily for the Medical Officers of Health," may yet prove interesting and useful "to all medical practitioners, to many members of Sanitary Authorities and Hospital Committees, and to that increasing proportion of the public who desire to know more of preventive medicine.”

Annual Charities Register and Digest, 1908.-998pp. Long mans, Green & Co. and Charity Organisation Society. 5s. net. 

No one who is engaged in any kind of charitable work in London should be without this book. For the completeness of its information there is nothing that can take its place, and we are glad to learn that the publishers have been able to reduce the price from 10s. 6d. to 5s., and to issue annually instead of every four years as hitherto.

This will be a great boon; such information loses much of its value unless it is kept up to date. By an annual publication this will be

PROGRESS 

secured. With our limited space it is impossible to convey any idea of the mass of information contained in its 1,000 pages, but something may be gathered from the headings of its various sections. 

Five of these are devoted to the various charities engaged in Relief of some kind or other, e.g.—Affliction (Blind, Cripples, etc.); Sickness (General and Special Hospitals, etc.); Distress (Permanent and 

Temporary); Reformatory (Industrial Schools, Prisoners' Aid, etc.). 

Other sections give particulars of C.O.S. work at home and abroad; information regarding Emigration, Employment, Religious Institutions, Trade, Benevolent and Friendly Societies, etc. Only fraudulent institutions and societies are excluded. In addition to the 

Register of these various Societies, 324 pages are devoted to an elaborate introduction by Mr. C. S. Lock on "How to help cases of distress." This covers almost every conceivable form of distress that can appeal for help, and gives legal and other information of the utmost value to all workers among the poor. The fact that the index alone takes 75 pages will give some idea of the copiousness of the information stored up in this volume. 

The Worker's Handbook. By GERTRUDE M. TUCKWELL and CONSTANCE SMITH. 

251pp. Duckworth & Co. 

3s. 6d. net.

We can cordially commend this book, not only to the attention of those for whom it is specially written, but also to that of all who have the interest of the workers at heart. It gives just those facts which every worker ought to know regarding laws already passed for his protection from infancy to old age, and also some account of the more important voluntary agencies that are endeavouring to supplement deficiencies in our legislation. Under its five main divisions- 

The Worker in Childhood; At Home; At Work; In Sickness and Want; and as Citizen-it gathers up and presents in clear and concise form a mass of information which otherwise could only be found by searching the pages of several more or less bulky books or reports. It gives, in fact, a bird's eye view of all that is necessary for the worker to know on the various subjects that it handles. 

Perhaps it is too much to expect that every worker will take advantage of the opportunity here offered of acquainting himself with the facts which so vitally concern him and his children, but we may hope that many will do so, and that they will talk about what they read to their mates. Apart from the worker this book should prove an invaluable Handbook to Guild or Health Visitors, who are often in perplexity as to the best method to help some particular case.

The Organisation of Agriculture.-By EDWIN PRATT. 472pp. 

John Murray. Is. net.

The economic advantage of Co-operation among farmers is now so fully recognised by all who have tried it, that Mr. Pratt has been well advised to issue a new edition of his book. The body of the work remains as it appeared in the first edition four years ago, but a lengthy Prefatory Note indicates some of the further developments that have since taken place. As one striking instance of the progress that has been made during the last four years, we are told that the Eastern Counties Farmers' Co-operative Association, which was then just founded, has now 686 members, some of whom farm over 2,000 acres. Several instances are given of the assistance given by these Societies to obtain large reductions for their members both in prices of seeds, feeding stuffs, manures, etc., and also in the railway and other rates for conveyance of produce to market. 

Even greater progress has been shown in Ireland, Denmark, and other countries. 

We are glad to learn that the Co-operative Banks Association has now amalgamated with the A.O.S., and that fourteen Agricultural 

Credit Societies have already been formed on Raiffeism lines. The great benefit of Co-operation, both for buying and selling, and also for obtaining loans on easy terms, only needs to be known for it to become universal; this book will spread the knowledge. 

Organised Games for the School, the Hall, or the Playground. 

By FRANK ELSTON. 

Leeds. 45. 6d. net. 

192pp. E. J. Arnold & Son (Ltd.), Educational Organised Playground Games. By THOS. CHESTERTON, with Preface by Thos. D. Savill, M.D. 119pp. 

Supply Association. 

Now that public attention has been roused to the importance o organised play, and the Board of Education has so far recognised its importance as to allow it a place in the curriculum, all that helps to promote a rational method of physical development along these lines has a distinct value. Mr. Elston's book is in two parts, containing together detailed instructions for playing no less than 147 different games, with numerous photographs and diagrams as further helps to the teacher, also a few selections of such music as is required to accompany the game. Those who heard Mr. Elston's lectures on this subject at the Franco-British Exhibition must have been impressed, not only with his enthusiasm, but with his competence to speak.

Mr. Chesterton's book is a revised edition of the one published in

PROGRESS 

1901. It practically goes over the same ground as that covered by Mr. 

Elston, but describes fewer games. Whether this is a disadvantage or the reverse, must be decided by the teacher. The General Rules for Teachers in Mr. Chesterton's book will be found valuable. 

As the time for such games in the elementary school is usually limited to the fifteen or twenty minutes' interval, and by the somewhat confined space of the hall or playground, the aim of the compilers has been to secure the greatest amount of pleasant and beneficial exercise for the greatest number of children in the shortest available time. The games provide for both boys and girls, either separately or in mixed classes. At first, perhaps, the very idea of organising the play of a child may appear unnatural; but those who realise the aimless way in which the majority of children employ the short breathing space allowed during school hours, will welcome any method which, while avoiding the cast-iron precision of the drill sergeant, will combine the greatest enjoyment with the greatest physical benefit. Our various Education Authorities would do well to provide all their head teachers with a copy of one or other of these books.

Social Degradation.-By MALCOLM SPENCER, M.A. 

18opp.. 

Student Christian Movement (93, Chancery Lane). Is. net.

If one may use the phrase in connection with so sad a subject, this is a delightful book; it is so full of sympathy and faith. It is delightful also in its style; facts are given, economic laws explained, and social forces are analysed in pithy, pregnant, epigramatic sentences. 

The author has studied to some purpose the art of compression and has succeeded in giving condensed meat without destroying the flavour. And yet the book is intensely sad; it is an attempt "to photograph the conditions of social degradation as they are, and to read their inner meaning," and such chapters as those which deal with "The Physical Handicap," "The Disruption of the Home, and "The Struggle for a Livelihood," reveal a condition of things. which, while it awakens sympathy, should lead to strenuous effort. 

We are not able to deal at any length with these three chapters which form the body of the book, and would simply refer to that on "The Disruption of the Home" which by its keen analysis of social facts and forces is a valuable contribution to the vexed problems of poverty.

Mr. Spencer describes his book as "A study of poverty from the point of view of the Christian ideal of human life," and in the last

chapter on "The Religious Need," touches a note often neglected. The three sections on Character, its physical, social, and industrial basis are preliminary to the final section on the Call to Service. "The present day recoil from the misery of poverty," says our author, "is somewhat too superficial. Till it attains the deeper note we shall lack both energy and insight for true remedial action." 

That note is found in Service, but that Service, says Mr. Spencer, requires four things there must be Friendship—“we must rid ourselves of the tacit claim to stand on higher ground"; Personal Contact—“human links with the poor"; a Corporate Ideal—“ the modern poor have no protector but the community"; and Corporate Feeling-"the readjustment of sympathies to their new relationships." 

Ideals of Charity. By VIRGINIA M. CRAWFORD. 159pp. Sands 

&Co. 2s. 6d. net.

In the last number of "PROGRESS" a short account was given of some of the many forms of Catholic Social work in this country, and we gladly welcome a book (written for Catholic women workers) which should do much to direct it wisely. The time is ripe for it, for, as 

Mrs. Crawford says, "the days have long gone by when Catholics. felt themselves impelled to hold themselves aloof from the social and political life of England.” They are certainly not doing so now. Some of the keenest and most ardent Social workers of the present day are devout adherents of the Catholic Church, many of whom, we are told, are converts who saw little virtue in effecting their own self-effacement, or in deliberately abandoning fertile fields of Social labour." They recognise that while there are some subjects—such as education—in which it is sometimes impossible for them to unite with non-catholics, yet there are "many otherssuch as temperance, hygiene, housing and allotments-which can be regarded from a purely national or civic standpoint." (c Mrs. Crawford writes for women, and she writes as a Guardian who-like most women Guardians—has taken her duties seriously.

It is largely the experience she has gained as a Guardian, combined with a big fund of common sense, that enables her to write so wisely and helpfully for her sister workers. She pleads for "a methodical study of Social conditions," "a broader outlook on Social questions," and a readiness to render personal service, and not be satisfied, as too many are, with "indolent philanthropy."

It is to the subject of "methodical study" that she devotes her first chapter, under the heading "How, and where to train." The 

Schools to which she gives prominence for this training are, the

PROGRESS' 

School of Economics, the School of Sociology (which works in close co-operation with the C.O.S.), and the British Institute of Social 

Service. Though written for Catholics, and some of it on what concerns them specially, the great body of the book will be found equally serviceable for all. The chapters on "the need for Cooperation in Charity," "Should married women work," "Home work," and especially that racy analysis of the qualifications of the District Visitor, in Chap. V., should be widely read by all Social workers.

Schools of To-morrow: How the Children of the Future will be educated.-By MARGARET MCMILLAN. 

Steele, Stoke-on-Trent. 6d. 

24pp. J. P.

In these few pages Miss McMillan focuses the light she has thrown on educational problems in her former books, and presents us with a charming picture of an ideal school. Its realisation may not be yet, though she has a strong hope that it will be in time. 

And there is much that is tending towards it; it may now be only a vision, but only those who are ignorant of those tendencies will put it aside as altogether fantastic. There is nothing in it that may not and ought not to become real. 

school building :- 

We take this picture of the It is not an isolated place, though it stands alone. It has nerves, so to speak, reaching out over the city, and beyond it. Its head master is in touch with the children's surgery at the top of the street, and with the invalid school beyond the suburbs. Its playgrounds are not confined to the roof-garden, the plots of grass, the flower beds and the ground in the rear. They include the great municipal school gardens and also the free open spaces far away, where many a child has his own pets, and where every child plants and digs and even builds, gaining new experiences and making his own discoveries in peace. 

Then as to the children :- "There is no deafness, no mouthbreathing; eye diseases have disappeared; a case of skin disease is unusual; ringworm is a name; parasites are unknown; dirt has vanished. The children are not only, clean, they are physically cultured." What discipline there is is the "natural discipline involved in all real work." Fatigue is not unknown, but "the teachers have learnt to understand the fatigue symptoms, and they know where to stop." There is nothing here that may not be realised, and, indeed, there is much in Miss McMillan's picture which has even now become fact in the Open-Air School. 

The saying that "Education only develops the pre-existing," strikes the key-note of the book. Miss McMillan pleads for the removal of all obstructions, that the true child-nature may have full play, but this will be done not merely by improved methods, but by "an entirely different conception of what education means.”

National League for Physical Education and Improvement. 

ITS AIMS, ACTIVITIES, AND INTERESTS. 

Chairman of Executive Council- THE LORD BISHOP OF RIPON. 

NON 

VIVERE 

ES 

VALERE SED VITA MART. 

Chairman of London Branch- THE LORD CHIEF JUSTICE. 

OUR motto which appears on the monogram, recently adopted by our Council, is the fittest expression of our aims and activities. We aim not only to preserve life, but to make life more efficient; we strive, not only that our fellow-citizens may have life, but that they may have it " more abundantly." Ruskin, when writing on "the honour that there is in rearing children," makes this note: "Observe I say 'rearing,' not begetting.

The praise is not in σπоpητós (sowing), nor in pvraλiá (planting), but in οπώρα (harvesting). It is strange that men always praise enthusiastically any person who, by a momentary exertion, saves a life, but praise very hesitantly a person who, by exertion and selfdenial prolonged through years, creates one. We give the crown 'ob civem servatum'; why not 'ob civem natum'? Born, I mean, to the full, in soul as well as body. England has oak enough for both chaplets." Our motto is our endorsement of Ruskin's comment. 

WHERE HEALTH VISITING IS DONE. 

CO-OPERATING with the National League for Physical Education and Improvement, the 

B.I.S.S. has compiled a list of places where 

Health Visiting is done, and where the Notification of Births Act has been adopted. An inquiry was addressed to Medical Officers of Health in all places regarding which the Institute had any indication that it would be of use. Each of the 206 M.O.H. addressed received a copy of the、 list and was asked to supplement it, but the additions were few, and it is felt that the list is still far from complete. The Institute will be grateful to anyone who will add to it. The inquiry was begun on Aug. 15th, and on Sept. 30th 173 replies had been received.

These returns show eighty-four places with 167 Health Visitors paid entirely out of the rates; seven with eighteen Health Visitors

NATIONAL LEAGUE 

(including eleven in Salford) partly paid from rates; and twenty with seventy rate-paid Women Sanitary Inspectors, whose duties include the work of Health Visiting. Fifty-three report Voluntary 

Organisations, co-operating with Health Departments or working independently, with thirty-three paid workers (including nurses), and a much larger number of honorary helpers. In some places, notably Cambridge, Health Visiting by a paid staff is thoroughly organised by a Voluntary Organisation which entirely relieves the Health Department of this work. In Manchester the Corporation has taken over all the fourteen paid Visitors formerly employed by the Ladies' Public Health Society. In most cases the M.O.H. feels that it is too early to give definite information as to the effect of Health Visiting on Infant Mortality, but thirty-three have no hesitation in stating its beneficial results. The list shows that 132 places had adopted, or decided to adopt, the Notification of Births Act.*

OUR own efforts to reduce the rate of Infant Mortality are receiving valuable support from the mass of excellent literature on the subject which is now issuing from the Press. It is an indication that public attention has been aroused. The fact that thousands of children die every year from preventible causes is leading to widespread inquiry, and experts in all countries are rendering solid service by giving to the world the results of their researches. Full and reliable facts on

NATIONAL LEAGUE 

this all-important subject are now so easily obtainable that there is no excuse for public bodies delaying action.

One of the most valuable of recent publications bearing upon this question is the new and revised REPORT ON INFANT MORTALITY 

(174 pp.; 2s. 6d. net) prepared for the County Borough of Huddersfield by its able Medical Officer of Health, Dr. Moore. Huddersfield has acquired such a well-deserved reputation for its efforts to save the lives of the babies, that one is not surprised to see such an elaborate Report. This new edition contains an important chapter on "The 

Relative Values of different Measures against Infant Mortality," but a considerable part of it is given to answers to inquiries relating to the establishment of Day Nurseries and Crêches in this and other countries. The results of these inquiries are tabulated in a valuable series of appendices. The subject is one of considerable importance. 

There are so many objections to these institutions-even when conducted without reproach—that some of the most earnest combatants in this crusade against infant mortality only regard them as temporary expedients, the necessity for which ought soon to cease. 

For those who find it necessary to make provision of this sort for mothers obliged to go out to work, the information given in the 

Huddersfield Report will prove most useful.

THE REPORT OF THE PROCEEDINGS OF THE NATIONAL CONFER- ENCE ON INFANT MORTALITY, March, 1908 (200 pp., King, 1s. 6d. 

net), is another of the recent contributions to the literature on this subject. In addition to a weighty address by the President of the 

Local Government Board, in which he gave official figures with regard to the adoption of the Notification of Births Act, the decline of the infant death-rate in certain localities, and other subjects of much importance to all who would co-operate with us in our efforts towards physical improvement, papers were read by several persons whose names have become household words in connection with this campaign. Among these may be mentioned Mr. Ald. Broadbent on “Education for Motherhood"; Miss Alice Ravenhill on "The

Education of Girls and Women in the Functions and Duties of 

Womanhood"; M. Henri Coullet (Paris) and Miss Margaret Le 

F. Boileau on "The Best Means of Helping the Mother below the 

Poverty Line"; but we would call special attention to the suggestive paper by Dr. Saleeby on "The Human Mother." In that paper 

Dr. Saleeby points out, what is of the utmost importance to those who are dealing with this problem, that "the human mother has only instinctive love and uninstructed intelligence" that the

instinct which enables the sub-human mother—e.g., the cat—to rear her offspring does not exist in the human mother, but in place of that nature has endowed her with intelligence. It is this intelligence that needs instruction, and it is to this that all efforts to reduce infant mortality must be largely directed. 

Dr. Temme's little book, “DIE SOZIALE URSACHE DER SAUG- LINGSSTERBLICHKEIT " (Berlin, Buchverlag der Hilfe, Is.) is excellent. 

In a small compass, and in clear and concise language, it gives to German readers just those facts and figures which need to be known, and discusses the various causes of the present high death-rate among infants. Those causes he classifies under the following heads :—

Artificial feeding; impure milk; social customs; factory work of mothers; illegitimacy; home work; bad housing; and alcoholism. 

FIGHTING CONSUMPTION. 

WHEN speaking in Dublin at the opening of the Tuberculosis Exhibition, both Mr. Birrell and Prof. Osler referred to the apathy which still exists with regard to this disease, and of the urgent need to get rid of this apathy if tuberculosis is to be stamped out. Mr. Birrell pointed out that not many years ago there was the same apathy with regard to typhoid fever, an apathy which arose from a sort of belief that, being one of those things "that flesh is heir to," it must be endured. 

I can remember well (said Mr. Birrell) a half century ago, when I was a boy in 

Liverpool, that typhoid fever was considered as much a Divine institution as the prison, or the workhouse, or the gallows, or as Her Majesty's Judges of Assize, or any other of those things. We submitted to it--a most terrible plague it was. 

There was scarcely a family that it did not enter to claim a victim. It hardly occurred to us that the thing had no right to exist; that it should not have existed; that so far from its being an act of God or the will of God that such a scourge should not only visit, but perpetually abide in, the city, it was really due to laxity, apathy, and indifference on the part of the community.

The death-rate from typhoid declined when the spread of knowledge removed the apathy. The same result may be expected with regard to tuberculosis. The spread of this knowledge is one of the main objects of the National League; we therefore gladly call attention to the splendid work that is being carried on in Ireland by the Women's National Health Association. Dr. Tatham's carefully compiled statistics show that while in England there is a terrible death-roll from this disease, it is far worse in Ireland-23 per 1,000 as against 13.

The Tuberculosis Exhibition (of which a detailed account has already been given in "PROGRESS "*) is one instance of the spirit with which this campaign is being conducted. In addition 

See "PROGRESS," April, 1908, p. 113.

NATIONAL LEAGUE 

to its statistical and pathological exhibits, the committee arranged for a series of lectures which have now been published in the two volumes edited by Lady Aberdeen, and entitled "IRELAND'S CRUSADE AGAINST TUBERCULOSIS" (Maunsel& Co., Dublin, 2s.). The two volumes are full of information that will be of equal value to those who are seeking to combat this disease in other parts of the United Kingdom, but the address by Dr. Osler, the distinguished Oxford 

Professor, on "What the public can do in the fight against tuberculosis," is the one that perhaps will be found most generally useful. 

It puts in a crisp and concise form just those facts which should be known by all. The Professor begins by enumerating eight things which he says are now thoroughly known—(1) The germ; (2)

Whence it comes; (3) How it gets into the body; (4) What happens when it does get in; (5) How the ground is prepared for its growth; (6) How to recognise the disease;´ (7) How successfully to prevent it; (8) How to cure it; and, last but not least, "We know that it is not hereditary." In conclusion he pleads for “a campaign of unending activity through the Press, the pulpit, by private effort, by lectures, and pamphlets." This campaign will become more vigorous as the "eight things" now thoroughly known by experts become thoroughly known by the public. 

IS SPREAD. 

In the course of describing to the Conference 

HOW DISEASE of Sanitary Inspectors the large amount of reconstruction of defective dwellings which has been carried out in Liverpool, Sir James Crichton Browne called attention to a highly important point of detail. In his visits to the homes of the poor he had often been struck by the dilapidated state of many inner walls, and on making inquiry of an expert he was informed of great differences in the quality of the plaster employed. 

Plaster that will stand without cracks for a century can, he is told, be produced at a very moderate price; and, when finished with three coats of oil paint and varnished, makes an excellent sanitary lining for a house, and is capable of being washed down from time to time. 

On the other hand, inferior plaster, such as is too often employed, is not only given to cracking and crumbling, but is decidedly insanitary and a menace to health. It is of the nature of a sponge, which absorbs and gives off morbid effluvia; it necessitates the use of cheap wall papers, which cannot be washed down, and it harbours vermin, fleas and flies, and bugs, which may all, in certain circumstances, become carriers of disease.

OUR efforts to obtain a pure milk supply are 

CLEAN MILK. 

being admirably supported by one of our affiliated societies-the York Health and Housing Reform Association. 

An association which has for its Hon. Sec. Mr. Seebohm Rowntree may be relied upon for thoroughness in any investigations it undertakes, and the report it has just issued on "The Milk Supply of 

York" fully justifies that confidence. What has been done in York ought to be done in every town and city in the country, and this report furnishes an excellent model on which to work. 

After giving the results of a careful investigation into the present conditions of transport, of the cowsheds, and milk-shops, and the cleanliness or otherwise, both in person and habits, of the various persons employed in milking, the Report makes sundry practical suggestions for reform, prefacing them with the remark that "it is quite possible on practical lines, and at small expense to all concerned, to secure a thoroughly scientific control of the milk supply." Various reforms are suggested for the dairies in public control by making the inspection of cowsheds more thorough, and above all "that every milk-vendor in the city-whether living inside or outsideshould be required to obtain an annual licence to sell milk." A copy of the report may be had for threepence; it should be widely read and carefully studied.

*Report on the administration of the Labour Laws in the 

United Kingdom. 47PP. 

(British Association for Labour Legislation, 4, Bloomsbury Square, W.C.) 

A Report drawn up by the British Section of the International Association for Labour Legislation, with the object of securing the proper observance of such laws as exist in various countries. This Report, which deals only with the Labour Laws of the United Kingdom, gives detailed and valuable information concerning the numbers, constitution, and methods of the Inspectorial Staff, and the co-operation of the workers in the administration of the law. Also with regard to the general control of administration and suggested reforms.

Pamphlet No. 1; Price 6d. each, post free. "We in this country have until recently, in our usual happy-golucky fashion neglected the systematic teaching of physical exercises, except in the case of the services, and the report before us may well give our educational authorities material for consideration. A sense of discipline is the crying need of the day, and this the foreign system of physical education inculcates more generally than our own systems, or rather want of system."- 

The Lancet.

Advertisements 

The International Institute for Lectures and Reviews 

(Institut International pour la diffusion des experiences sociales).

General Secretary: DR. RODOLPHE BRODA. 

HAS recently been established in order to promote the exchange of 'social experiences between the different nations of the globe. To attain its aim the Institute shall dispose of the following means of action : (1) "PROGRESS," with which is incorporated "The International," is the English medium for the announcements of the Institute, while "Les Documents du Progrès” and "Dokumente des Fortschritts," are respectively its French and German organs. (2) The Institute issues monographs on problems of social reforms, and organises lectures both in England and other countries; especially addresses by leading authorities from abroad. (3) It has organised an Inquiry Bureau, which gives information respecting social Reform abroad, and International Inquiries and Congresses on concrete points of social reforms. 

The first International Inquiry will be on the subject of:

"THE ROLE OF VIOLENCE IN THE CONFLICTS OF OUR TIMES." 

The following questions will be addressed to about 1,000 prominent personalities of all nations of the earth :- (a) Can we consider violence (wars, revolutions, strikes) to be the best means to decide political and economic conflicts? (b) If so :-What are the advantages compensating the material damages resulting from the use of violence? (c) If not :-What forms of law or arbitration do you propose to replace these methods of violence?

The first Monography will treat of the following subject :— THE MODERN PROLETARIAT: A STUDY OF SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY. 

THE FIRST LECTURES delivered in London by foreign statesmen and writers will deal with: 

I. 

Social Insurance in Germany. 

2. 

The results of Moral Instruction in French Schools. 

3. 

Women's Suffrage in Finland and Norway. 

4. Municipal Socialism in Vienna. 

5. Co-operative Societies in France (27 Mai). 

Subscribers to the Institute residing in English-speaking countries will receive:

I. 

The review" PROGRESS." 

2. 

The "DOCUMENTS DU PROGRES 

99 

or "DOKUMENTE DES FORTSCHRITTS," 

according to their choice. 

3. All reports on International Inquiries, monographs, etc., which are published in 

English; and such as are not published in English, either in French or German, according to choice. 

4. They will be entitled to the use of the International Inquiry Bureau. 

5. They will have the right of attending all lectures and meetings arranged for by the 

Institute. 

The Subscription Fee to the Institute, entitling subscribers to all the above-named advantages will be ONE GUINEA PER ANNUM (10 shillings only if no other publications in foreign languages besides the "Documents du Progres" are required, and should be sent to the head office of the Institute, 59, Rue 

Claude-Bernard, Paris. 

List of some persons under whose patronage the Institute has been established : 

Rt. Hon. Thos. Lough, M.P., Philip Snowden, M.P., F. Maddison, M.P., Sidney Webb,

Prof. Patrick Geddes, E. R. Pease, W. T. Stead, Sir John Cockburn, Ramsay Mac- Donald, M.P. 

E. Pernerstorfor (Vice-President of the Parliament), Vienna; Prof. A. Vambéry, Budapest; Emil Vandervelde, Brussels; Paul Deschanel (Ancien Président de la Chàmbre des 

Députés), Paris; Baron d'Estournelles de Constant (Member of the Senate), Paris;

Anatole France, Paris; Prof. K. Lamprecht, Leipzig; Prof. Ostwald, Leipzig; Lino

Lastberg, Minister of Justice, Kristiania.

Advertisements Les Documents du Progrès and the 

(PARIS) Dokumente des Fortschritts (BERLIN). 

HESE two monthly Reviews edited by Dr. Rodolphe 

Broda, Paris, give each month a survey of the world's progress, and publish reports on social reforms of every kind and accomplished everywhere, written by their 200 correspondents in all parts of the globe. Readers of "PROGRESS" will often find short notes taken from these Reviews, with which a special agreement has been made for this purpose. To enable them to read the full articles in the original German and French languages, a special arrangement has been made, whereby readers of "PROGRESS shall enjoy a reduced subscription (8/- a year for the "Documents du Progrès," postage incl., and 10/- a year for the "Dokumente des Fortschritts," postage incl.). The subscription fee should be sent to the Head Office, 59, Rue

Advertisements British Institute of Social Service, 4, TAVISTOCK SQUARE, LONDON, W.C. 

Telegrams: SOCIALAYD. 

Office Hours: 

9.30 a.m. to 5 p.m.; except Saturdays, 9.30 a.m. to 1 p.m.

OBJECTS: 

To collect, register, and disseminate information relating to all forms of Social Service in order :- 

(1) To make such information available to all concerned in the improvement and elevation of our national life; (2) To promote the initiation and development in this country of the most beneficial and successful forms of Social Service; (3) To give assistance to all organisations that have social aims, and to facilitate co-operation between them; and, as far as possible to prevent overlapping.

SCOPE: 

Some idea of the forms of Social Service at home and abroad regarding which the Institute collects and disseminates the best experience may be gathered from the following list : 

Infant Mortality.-Prompt Registration of Births; Maternity Instruc tion; Milk Depôts, Crèches.

Child Life. Meals; Clothing; Country Holidays; Labour Restrictions; 

Juvenile Courts; Medical Inspection; Visiting Invalid Children. 

Education. Physical, Hygienic, Domestic; Trade Schools; Apprentice ship Committees; Special, Country Recovery, and Vacation Schools;

Play Centres; Arts and Crafts; Libraries, Reading Circles, Experimental Fee-charging Schools. 

Labour Conditions.-Trade Unions; Dangerous Trades; Safety Appliances; Women's Work; Sweating. 

Unemployment.--Labour Bureaux; Labour Colonies; Vacant Lot 

Cultivation; Emigration.

Industrial Betterment.-Sanitary Conditions; Factory Dressing Rooms; 

Dining Rooms, Reading and Recreation Rooms; Social Secretaries; 

Profit Sharing; Pension Systems; Thrift Societies; Sick Funds; 

Savings Banks.

Social Centres.--Settlements; Institutional Churches; Clubs and Brigades; Social Institutes. 

Temperance. Attractions to compete with Public Houses; Dis interested Management; Public House Trusts. 

Co-operation. -Credit Banks; Loan Societies; Benefit Societies;

Building, Farming, Manufacturing, and Distributing; Houskeeping. 

Municipal Enterprises.-Experiences in Public Ownership. 

Public Health.-Sanitation; Milk Inspection; Smoke Abatement: 

Health Visiting, etc. 

Land. --Allotments; Small Holdings; Rural Depopulation; Village 

Handicrafts.

Advertisements 

V 

Housing.-Town Planning; Overcrowding; Statistics; Experiences, etc. 

Garden Cities.-Garden Villages and Suburbs; Urban Aesthetics; 

Provision of Parks; Window Gardening, etc. 

Poor Relief. -Vagrancy; Elberfeld System; Guilds of Help; Old Age

Pensions; State Insurance; Co-ordination of Voluntary Effort. 

Criminology. - Probationary Methods; Borstal System; Physical Development; Prison Experiments. 

Publication.-"PROGRESS: Civic, Social, Industrial," the 

Institute's quarterly magazine, is published in the middle of 

January, April, July and October, price Sixpence. It is intended to appeal to all who are interested in social service and the betterment of life and labour conditions, to stimulate readers to carry their inquiries beyond the limited extent to which they can be dealt with in its pages, and to contribute the results of their own social experiences, and to distribute information as to betterment movements and experiments gathered in the Library of the Institute from originating points in all civilised countries. One of its most valuable features is a list of the previous quarter's publications on social topics. 

Membership in the Institute may be obtained by sending a subscription of not less than one guinea per annum to the Secretary, but it is hoped that those interested in the Institute will remember that this is the minimum subscription and that those who can afford to do so will make contributions in much larger amounts. Membership in the Institute is of three kinds, classified as follows:- 

(a) LIFE MEMBERS, being the Founders of the Society, and all who shall at any time have contributed not less than £250 in one or more sums to the

Institute. 

(b) SUBSCRIBING MEMBERS, who shall be those who shall subscribe not less than one guinea per annum to the Institute. 

(c) HONORARY MEMBERS, who shall be those per sons who are co-opted by thc Executive Committee.

The efficient working of the Institute can only be maintained either by a substantial endowment such as is enjoyed by the Musee Social of Paris, and the Humanitarian Society of Milan; or, as in America, where a sum of £3,000 a year has been guaranteed by wealthy and philanthropic individuals or firms desiring to promote social effort.

By L. COPE CORNFORD. Demy 8vo. Cloth 6s. net. 

Daily Chronicle:-"As the title would suggest, the volume deals with various aspects of London life. Some of the sketches have appeared in the newspapers, some are quite fresh. Some paint Black London, some 

Bright London, but always it is London. Well, is there any better chessboard than London on which to study humanity as a whole?” 

INFANTILE MORTALITY. 

It con 

Statistical Analysis of Infant Mortality and Its Causes in the United Kingdom. By HELEN M. BLAGG. Demy 8vo. IS. net. 

Nottingham Guardian:-"A most careful and valuable paper. tains a quantity of facts and figures of the utmost value for reference, and all bringing home the author's conclusions that thousands of healthy babies are yearly done to death by preventible diseases and unnecessarily evil surroundings, while thousands more become mentally and physically deteriorated because they have never had a chance to live a healthy life, and a certain number are born unfit to live because of the evils amid which their mothers live."

The Case for the Guardians. By SIR WILLIAM CHANCE, BART. Demy 8vo.

Spectator:-"Sir William Chance does a public service by taking the field on behalf of moderation and commonsense in the matter of Poor Law reform." 

PHILANTHROPY AND THE STATE : 

Or Social Politics. By B. KIRKMAN GRAY. Edited by ELEANOR KIRKMAN GRAY and B. L. HUTCHINS. Demy 8vo. Cloth, 7s. 6d. net. 

The change from philanthropy to social politics has been great, and the present volume seeks to illustrate the process. There has been some development in thought, but as yet we have no consistent doctrine of the relation of the State to the weaker classes of society (social pathology), and the work of assistance to these classes suffers in consequence. In the following pages illustrations are given of the bad practical results of this lack of a clear and consistent theory of society. 

A HISTORY of ENGLISH PHILANTHROPY. 

By B. KIRKMAN GRAY. Demy 8vo. Cloth, 7s. 6d. net. 

Economic Journal.—“ There is no study more full of 'human interest' than that of the many forms taken by philanthropy, including, as it must, both the needs of those who are to benefit and the motives of those who are to give. From this point of view, the work before us will repay the readers by the wealth of curious information it brings together. The spirit of the book throughout is one of genuine sympathy with the difficulties of the poor.
