A sense of discipline is the crying need of the day, and this the foreign system of physical education inculcates more generally than our own systems, or rather want of system."- 

The Lancet.

Advertisements 

The International Institute for Lectures and Reviews 

(Institut International pour la diffusion des experiences sociales).

General Secretary: DR. RODOLPHE BRODA. 

HAS recently been established in order to promote the exchange of 'social experiences between the different nations of the globe. To attain its aim the Institute shall dispose of the following means of action : (1) "PROGRESS," with which is incorporated "The International," is the English medium for the announcements of the Institute, while "Les Documents du Progrès” and "Dokumente des Fortschritts," are respectively its French and German organs. (2) The Institute issues monographs on problems of social reforms, and organises lectures both in England and other countries; especially addresses by leading authorities from abroad. (3) It has organised an Inquiry Bureau, which gives information respecting social Reform abroad, and International Inquiries and Congresses on concrete points of social reforms. 

The first International Inquiry will be on the subject of :

"THE ROLE OF VIOLENCE IN THE CONFLICTS OF OUR TIMES." 

The following questions will be addressed to about 1,000 prominent personalities of all nations of the earth :- (a) Can we consider violence (wars, revolutions, strikes) to be the best means to decide political and economic conflicts? (b) If so :-What are the advantages compensating the material damages resulting from the use of violence? (c) If not :-What forms of law or arbitration do you propose to replace these methods of violence?

The first Monography will treat of the following subject :- 

:- 

THE MODERN PROLETARIAT: A STUDY OF SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY. 

THE FIRST LECTURES delivered in London by foreign statesmen and writers will deal with:

I. 

Social Insurance in Germany. 

2. 3. 

The results of Moral Instruction in French Schools.

Women's Suffrage in Finland and Norway. 

4. Municipal Socialism in Vienna. 

5. Co-operative Societies in France (27 Mai).

Subscribers to the Institute residing in English-speaking countries will receive: I. 

The review" PROGRESS." 

2. 3. 99 66 

The "DOCUMENTS DU PROGRES or DOKUMENTE DES FORTSCHRITTS," 

according to their choice.

All reports on International Inquiries, monographs, etc., which are published in 

English; and such as are not published in English, either in French or German, according to choice. 

4. They will be entitled to the use of the International Inquiry Bureau. 

5. They will have the right of attending all lectures and meetings arranged for by the 

Institute. 

The Subscription Fee to the Institute, entitling subscribers to all the above-named advantages will be ONE GUINEA PER ANNUM (10 shillings only if no other publications in foreign languages besides the "Documents du Progres" are required, and should be sent to the head office of the Institute, 59, Rue 

Claude-Bernard, Paris.

HESE two monthly Reviews edited by Dr. Rodolphe

Publication.-"PROGRESS: Civic, Social, Industrial," the 

Institute's quarterly magazine, is published in the middle of 

January, April, July and October, price Sixpence. It is intended to appeal to all who are interested in social service and the betterment of life and labour conditions, to stimulate readers to carry their inquiries beyond the limited extent to which they can be dealt with in its pages, and to contribute the results of their own social experiences, and to distribute information as to betterment movements and experiments gathered in the Library of the Institute from originating points in all civilised countries. One of its most valuable features is a list of the previous quarter's publications on social topics. 

Membership in the Institute may be obtained by sending a subscription of not less than one guinea per annum to the Secretary, but it is hoped that those interested in the Institute will remember that this is the minimum subscription and that those who can afford to do so will make contributions in much larger amounts. Membership in the Institute is of three kinds, classified as follows:- (a) LIFE MEMBERS, being the Founders of the Society, and all who shall at any time have contributed not less than £250 in one or more sums to the 

Institute. 

(b) SUBSCRIBING MEMBERS, who shall be those who shall subscribe not less than one guinea per annum to the Institute. 

(c) HONORARY MEMBERS, who shall be those per sons who are co-opted by the Executive Committee.

The efficient working of the Institute can only be maintained either by a substantial endowment such as is enjoyed by the Musee Social of Paris, and the Humanitarian Society of Milan; or, as in America, where a sum of £3,000 a year has been guaranteed by wealthy and philanthropic individuals or firms desiring to promote social effort.

PROGRESS Civic-Social-Industrial. 

APRIL, 1910. 

QUARTERLY. 

The Problem of the 

Feebleminded.

BY MARY DENDY. 

ANYONE who wishes to study this problem intelligently must first learn from a variety of persons: from the medical expert as to what weakness of intellect is; from the teacher how to discover and train whatever mental faculty the sufferer may possess; from the Poor Law guardian and magistrate and inspector of inebriate homes as to the terrible evils which arise from leaving such sufferers unprotected; from the professor of eugenics as to the deterioration of the race which must follow from the union of defectives; from the lawyer as to the means of protection which we can already command; and then we need a wise and far-seeing philanthropist to devise means for carrying into effect the knowledge thus gained, and a skilful organiser to show us how the ideas of the philanthropist may be carried out without undue cost to the community. Too often people begin at the wrong end; they will be philanthropists and they fail utterly in their aims because they have not the patience to begin at the beginning and pick the brains of those who are wiser than themselves. 

The beginning is not easy, for we have to say, first of all,

PROGRESS 

what is feebleness of mind? I think it is generally admitted that it is, in most cases, a milder degree of the same defect, or combination of defects, that produces idiocy. But what is idiocy? Dr. Ashby says: "The main cause of feebleness of mind is an inheritance of nervous instability." This inheritance may account for the majority of the cases, but there are still very many which occur without any such cause being ascertainable. Mr. Amos Butler says: "Occasionally the children of normal parents are feeble-minded." In fact, there are freak children.

Whatever the cause, its victims can be treated by the same agencies, and, with due regard for individuality, in the same institutions—but they can not, with propriety, be treated together with imbeciles or idiots, nor with pronounced epileptics. Though the dividing line be difficult to draw in theory, it is in practice easy to distinguish the feeble-minded child from these types on the one hand and from the merely dull and backward child on the other. The idiot can not wash and dress itself; often can not feed itself or sit up; indeed, I have seen cases in which there was no evidence of any mind or instinct. The imbecile is better than the idiot, but still too helpless to be classed with the feeble-minded child. There is nothing to be done with the idiot by training; there is not much to be done with the imbecile, but there is a great deal that can be done with the feeble-minded child. 

On the other hand, the merely dull and backward child is free from the eccentricities of demeanour and weakness of will-power which mark the children we are now considering. 

A child who is low in his standards at school from neglect or ill-health may show very considerable common sense and understand how to guide himself in the emergencies of his daily life.

The main point to be remembered is that whatever the cause of weakness of intellect, it will, unless it be the result of injury to the individual and therefore an acquired characteristic, tend to be handed on to the offspring of the sufferer. 

Mr. Butler gives us some appalling figures bearing upon this question of inheritance, and asks most pertinently: "Is there

THE PROBLEM OF THE FEEBLE-MINDED 85 

anything else which reproduces itself so surely?" And the next point to bear in mind is that it is an incurable trouble. 

It is because of this fact that it is hereditary and incurable that it is the most pressing of all the social problems of our time. 

Moreover, because of its nature, it is the only one which, in my opinion, justifies us in overruling the responsibility of the parent for his child. Indeed, in too many instances there is no such responsibility. The father is often very little, if any, better than his child.

It is a problem which lies at the root of all others, and until we deal with it in a rational way there is little hope, indeed, of bettering the general condition of our people. Almost all the evils which occupy the attention of sociologists are brought about very largely by weakness of intellect. Among these I would name infant mortality, child neglect and underfeeding, drink, consumption, purposeless crime.

The question of Infant mortality is one that is being considered from many points of view, but I do not think we have yet reached all its causes. I am sure it is due, in great measure, to the mental deterioration of individual families-not to the deterioration of the race as a whole. I do not think such deterioration exists. With regard to Child neglect and underfeeding. There is here an unfortunate tendency to put the cart before the horse. People have been anxious to prove that want of food will make a defective child out of a normal one. As to this, Sir Clifford Allbutt writes me:- "That defective children can be manufactured by physical deterioration acting upon normal minds is ridiculous; no such cases can be produced. If a slightly feeble child be made worse by bad conditions, recovery under normal conditions to such capacity as he was born with will ensue. By breeding only is this variety of man continued." 

So when we come to the question of Drink. For generations people have been trying to kill out this evil habit of excess; every bad feature of our lives has been put down to it and now, when a great deal of attention is being paid to disease of mind, or perhaps I should rather say lack of mind. in our children, there is a jealous desire on the part of some

PROGRESS 

total abstainers to make out that this also is a result of the use of intoxicating liquor. There could not be a greater mistake, and it is a very serious mistake, for we shall not help people in a trouble they have by treating them for one which they have not. Drink is most often a result and not a cause of feebleness of mind. This is not now a question of opinion, but of ascertained fact. Dr. M. Branthwaite, H.M.I. of 

Inebriate Homes, tells us that 62 per cent. of all the cases committed to these homes are insane or mentally defective, a great majority coming under the latter heading.

With regard to Consumption. It is argued that consumption in a family, by lowering the vitality of the stock, acts upon the germ cell and causes it so to deteriorate that in the succeeding generation it develops into a mentally defective individual. There is no evidence whatever that consumption causes mental defect; on the contrary, many consumptives are particularly bright and clever. What there is evidence of is this: Where there is deterioration of the family stock it may show itself in various ways; one member of the family may take to drink, another may be consumptive, and a third may be weak in intellect, and this last will be the one who is most likely to have children and pass his defect on. his defect on. With regard to Crimes of violence, theft, and so forth, the reason why the weakminded are specially prone to commit them is not far to seek. 

These poor souls do not, in in any real sense, possess themselves,

They have not the will to protect themselves either from the evils of the outer world or from the temptations of their own animal passions. They obey the next impulses, whether they be from within or without, and they would generally be put to it to explain why they have done anything that is wrong.

Think for a moment what all this means and let us remember that from the point of view of the mischief done to the race, it is much the same whether the case be that of a hooligan on the streets or a marquis spending an enormous fortune on neckties and jewellery. These people are the failures of life, and they are a dead weight upon the successes. 

But it is not only that they are themselves failures; they are

THE PROBLEM OF THE FEEBLE-MINDED 87 

almost all of them at large, and at liberty to take upon themselves the duties and responsibilities of parenthood, and it is certain that they hand on their incapacity to some at least of their descendants, often in an aggravated form. Nor does it make against my argument that some of the children of a weak-minded man may not themselves be weak-minded; they are of a degenerate stock; the trouble may be latent in them, but it will crop out again and the world will be the worse for their existence.

Whatever alternative terms we may employ for this terrible affliction, however we may try to account for it, one thing is sure it is hereditary.

It is this feature of the question which makes it a public duty to take charge of the feeble-minded from the very beginning in such a way that they cannot harm themselves or other people. It is the duty which we owe to posterity, for we have interfered with the laws of natural selection in this matter and are responsible.

Archball Reid, in a book which deserves to be more widely known than it is, says: 

Until very recently, in the vast majority of cases, the unfortunate lunatic was placed under circumstances which ensured death or permanent insanity. From the very moment that his mental unsoundness declared itself, he ceased to have offspring. The natural selection of the same was therefore very stringent. For some generations, however, lunatics have been treated with very great humanity and skill. 

Beyond all classes of the community they are watched over by the State. 

The insane are removed to special sanatoria, where, without expense to themselves, they receive food and lodging, and are placed under the care of trained nurses and medical specialists, over whom, in turn, the Commissioners in Lunacy exercise a jealous supervision. As a consequence, the lunatic frequently recovers and is restored to his family and the right to have as many children as he pleases. 

What Dr. Reid says of the lunatic applies with even more force to the feeble-minded, for in his case the right to be a father has never been denied.

It is curious, by the way, that while the proportion of males to females of this class is three to two, nearly all efforts have been directed to caring for the women. Yet the man is at least as dangerous as the woman, and very nearly as suffering.

PROGRESS 

We see the children of the feeble-minded woman. Who counts the children of the feeble-minded man? The fact is that for generations past we have chosen to play the part of Providence to our weaklings, but if we will play Providence we must be wise as well as pitiful and work with the laws of nature and not against them. We must remember that if the ne'er-do-wells of society have their rights, so also have the well-doers.

Deliberately to make it more easy for degenerates to survive without securing that they shall survive in circumstances which render them harmless is a piece of folly which approaches nigh to wickedness. Yet, as the law now stands, we in England have no power to interfere with the feebleminded over the age of 16 until they have brought themselves under notice as criminals or paupers. Even then the notice taken of them is regulated not by their condition, but their crime.

For the purposes of English legislation the feeble-minded child comes into existence at the age of 5 years and ceases to exist at the age of 16. We have not yet got away from the idea that weakness of intellect is a curable disease which can be set right by differential treatment of the child affected. It is because of this fallacy that we meet with so many statements from the managers of special schools that the children under their care have been returned in a normal condition to ordinary school or have taken up the work of life like ordinary people. Many children are, at a great expense of time, money, and trouble, taught a certain amount of parrot-learning and are made to appear, for a time at least, like a low grade of normal children. All this learning is of no use to them, and makes them a greater danger to society than they would have been without it. The facts put together by the Birmingham After-Care Committee, which has done its work with more accuracy than any other, show that in the most favourable conditions, with all the help that can be given, short of permanent detention, the very best of the feeble-minded are employed only irregularly and that the great majority are unemployed or criminals. To my mind it is

THE PROBLEM OF THE FEEBLE-MINDED 89 

a great mistake to make employment the test as to whether a man or woman is or is not fit to be at liberty. No training can cure the feeble-minded child.

So we come to our special schools. I think no one will deny that they are a step in the right direction. To leave the feeble-minded in ordinary schools is a great waste of them and of their teachers. It is a great waste of money also. 

Can anything be more stupid than to pay a teacher for ten years to teach a boy that which it is impossible that he should learn? We do not struggle to teach our blind to see; we try to substitute the sense of touch for that of sight. The only rational plan with our feeble-minded, our blind in mind, is to teach them useful habits of imitation in lieu of the habits of memory and reason which serve the ordinary mind. 

It is because this has to a great extent been forgotten that our really splendid special schools have achieved so little lasting success. There is still far too much in them of teaching children to read and write and cypher, when, however much they may learn of the three R's, it is of no use to them in after-life. It seems to be a great achievement to teach a child to say its tables; it is nevertheless a pity to spend time upon it when the same child cannot take away three straws from four.

What is needed is that, since it takes so much time and trouble to teach the feeble-minded anything, all they are taught should be of value to them in their after-lives. In some of the best schools this is recognised, and the teachers give their whole hearts to training the children into decent habits and useful ways of employing themselves. I do not think that after infancy mere kindergarten work is proper occupation for weak-minded children. It is of no use to a boy to know how to weave paper or to wind wool into a ball. 

Nevertheless, the trained kindergartner makes the best special school teacher; she will know how to apply her science so as to enable her children to acquire useful finger habits. A boy who can black his own boots and dress himself neatly and knit his own stockings, besides making baskets which are serviceable and caning chairs, has really benefited from his schooling. But

PROGRESS 

all this preliminary training should be only a part of life-long care.

It is hard on our fine special school teachers to see the boys and girls whom they have trained so lovingly and patiently, drifting away, and in a few months after leaving school, scarcely better than when they entered it. Their work is largely wasted because there are no institutions to take it up. where they leave it off.

It has been supposed that the special school was to cure the mental disorder from which the children are suffering. For the same reason, the efforts of our idiot asylums are largely thrown away. Wonderful results are obtained during the seven years, which is the longest period of detention (with few exceptions) given to the inmates of these asylums. Very generally, the committees do not now admit idiots, and the feeble-minded children who come in are capable of making a much better show after they have been polished for a few years than the idiots and imbeciles who were originally admitted. But these excellent results are seen only just so long as the conditions which produced them exist. 

The Government has before it three Royal Commissions' Reports on the Poor Law, on Inebriety, and on Feebleness of Mind. It would be premature to predict what will be the outcome of the deliberations on these reports, but I should like to dwell for a moment upon what the law at present allows us to do for the weak-minded who are not certifiable under the Lunacy laws. 

Education Authorities can take action under the Elementary Education and Epileptic Children Act of 1899. It is a permissive Act, and has not been by any means universally adopted. Especially in the counties, it is still hardly put in operation at all. A number of towns have adopted it, but still the great majority do not put it in force. 

While it is not compulsory upon Education Committees to provide schools for weak-minded children, it is compulsory upon parents to avail themselves of such schools when they are provided and if they are directed to do so by the education authority responsible. This leads to a great deal of difficulty.

THE PROBLEM OF THE FEEBLE-MINDED 91 

Children can be taken in hand at 5 years of age. They can be legally compelled to attend school until they are 16 years of age. It is noteworthy that the London Asylums Board, which had availed itself of special schools for pauper children coming under its notice, took power to extend the age of detention from 16 to 20 years. After the age of 16 the law does not actively help us, but we gain something from the fact that a boy over the age of 14 and a girl over the age of 16 have power to choose their own place of residence and cannot therefore be removed against their will from an institution. On the other hand, neither can they be forcibly detained unless they be Poor Law cases. Boards of Guardians have greater powers than Education Committees, and can adopt any child and put it in a place of safety up to the age of 18; apparently they can also go on paying maintenance charges for such children. indefinitely.

What we need is a law which shall make early recognition of the cases compulsory instead of permissive, and which shall secure that care shall not stop with its life at the day school, but shall persist as long as it lives. Residential and permanent schools should be provided and children should be admitted to them at the age to be determined in every case by the medical man in charge. It is necessary to seclude some children at a much earlier age than others.

The welfare of the nation depends upon whether or no rational action be taken in this matter, and rational action must be in accordance with the laws of science. But this thing is sure that which is in accordance with the laws of science is in accordance with the laws of God, and a serious responsibility indeed rests upon those who are told and who may know but do not choose to know, or knowing, do not choose to act.

The Majority Report of the Poor Law Commission. 

By F. H. BENTHAM. 

ALTHOUGH there are two Reports and several dissentients, it is important to note that upon a great many of these recommendations, involving great changes in the present system, there is substantial agreement between all the members of the Commission, and that not one of them favoured the status quo. It may, therefore, be taken as the unanimous opinion of the Commission that the Poor Law system needs recasting in order to adapt it to the requirements of the present day. 

It is when we come to consider the methods of assistance, and the necessary changes in machinery which are involved, that differences of opinion are revealed. 

Both Reports strongly emphasise the necessity for greater facilities for specialised treatment. Both agree that the present areas are too small for the purpose, and that the administrative county and county boroughs are the most suitable. But when we come to the authority which shall be set up we come to one of the most vital of all the points upon which there is disagreement. The Majority suggest that a Statutory Committee of the County and County Borough Councils should be set up and entrusted with the whole duties of public assistance within their respective areas. The Committee is to be constituted of not more than one-half its members from the council and the other half of non-members experienced in Poor Law administration or cognate work. The whole of the members have to be appointed by the council, and to retire one-third in each year. When constituted, it becomes the 

Public Assistance Authority. In county areas there are to be local committees to deal with applications, and the local areas are to be the present union areas. Adjustments in boundaries will be necessary where they overlap counties. The authority will be ad hoc, but not directly elected. By this method of 1

constituting the authority there will be a much closer bond of unity between Public Assistance and other Local Authorities, with consequent greater harmony and less overlapping. There is at the present time a tendency for these local bodies to trench upon duties which naturally belong to existing Poor Law Authorities, and for both to work upon their own lines regardless of each other. By the proposed method of constituting the authority there will be a much greater likelihood of a more clearly defined line of action being agreed upon than could possibly be obtained between directly elected authorities. The most serious criticism of this scheme is that a non-elected body is to be entrusted with the expenditure of public funds. The Public Assistance Authority will, how ever, have no power to lay a rate or to collect one. They will have no borrowing powers. Their plans for new buildings will be subject to sanction by the Local Government Board. 

Members, on the expiration of their term of office (three years), will not be re-appointed unless they have the confidence of the County or County Borough Council. These bodies will be very careful whom they appoint to administer their funds, even, I venture to say, much more careful than the electors are now. They will have a larger number of suitable persons to draw from than the electors have now from candidates offering themselves for election. It cannot be said now, except in rare instances, that the electorate take a deep interest in the election of guardians. Rarely more than a third of the electorate take the trouble to exercise their franchise. The alternative seems to be that of placing the power in the hands of County and County Borough Councils to appoint the Public Assistance Authority from its own body or otherwise at its discretion. This method would be open to the same objection which the Majority take to the Minority scheme. There is abundance of evidence to show that it would be fatal to good administration. Those bodies have already as much work as they can efficiently perform, and the more work there is put upon them the less likelihood is there of getting capable men to undertake the duties. The principle of the Majority scheme would, however, not be

PROGRESS 

sacrificed if a bare majority were appointed from the members of the County or County Borough Councils. 

The Minority scheme does not place implicit trust in directly elected representative committees. We read :- 

It is impossible to expect to get either uniformity or impartiality in decisions on successive cases if these decisions are arrived at without automatic check or guidance by such a many-headed tribunal of such mutable membership as is presented by a representative committee. 

Merely to transfer the work of the Boards of Guardians to a single Poor Law Committee of the Town Council would therefore, in this respect, produce no sufficient reform. Nor could outdoor relief be withdrawn from their jurisdiction (p. 1016).

It is only by means of several committees dealing with the same case that they get the "check," and, since it is impracticable to allow each to grant "home aliment," a judicial officer -called the Registrar of Public Assistance—is introduced, not only to give the "guidance," but to exercise an absolute veto regarding it. In this way the Minority propose to get over the difficulty of taking the power of discretion, as between "home aliment" and other forms of assistance, out of the hands of directly elected representatives. The allpowerful Registrar of Public Assistance is preferred to a committee of elected representatives. The Minority propose the merging of Public Assistance into other branches of public service. The Majority are doubtful "whether the work of maintaining those members of the community who have lost their economic independence can be safely entrusted to authorities whose primary duty is something quite distinct -such as that of education or sanitation or whether it is not essential that there should be an authority devoting itself entirely to the work." The economic independence of the individual, as an ideal to be aimed at, is not emphasised in the Minority Report; the economic condition of a person is held to be of no importance as a factor in deciding when a person is entitled to public assistance. We read :- 

The necessary conditions may or may not co-exist with the presence of material resources-a consideration which may or may not affect the pecuniary charge to be made in return for the services of the Public Authority, but not the rendering of the services themselves. . . . These authorities must, in order to prevent injury to the community, take action whether or not there are material resources (p. 1,019.)

The specific form of services to be rendered under such circumstances is not quite clear, but the condition of persons entitled to such services is designated as "personal destitution," and this is explained as being a "condition which, without the intervention of the Public Authority, would produce consequences inimical to the common weal." In actual practice this would be a very difficult line to draw. It would be more a matter of opinion than a matter of fact. The widest possible variation in practice could obtain. Every ailment, however trivial, every want short of the "common standard of economic necessity" (which the Registrar has to keep in mind) may be considered inimical to the common weal in one locality, while in another the practice would resolve itself into one of personal application only. The action which is now taken by Poor Law Authorities under the Lunacy Acts in the case of dangerous lunatics is cited as a case in point. As a lunatic is dealt with in the interest, not only of himself but of the "common weal" regardless of "material resources," so should all other classes of persons be dealt with on the same principle. The fact is apparently overlooked that the lunatic is an irresponsible person, and a principle which may be applied with advantage in regard to such person is not necessarily the wisest to apply to persons who are fully responsible. 

Again, some local sanitary authorities are very backward in adopting even elementary precautions in the prevention of disease; it would be courting disaster to place further duties upon them.

Assistance from public funds may be given either in the interest of the individual, the community, or of both, and it would appear to be the wisest course to take these matters into consideration in administering public assistance. To the receiver it is entirely an individual matter. The assistance afforded is accepted for his exclusive benefit, in which no one else shares, and his "condition" which entitles him to assistance is very largely in his own hands. But the fact that he is able to purchase for himself all that he requires for the maintenance of his physical fitness is not to be regarded as a reason for withholding assistance from public funds. This is a matter

PROGRESS 

for after consideration by the Registrar of Public Assistance, who alone has the power to order repayment for services rendered. Although the Minority lays no emphasis on the economic aspect of public assistance, it cannot quite ignore it. 

It says:- 

It would never do to let all the several Committees be granting home aliment without a common standard of economic necessity and due regard for the possible effect in subsidising wages. 

Is it not rather curious to advocate a line being drawn at "economic necessity" when it is a question of home aliment -which means money payment-while on any other kind of assistance—which is money's worth—“ economic necessity" is held to be no bar to assistance?

After distributing the Poor Law services over the respective committees of the County and County Borough Councils it is found necessary to co-ordinate them in order to make the scheme workable. After breaking a plate to pieces an attempt is made to cement it together again in order to make it serviceable. However well this might be done, it can never be made quite so serviceable as before. As each committee has its own staff for searching out cases and investigating circumstances, one family may often be under consideration by several committees at the same time. It is therefore necessary to have a centre round which the whole system can revolve, and to which all the particulars of every case must be sent. This Central Bureau is to be under the direction of the "Registrar of Public Assistance." He will have his staff of investigators and clerks to make inquiries and to keep records. 

His duties will be multifarious. He has to decide all cases of home aliment, to allocate stray and marginal cases, to assign all cases in the receiving houses to suitable institutions, to keep records of every person receiving public assistance of any kind, to order recovery of cost of relief, and to act as pension officer.

The safeguard against abuse is the Registrar of Public 

Assistance, who may be regarded as a "bogey" to frighten respectable but timid people from accepting proffered and unsolicited assistance. I can well imagine the brazen, idle-

but otherwise inoffensive-individual always being just on the verge of existence, below which he cannot be expected, or made, to pay, so arranging his affairs as to get all the comforts he needs with a minimum of exertion. It may be said to be a policy of supplying all that is needed for a healthy existence in return for such payment as can be enforced. Is such a policy of public assistance expedient or practicable? Again, if we insist upon giving a person that which we think he needs but which he is unwilling though able to purchase for himself, should we be able to make him repay the cost? The removal of infectious cases to isolation hospitals in the interests of the community renders it impracticable to recover the cost of treatment. What the community insists upon doing in the interests of the common weal can scarcely be charged to the individual. On p. 1019 it is stated that the level of existence, below which a person cannot be called upon to repay, would be "at whatever level of means Parliament may decide." Would not this in effect be establishing a minimum standard of living below which level every person would have the shortage provided out of the rates in the interests not particularly of the individual but of the "common weal"? The Majority hold the view, rightly or wrongly, that it is in the interests of the community as a whole that there should be as few dependents upon public funds as possible consistent with a minimum of necessitous persons. 

It therefore follows that whatever methods of assistance be adopted they should be designed with the object of restoring the recipients to a position of independence. They should be open to the smallest possible risk of weakening self-reliance and increasing dependence. It is urged by the Minority that as the Majority do not propose to extend the title to assistance, so whatever methods of assistance be adopted and whatever changes are made in the machinery it will bring about no improvement. The limitation of assistance to persons who are already destitute, it is held, will effectually limit its usefulness, and indeed the whole conception of a Poor Law or Public Assistance Authority is wrong. Now the word "destitution" seems to be used in a double sense. By some it is understood to mean the condition

PROGRESS 

of a person who has exhausted all his means—that is, until he has realised all his property and effects he is not destitute. 

The following definition of the word "destitution" by Mr. 

Adrian, the Legal Adviser to the Local Government Board, shows that this is entirely a wrong interpretation. 

Destitution when used to describe a person as a subject for relief implies that he is for the time being without material resources (1) directly available and (2) appropriate for satisfying his physical needs (a) whether actually existing or (b) likely to arise immediately. By physical needs in this definition are meant such physical needs as must be satisfied, (1) in order to maintain life, or (2) in order to obviate, mitigate, or remove causes endangering life or likely to endanger life or impair health or bodily fitness for self-support. 

The Majority accept this definition as being wide enough for all purposes of public assistance, but suggest that "necessitous" is a more appropriate word. The Minority, on the other hand, say: "We are disinclined to go as far in the provision of public assistance as is involved in Mr. Adrian's words," since it might involve a large expenditure in specialised treatment such as "an expensive surgical operation," "mechanical appliances," and "maintenance in convalescence." Yet they propose to give powers to County and 

County Borough Councils to provide by way of treatment whatsoever they wish. Their objection is, therefore, not against such services being rendered by Public Authority, but by the "Public Assistance Authority." It should be understood that Mr. Adrian's definition holds good at the present time and Boards of Guardians have, therefore, the power to which the Minority object. In the same way, under the 

Majority scheme, it would be possible to provide appropriate methods of treatment for every "necessitous" person, and this is as wide a power as the Minority claim for their scheme. 

There is, however, one important difference. In the former the economic condition of the person assisted is ascertained on treatment being ordered, while in the latter it is not. 

Under the Minority scheme the cases referred to the Health 

Committees of the County and County Borough Councils form a very large proportion of the total number to be dealt with. At the present time, however, County Councils are not

Public Health Authorities, and it would therefore be necessary to confer public health duties upon them. The Minority suggest that non-county boroughs with less than 10,000 population, and urban districts with less than 20,000, and all rural districts, have "neither the means nor the staff that are requisite for the performance of the duties assigned by them under the Public Health Acts." These districts, numbering no less than 1,532, would be abolished as Public Health Authorities and their duties transferred to the County Council. 

Administrative difficulties are at once apparent since no health. committee of a large county can deal locally with all these classes which are assigned to it, namely, the sick and permanently incapacitated, the infants under school age, and the aged. Every individual case would therefore be dealt with by the peripatetic Registrar of Public Assistance and his staff, or by the official of the Health Committee of the County Council. Again, in non-county boroughs with 10,000 population and urban districts with 20,000 population, which number 199 Public Health Authorities, medical assistance would be dealt with locally, whilst all those needing institutional care would come under the County Health Committee. 

Under the Majority scheme the present union areas for local administration would be retained and a local committee appointed for dealing with all cases needing public assistance whether medical or otherwise, institutions for all cases being provided by the County Public Assistance Authority. 

The Majority suggest the establishment of Voluntary Aid Councils in counties and county boroughs, and Voluntary Aid Committees in local areas, in order to secure the co-operation of all voluntary agencies; they would encourage charitable work by means of grants, where necessary, for organisation. purposes, but not for actual relief, believing that by this means much more preventive work might be done than is possible under present conditions. On the other hand, the Minority place no importance upon the growth of charities, merely suggesting that use should be made of charitable institutions which already exist. They take this position:

PROGRESS 

We are definitely of opinion that no encouragement whatever should be given to any distribution of money, food or clothing in the homes of the poor by any private persons or charitable societies whatsoever. The only exception to this rule should be a regular pension to a particular person, and this ought in all cases to be notified to the Registrar of 

Public Assistance. 

It would appear, therefore, that even persons needing temporary assistance should not come under the care of charities. 

Their work would therefore be confined to the provision of institutions. They suggest the formation of Health Visitors and Children's Care Committees to search out cases needing public assistance and to keep them under observation both before and after treatment has been afforded from the rates. 

The whole scheme of the Minority seems to stand in the same relationship to human wants as a main drainage scheme to the disposal of human refuse. Every ailment is to be considered as a public nuisance for which a public remedy has to be provided, while an attempt (which is sure to fail) is to be made to recover the cost from those able to pay. Can this mean anything less, in practice, than a system of free medical relief, and indeed of everything necessary to maintain physical efficiency to all who fall below a suggested national minimum? It is indeed difficult to see how, under this scheme, any organisation which has for its object the improvement of the condition of the people, such as Friendly and Mutual Benefit Societies, etc., can possibly survive, whilst a contributory scheme of insurance either for sickness, invalidity, or unemployment would be practically impossible. Whilst on the one hand the 

Majority scheme is designed to provide assistance to necessitous persons in such a way as to foster the instincts of industry and self-provision and to fit individuals to stand on their own feet, the Minority scheme is designed to provide methods of assistance with the object of continuously keeping them off their feet. The crutches will never be removed so long as the individual prefers them to standing alone. The Majority suggest that all assistance provided by public funds for individual needs should be frankly recognised as public assistance, and that it should be administered by one public authority.

Voluntary Agencies and Public Assistance. 

BY THE EDITOR. 

THE large and enthusiastic meeting which was held at the Mansion House for the formation of a Social Welfare Association for London; the influentially-signed letter which appeared in the papers some time previously calling for a League of Personal Service; the establishment of numerous 

Guilds of Help, Civic Leagues, Citizens' and Public Wel fare Associations, and other movements of a similar kind, all point to an awakening of the civic conscience with regard to personal responsibility for the care of the poor. There are strong forces behind all these voluntary agencies which need. to be mobilised-they are the "territorials" of our armybut when mobilised they ought to be given an important and responsible place in any wise scheme of public assistance. 

There is, however, a feeling in the minds of not a few—who have given earnest consideration to the two Reports presented by the Poor Law Commissioners-that no such place is found for these agencies in either of their schemes. This feeling has been expressed to me more than once by letter and in conversation, and it has also found expression in published criticisms. 

Those who share this feeling-and with them I associate myself-have a rooted conviction that we shall never abolish destitution or uplift the most abject by a system of officials however well that system is administered, and therefore they contend that no system will be satisfactory unless voluntary agencies are accorded a larger and more responsible position. 

Since those Reports appeared the Rev. Russell Wakefield 

-now Dean of Norwich-addressed the Bradford Guild of 

Help on "The Sphere of Voluntary Agencies under the 

Minority Report."* In the course of that address he indicated very clearly the place that, in his opinion, voluntary * Now published in pamphlet form by the National Committee, 5, Clement's Inn, W.C. Id.

PROGRESS 

agencies on the lines of existing Guilds of Help should take in any scheme of Poor-law reform and the functions that should be assigned to them. How far his statements meet the objections referred to I will inquire later, but first of all let us try to understand on what points, and in what way, the Reports of the Commissioners have left in the minds of many a feeling of unsatisfied desire. 

This feeling has been expressed in two thoughtful articles on "The Organisation of Public and Voluntary Relief,” contributed by Mrs. Moser, to Help, the organ of the Bradford 

Guild of Help. To all Guild of Help workers, and to a wide circle outside, Mrs. Moser is known as one of the wisest counsellors in all that concerns that movement. Her close study of these Guilds, and her own active participation in the working of that at Bradford, have convinced her that in any satisfactory scheme of public assistance voluntary agencies must be assigned "a definite part of the civic responsibility for the condition of those in need of assistance." She does not feel that this is secured by either of the proposed schemes. 

Mrs. Moser regards the proposal of the Majority to establish Voluntary Aid Councils and Committees co-ordinate with that of the public relief authority but with separate functions as utterly inadequate : 

The methods proposed in the Majority Report for co-ordinating and using voiuntary agencies and workers will not, in my opinion, have the results anticipated. To separate the functions of the public and voluntary bodies by leaving one class of persons to one authority and another class to the other, will be fatal. The causes of distress are so complex that it is frequently only by the co-operation of both that anything can be achieved. Then also the demoralising effect of charitable relief, administered, not in proportion to the need, but in relation to the supply of funds, will be perpetuated, and we shall continue to have necessary work crippled for want of funds, and wasteful lavishness where money happens to be available.

The proposals of the Minority seem more hopeful, but they too fail to satisfy, for while they "gently recommend co-ordination of voluntary and public relief work, they give it no proper opportunity." Mrs. Moser recognises "many and great advantages in having special committees dealing with each member of a family according to its particular

VOLUNTARY AGENCIES 

103 need," but she sees "a very real danger in having no one that realises the family as a whole." In the following passage she indicates what she considers its main defects: 

Whilst recognising the need" for the personal service of both men and women of good will," it is left entirely to each local authority to decide whether any effective use will be made of such services, and whether the conditions under which such services will be accepted will be such as to attract the best men and women workers. . . . The scathing condemnation by the Minority Report of the alms dispensed by well-meaning persons in the mere relief of distress is none too strong, and yet I fail to see how we are to abolish this form of human sympathy unless we provide another outlet by having voluntary service as a recognised part of our civic work. 

as It is this same feeling of unsatisfied desire which seems to me to lie at the back of the objections raised by Professor Muirhead to this part of the Minority Report in his postscript to the second edition of " By What Authority?" After referring to "the impulse of voluntary helpfulness ' something "which we cannot afford to get rid of," he points out that while the Minority Report "cannot be said to be blind to the importance of organising these voluntary agencies," it makes no satisfactory proposals for doing it. 

What, I think, it fails to recognise is the particular strength of the movement towards the corporate and responsible organisation of voluntary agencies and the necessity of approaching the whole subject not from the side of the least responsibility that, as things are, is consistent with the existence of voluntaryism at all, but from the side of the utmost that it is possible to give, consistently with authoritative local control both of ends and means.

In the body of his book the Professor had stated his opinion that in their attitude towards voluntary aid the 

Minority had missed a vital point.

It is not that it denies the necessity of surrounding certain classes with the atmosphere of love that voluntary workers can alone supply, or that it fails to recognise the value of private enterprise in setting on foot new efforts or improving upon the methods of old ones, but that for the main work of Public Assistance it has a rooted distrust of amateur philanthropy. Volunteers, in its view, may serve well enough as camp followers, in rare and exceptional cases as a flying column of scouts and reconnoitrers, but they are wholly out of place in the main body of the fighting force.

What these friendly criticisms seem to point to is this: that no scheme of Poor-law reform which does not provide

PROGRESS 

for the mobilisation of the volunteers; the care of the family as a whole; and the preservation of a sense of communal responsibility for the care of its poor, will fully meet the essential requirement, and this can only be done through a central organisation, somewhat on the model of our Guilds of Help and other kindred societies, but freed from all responsibility as regards the giving of material aid. 

This was evidently in the mind of Mrs. Moser when she urged the need for an organisation that can "take a definite part of the civic responsibility for the care of those in need of assistance," and Professor Muirhead, when referring to "the setting up of one authority," pointed to the Birmingham City Aid Society as providing the analogy on which it should be framed, adding later on a prophesy as to what will happen if this be not done.

Supposing that the Minority's proposals were carried out, the first thing that efficient administrators would seek to do would be to organise some approved mode of consultation and co-operation between the members of the separate committees which are dealing with the needs of particular families, and herewith you have the nucleus of an authority of precisely the kind that I have tried to show will be necessary for the effective working of my system. You may expel the co-operative spirit with a fork, it will return whenever the real nature of the situation makes itself felt.

The same line was taken by Dr. Paton in the evidence he presented to the Royal Commission. In that evidence he elaborated a carefully thought-out "scheme by which the methods of the Elberfeld system of poor relief may be modified and applied to England,"* and he expressed a strong hope that "the promised reform of our Poor Law will ensure in some effective way the adoption of that humane and, I may say, perfect system." He thus sums up his reasons for that recommendation: 

It is recognised by all who have studied the problems of poverty that it is only by individual and personal care that the poor can be wisely dealt with, that appropriate and needful sympathy can be given to the old and sick, and that strong stimulus and patient effort can be applied to restore those who have become idle and listless. By the sub-division of labour as in the Elberfeld system-where each Helper has charge of only a small district for which he is responsible and is thus able to become 

For fuller details of Dr. Paton's scheme see "PROGRESS," Vol. III., pp. 254-61.

VOLUNTARY AGENCIES 

105 acquainted with the condition of all the poor in it-new conditions are created in dealing with poverty, and we may be quite certain that equally good results will follow in English towns as have been seen in Elberfeld and elsewhere.

Now when we turn to the Reports of the Majority and Minority we do not find in either of them any definite provision for placing within "the main fighting force" local bodies of voluntary workers corresponding to our Guilds of Help, but with the municipal authority that is accorded them in Germany. It is true that the Majority propose to establish 

Voluntary Aid Councils and Committees, but these would only deal with a part of the cases needing assistance, and would, moreover (as Mrs. Moser points out), possess the "fatal objection of separating the functions of public and voluntary bodies." The Report of the Minority, as it stands, fails also to meet the requirements of the case. It recognises the value of certain forms of voluntary service- 

Health Visitors, Children's Care Committees, etc.—but these are separate entities, and the Report leaves us entirely in the dark as regards any provision for their co-ordination.

On all these points, which by many are considered vital, both Reports fail to satisfy. How far are they satisfied by the Dean of Norwich in the address referred to above? The 

Dean speaks as one of the signatories of the Minority Report and therefore presumably he expresses the opinions of his colleagues. In that address he defines what the Minority meant when claiming for their scheme that "it affords the opportunity for initiating a really systematic use of voluntary agency in order to give to the public assistance that touch of friendly sympathy which may be more helpful than mere maintenance at the public expense." This might mean much or little; its indefiniteness left much to be desired. We now have the interpretation. The Dean tells us plainly that by the "systematic use of voluntary agency was meant the organisation of voluntary workers, with municipal status, on the lines of the Elberfeld system, as now interpreted by our 

Guilds of Help. He commends as the great attraction of that system "the division of the whole town among volunteer workers, with a Helper to so many families, a captain over so 

---

PROGRESS 

many Helpers, the municipal administration of the town standing behind and above the captains, and helping the poor through them." After eulogising the Guilds of Help, he expresses his regret that at present they cannot gain municipal status. As, however, he distinctly states that this is an "essential feature," we may fairly assume that its possession is presupposed when he states that "in every Borough or County there ought to be a body of the nature of a Guild of Help uniting the different departments of the public authority and all the charitable agencies, by means of an organised staff of personal volunteers, into one great army warring against destitution." In defining in detail the various functions of these Guilds he gives the following as the most important: 

To organise and maintain in numbers and efficiency a staff of friends, to each of whom a street or a certain number of houses should be assigned; and each of whom should have a duty, not to visit regularly, but just to make the acquaintance of every family in his section, whether in need or not, and to drop in as a friendly visitor from time to time, not to give pecuniary help, but in order (a) to supply the support of a helpful personality in case of trouble; (b) to give advice and counsel whenever applied to; (c) to ensure that no case of need or suffering escapes notifica. tion; (d) to report every case that he hears of, in its most incipient stage, to the proper authority, and especially to the volunteer Health Visitors or to the Children's Care Committee for the locality; and (e) to bring to the notice of the Guild's own. organisation any special cases not coming within the sphere of any public authority's treatment, or requiring something more than or something different from anything that the public authority can supply.

It is one of the inevitable defects of the official machine that it cannot always be adapted to meet exceptional cases. The right treatment in one case might be a dangerous precedent for others, and so the individual may have sometimes to be sacrificed, if nothing but the official machine be available. But the Guild may often, in respect of extraordinary cases, be able to find ways of doing what the public authority cannot, or dare not do; and the experience thus obtained may subsequently be of great value in the development and extension of the public services on safe lines. "All this," says the Dean, “is in the spirit of the Minority Report scheme." If this be so, and I have good authority for assuming that it is, it seems to supply the lacking desiderata. It provides "a corporate and responsible organisation of voluntary agencies "; it gives to these agencies recognised part of our civic work," and it assigns them at 

---

VOLUNTARY AGENCIES 

107 distinct place “in the main body of the fighting force." It does more than that. By the adoption of the Elberfeld plan of dividing and subdividing the whole town into districts and sub-districts, and placing the responsibility of oversight upon an organised band of voluntary workers-"a staff of friends" -to each of whom is entrusted "the care of a street or a certain number of houses," it provides, in each case, a friend who can not only "realise the family as a whole," but who, having but few cases to deal with, will be able to individualise. 

Moreover, this scheme of voluntary service is a distinct recognition of the fact that "destitution can never be abolished, or the most abject uplifted," by an "official machine," however perfect in itself.

But there is another, and perhaps an even more important, service that can be rendered by voluntary agencies thus organised. In any scheme of voluntary assistance which aims at dealing effectively with every case of need that may arise —and the community will be satisfied with nothing short of this-there is no little danger of sapping that spirit of selfreliance which is the real strength of a people. Professor 

Muirhead points to this: 

There is the danger of fostering in a certain class of individuals a spirit of reliance upon what the community will do for them under certain circumstances rather than upon what they can do for themselves by their own enterprise and perseverance. No one who has any practical experience of social work among the class referred to can have failed to be impressed by the fatal, often the pathetic, facility with which it learns to lean on every proffered support. 

This danger is a real one, but need must be met and the danger must be faced; and in no way can it be faced more successfully than by voluntary agencies exercising the functions assigned to them by the Dean of Norwich. Freed from all responsibility for rendering material aid, they would then become purely moral, redemptive agencies; imparting hope to the despairing, self-reliance to the indolent, and seeking by "the free intercourse of neighbourliness" to give that “touch of friendly sympathy which may be more helpful than mere maintenance at the public expense."

Chips from Social Workshops. 

OUR OWN WORKSHOP. 

IT IS with great reluctance that we again refer to our urgent need for increased financial support, but the need has now become so pressing that unless our appeal meets with a prompt and generous response we shall be immediately obliged to seriously consider the possibility of continuing our work. This would mean a very serious loss to a very large number of social workers. During the 5 years of our life we have replied gratuitously to more than 4,500 inquiries which have come to us from private persons, the heads of industrial concerns, government and local authorities in all parts of the world; we have collected a Library containing more than 20,000 books, pamphlets and periodicals, and about the same number of newspaper cuttings all relating to Social Service and all so tabulated and arranged as to be available for every inquirer. This Library is a national asset, the value of which can scarcely be overestimated, and if it were lost there is nothing to take its place. In addition to this we have distributed nearly 60,000 copies of "PROGRESS," "every number of which," says Prof. M.

E. Sadler, "is full of interest and of practical suggestion to social workers."

Every day is proving to those of us who are carrying on this work the urgent need for such a Central Bureau of Information. Inquiries are received in ever-growing numbers; in 1906 we had only 

472, last year they were 1,673. Students, journalists and social workers are learning that time can be saved by applying to the Institute for information or references on topics which are of public importance, and many who have long been engaged in some form of social service are learning that their efforts must be co-related with the endeavours of those engaged in other fields if the best results are to be obtained.

In the first number of "PROGRESS" Lord Lytton wrote as follows: 

When the British Institute of Social Service was founded a step was taken which may have most important consequences in the future. Every day some new society springs into existence, and the congestion of vital organisations is becoming bewildering. The remedy for this state of things must be found in some central organising body whose business it is to survey the whole field of Social Service, and to concentrate the available forces in the most profitable directions. It is here that the 

Institute of Social Service will find its most useful work.

How far we have been able to realise our aims must be left to the testimony of others. Two years after Lord Lytton wrote, the 

Bishop of Ripon, addressing the National Conference of Guilds of 

Help, referred as follows to "the need for such an organisation as the British Institute and its bearing on some of the difficulties of modern life":- 

Efforts have been made in different directions to secure some orderly method in the general benevolence of the country, but the British Institute of Social Service seems to me not only to have brought forward many of the ideas that have been current, but to have brought them forward in a larger and more practical form. The Institute seeks to cover all conceivable organisations which exist for good in any locality, and by combining the natural impulses of charity and kindliness of heart with a readiness for social service to bring the whole on to one common basis of energy. I know that we live in days in which certain aspects of religious controversy are certainly more keen than they were of old; therefore I welcome movements such as this British Institute of Social Service, because it is putting before the world two great ideas: one is the duty of service, the other is the necessity of co-operation in service. 

Unless the great philanthropic impulses of our own nation, or of any nation, are brought into conjunction with intelligence and wisdom and observation, we are very likely to arrive at a shipwreck of our philanthropic impulses, and at the same time the large failure of our administrative charities.

Twelve months later the Rt. Hon. Herbert Samuel, M.P. (the present Postmaster-General), bore testimony to the value and importance of our work from his personal knowledge of it : 

Ever since the foundation of the British Institute I have taken a close interest in its proceedings. Quarter by quarter I have read its excellent publication "PROGRESS" with great profit. This Institute exists in order to make our social machine work more smoothly. Often a pennyworth of oil in the bearings of a machine is of more value than a shillingsworth of coal in the furnace. This Institute is the pennyworth of oil which helps the various parts, the cogs and the pistons, to run easily, and is just as valuable as introducing more force into the machinery itself. It is not a competitor with any existing organisation. 

It supplements other societies and does not try to oust any. I trust that it will receive a continually growing and generous support, as I believe it plays a quite indispensable part in the machinery of social reform.

Hitherto every service we have rendered, has been rendered gratuitously and we are most anxious to continue to do so. Had we charged merely five shillings for each of the 1,673 inquiries answered during last year alone, our present financial difficulty would not have existed. This, however, we did not feel able to do, since very often those most needing our help were least able to pay

IIO PROGRESS 

for it; we therefore appeal to those who have, to help those who have not. 

Since our last issue we have changed our Headquarters. Last month we removed from 11, Southampton Row to 4, Tavistock 

Square. Our new quarters are not more than ten minutes' walk nearer Euston Road, and though not quite so central, are more commodious, and our removal will effect a considerable saving of expense. The heavy cost of removal, furnishing, cleaning, etc. (amounting to fully £170), is the only cloud on our satisfaction at having effected this saving. This is a very serious drain upon our resources at a time when we can ill afford it, but as this one present expenditure of £170 means a saving of £180 a year in the future, the Committee felt that no other course was open to them. 

We have now beautiful rooms, and we hope that our readers will visit us from time to time in order to see what we are doing. 

Inquiries during the first three months of the present year numbered only 346, as compared with 486 in the corresponding period of 1909, but this reduction is easily accounted for by the general election. We are now receiving a larger number than ever before.

Industrial Betterment 20 18 7 Advanced Methods of Education. We beg to remind our readers that we have established a Bureau, from which the latest information can be obtained regarding any higher Secondary Schools which have adopted advanced methods. It was started to meet the complaint that ordinary agencies are more concerned with examination successes or outward equipment than with liberal education

III 

as an end in itself, and consequently parents desiring such education for their children are often at a loss where to inquire. As the 

Bureau is in no sense a commercial undertaking, it never recommends particular schools, but merely furnishes addresses when required to answer inquiries. The Bureau is under the charge of 

MRS. USSHER, 20, Glenmore Road, Hampstead, N.W., who will gladly furnish information to anyone who will write to her concerning their difficulties.

A CARE COMMITTEE 

GUILD. 

IF success depended merely on numbers that of the Care Committees established in connection with the London County Council 

Elementary Schools* is already assured. The 

Dean of Norwich, in his address to the Bradford Guild of Help, stated that 6,000 volunteers had already been enrolled in this service, and added, “the people are simply clamouring to be allowed to serve on them." But all who have had to do with bands of amateur, inexperienced visitors are keenly alive to the fact that something more than numbers is necessary to success. It is so with these committees, and to bring them up to a standard of efficiency this Guild has been formed. A circular issued by its Executive 

Committee draws attention to the fact that "Care Committees are on their trial. Much of their work is ineffective and slipshod. They are handicapped in many ways. New responsibilities are constantly devolving upon them, while adequate means for carrying these out are not yet organised."

The objects aimed at are thus stated :— (1) To encourage and assist Care Committees to perform their work thoroughly, especially that which is concerned with medical inspection and treatment, school feeding, and finding suitable employment for boys and girls on leaving school. (2) To further the extension of the Council's work for the physical welfare of children, especially an adequate provision of school baths, school clinics, holiday colonies, and openair schools.

In pursuance of these objects the Guild will arrange conferences at which members of these committees can compare notes on their work and on methods or difficulties, and also discuss new plans and suggestions. It will also investigate matters affecting school children, and make known the results to its members. Membership is open to any in sympathy with these objects on payment of a minimum fee of Is. per annum. HON. SEC., Miss Maud F. Davies, 23, Hogarth Road, Earl's Court, S.W.
