PROGRESS THE CO-OPERATIVE 

DR. JOHNSON tells us that "the man who lays out his own time is sure to have some tedious hours;" the Co-operative Holiday

HOLIDAY Association, by laying hold of one's leisure 

ASSOCIATION. 

reduces tedium to a minus quantity.

The movement began in a small way. 

Mr. Leonard-now its

General Secretary-was then a minister in Colne, and noticing how many profitless holidays were spent by the Lancashire folk, he organised long week-end excursions, first to the lakes and then 

NEWLANDS VALE GUEST HOUS By courtesy of the Co-operative Holiday Association. 

further afield. In this way many health-giving, enjoyable holidays were spent under intelligent leadership and with pleasant companions. 

Dr. Paton with his keen scent for a good thing-soon saw its larger possibilities and through his influence the Co-operative Holiday Association was called into being. It has now ten centres, or Guest 

Houses, in the United Kingdom and three on the Continent, at which, last year, 12,479 guests were received (7,145 in England, 2,626 in Scotland, 446 in Wales, 617 in Ireland, and 1,645 abroad).

Like the old monks who built our Abbeys Mr. Leonard has a keen eye for nature's beauties, and he has selected some of the most beautiful

spots in these countries to locate his Centres. When to beautiful scenery and bracing air there is added delightful companionship, a Bohemian life, a good table and moderate charges, there is no wonder that thousands of holiday seekers every year avail themselves of such unique advantages.

One of the best features of the movement-and one which shows its thoroughly healthy tone-is the way in which the domestic service problem has been solved : 

Our "Domestic Helpers" are sensible and educated women who undertake to do the ordinary drudgery of the house on the understanding that they have fixed hours of leisure and service, a fair wage, and-most important of all-are enabled to share in all the 

By courtesy of the Co-operative Holiday Association. 

A CO-OPERATIVE HOLIDAY ASSOCIATION LUNCH PARTY. 

pleasures and advantages of the Guest House whilst they are off duty. This frank acceptance of all that is involved in the theory of the dignity of labour has never been regretted, and the pleasant feeling of friendliness never fails to strike a new comer to one of our 

Centres. 

And the Co-operative Holiday Association is equally particular about the choice of its guests. It caters only for the robust and for those who are prepared to rough it when occasion calls; not for the hypochrondriac or molly coddle. Those who cannot enjoy long days in the open, walking, hill-climbing, and such like active exercise, or who expect the conventionalities of the ordinary boarding house are advised to go elsewhere. It is essentially a democratic movement; there must be no "airs," no cliques, no "side"; plain living, simple dress, and brotherly intercourse is the rule in each of

PROGRESS 

these Homes.

But there is no levelling down, it is all the other way.

The whole movement has a distinctly-one may almost say an aggressively-educational side. Naturalists, and enthusiasts on many sides of open-air topics, accompany all excursions and give wayside talks on the botany, geology, and archæology of the district. 

It has also (writes Mr. Leonard) its ethical side:- 

Our Association has a definite ethical mission to fulfil, namely, the cultivation of character, and we seek to achieve this at a season when men and women are most susceptible to better influences. Almost every grade of society is represented in our Guest 

Houses, and amid all differences of creed, of political opinion, and social status we discover how much there is in which we are agreed as we tramp together, sit together at the same table, learn the same lessons of tolerance and goodwill, and gather together at the feet of Mother Nature to listen to her teachings. Comradeship, simplicity, reverence, may perhaps be regarded as the watchwords of our movement. 

By courtesy of the Co-operative Eoliday Association. 

ARDENCONNEL AN AUTUMN YIEW. 

It was in one of these Guest Houses that the Summer School for the Study of Social Questions has held its delightful meetings during the last five years. A company has been formed and trustees appointed to purchase, lease, and furnish the necessary buildings, but the Co-operative Holiday Association is in no sense a commercial undertaking. 

Dividends are limited to 5 per cent., and surplus profits are *This year the Summer School meets at the Guest House at Hebden, near Skipton, from June 18th to 24th. The general subject for discussion will be "The Land and the 

Housing Questions in their relation to Social Problems." Messrs. T. C. Horsfall, R. C. Aldridge, Montague Fordham, F. Marquis, Prof. Adshead, Rev. J. F. B. Tinling,

Mrs. Higgs, Miss Churton, and the Editor of "PROGRESS," have already been secured as speakers.

distributed in grants to the N.H.R.U., Footpaths Preservation Society, and that for Preserving Places of Great Natural Beauty, etc. 

But not content with providing for those who can afford to meet its moderate charges, the Co-operative Holiday Association has a watchful eye for those who cannot. Certain weeks are set aside every year for free guests. These are respectable, hard working men and women who rarely, if ever, enjoy a holiday, and yet who most need this "lift by the way" to enable them to continue the fight. They are invited for a week at a time on the nomination of the ordinary holiday guests, and they are treated exactly the same as those who pay the full chargesindeed better—for all lay themselves out to make their stay as full of mirth and enjoyment as the time and place afford. Last year there were 395 thus invited; the expense being defrayed by collections every Sunday throughout the year from the ordinary guests. 

Would that space permitted to describe these delightful gatherings and to give extracts from the grateful letters received. Between seasons nominated persons needing the rest but unable to pay in full are received at the nominal charge of 6s. a week (the ordinary charge averages 28s.). They can stay a fortnight or longer if necessary for health.

FIGHTING CONSUMPTION. 

THE assurance of victory is inspiring the com batants. The fact, now recognised, that consumption is a preventible disease, and that this scourge can be stamped out as effectually as small-pox, is rapidly swelling the ranks of the crusaders. Fresh efforts are constantly being put forward, an increasing number of which are by public authorities, though many are still dependent on private enterprise. 

Among the former we have already noticed the spirited action of the Birmingham City Council* in the erection of a Sanatorium at a cost of £25,000. A more recent effort is the provision of 

A Shelter for Consumptives by the County Borough of Rother ham, acting on the advice of Dr. Alfred Robinson, its Medical 

Officer. Though on a much less pretentious scale than that in 

Birmingham, it is, in its way, equally important as showing at how small a cost a municipality may do a really effective work. The total cost of the structure and equipment was only £150; this small cost being made possible by placing the shelter in the grounds of the Isolation Hospital, a plan by which further economy was effected in 

Vide." PROGRESS," Vol. IV., p. 50.

By courtesy of the Rotherham Health Committee. 

THE ROTHERHAM MUNICIPAL SANATORIUM,

the administrative expenses. might seem to be an objection, but Dr. Robinson assures us that the objection is more sentimental than real. Not only is the Shelter several hundred yards distant, but it is a well-known fact that consumptive patients rarely if ever take an infectious disease. 

Proximity to an Isolation Hospital 

It will be seen from the illustration that the Shelter is always open-Dr. Robinson having found that revolving shutters are unsatisfactory. It has sleeping accommodation for twelve patients, but as the average stay of each is only eighty-six days provision can be made for as many as fifty during the year. The weekly cost per patient is only 15s., which includes maintenance, nursing, medical attendance, miscellaneous expenses, and capital charges. The diet is fully up to the standard required by the most up-to-date treatment, and consists of three substantial meals daily of fresh meat, bacon, eggs, etc., with four pints of milk served at intervals from 6.30 a.m. to 8 p.m.

Patients sent by the Guardians are paid for; for the others all charges, both of maintenance and treatment, are defrayed by the Corporation. 

The experiment has not been tried long enough to yield any definite results as to permanent effects, but so far they have far exceeded expectation; in every case (except one wrongly diagnosed) the weight has materially increased (in one case as much as 36lbs.) and the physical condition improved. The atmospheric conditions under which the experiment has been tried are not the most favourable, but as these are the conditions under which the patients will live when they return to their homes it has been thought better to accept the inevitable and at the same time to put them in the way of resisting the ill effects of their environment when they leave. The example set by Rotherham is excellent, and it will be watched with considerable interest. Public authorities, fearing the cost, have too often neglected to take action early enough. Patients who might have been saved by early treatment, have been driven when too late to seek refuge as paupers in the workhouse infirmary. The cost to the ratepayers has been ultimately greater, and with far less satisfactory results. "My endeavour," writes Dr. Robinson, "has been to get hold of cases before they become paupers." 

A Sanatorium for the Treatment of Consumptives has just been provided at Bury St. Edmunds out of the profits from the pageant recently held in the town. The Hospital has two large wards-one for each sex-with doctor's room, dining hall, etc. The wards are fronted with verandahs to which the beds can be transferred. 

It stands 175 feet above sea level.

PROGRESS OPEN-AIR SCHOOL, SHEFFIELD. 

THE Sheffield Education Authority has come into line with the more advanced educationists in recognising the value of the openair school. The School has only had a short account of it given in School Hygiene for last month is specially instructive, as showing how much can be accomplished for a comparatively small cost. experience, but the

The School was opened last June for 50 children and remained open, for 5 days in each week, till October 29th. The situation is described as ideal-"surrounded by wooded meadow land and close 

By courtesy of the Sheffield Education Authority. 

OPEN-AIR SCHOOL CHILDREN IN THEIR GARDEN. 

to the Derbyshire moors." The distance from the centre of the city is about five miles, and the children were conveyed to and fro every day by tram and waggonettes. They arrive 9.20 a.m. and return at 5.45 p.m. With slight modifications the school life and arrangements were the same as those described more than once in former numbers of "PROGRESS." * As in Bradford, three meals only were served, but these were good and substantial. ridge and milk, always served on arrival, was at first distasteful to many of the children, but it became so popular that the mothers The por- 

* See especially an article on "Open Air Schools," by Dr. Frederick Rose, Vol. III., p. 87; also Vol. III., p. 278 (Bradford and Halifax).

asked for the recipe as their children clamoured for it at home. The cost of all three meals worked out at about 2/6 a day, towards which the parents contributed as much as they were able. Formal instruction was limited, so that as much time as possible could be given to lessons in the open air. There was of course the two hours' rest on deck chairs and meals in open sheds. A special room has been provided for medical inspection, also a bath-room fitted with four hot and cold shower baths-each child taking two warm shower baths every week. In addition to the school doctor, two nurses have been engaged to assist in taking weights and measurements and also to visit the homes and impress upon the mothers the great importance of sending their children early to bed, keeping their bedroom windows open and, if possible, allowing them to sleep on Saturday and Sunday afternoons when they were not at school. 

Every endeavour was made to impress upon both parents and children the extreme importance of scrupulous cleanliness. When at school each child was made to cleanse both teeth and hands before each meal. 

It is too early to judge of permanent results, but so far they are reported as very satisfactory notwithstanding the unfavourable weather of last year. In the case of thirty-five of the children there was a marked increase in the chest measurement, while, as to weight (taken fortnightly), there was an average gain during the four months of 2.7 kilos. (5.9 lbs.) as compared with an average gain of 1.1 kilos. (2.4 lbs.) in the ordinary school. As to general appearance and character the report states that children at first dull and listless, in a few days became bright and lively, and all showed a striking improvement in conduct during lessons and meals. 

All this has been accomplished at a net cost to the Sheffield ratepayers of £288. The gross amount expended was £366, of which £70 came from Government grants and £18 from parents' repayments. 

SETTLEMENT WORK IN VIENNA. 

same lines.

THERE is in Vienna an institution which has taken our English word "Settlement " describe its activities. In many respects it is doing much the same work, and on the Yet it is not a Settlement in the full sense of the word as understood here. Its objects are very similar; it aims at the physical, moral, and spiritual uplift of the people in one of the poorest parts of the city, and seeks to accomplish this by the close and constant personal contact of cultured men and women

PROGRESS 

with those they would help. Its work is largely with the children, and especially with the neglected, beginning with the Kindergarten for those under school age, and doing for the school children much of the work of our London Care Committees-provision of meals, clothing, etc., and general care for their welfare. It has also its 

Recreation Evening School, organised play, and holiday colonies 

(Ferien Kolonien). Nor does it neglect the older ones. For these it provides, in the Institute, a library and reading-room, concerts and lantern lectures, the "poor man's lawyer," a labour bureau, and a girls' club; while outside, in the often wretched, overcrowded homes in its neighbourhood it carries on all the kindly ministrations of our Health Visitors. 

That which differentiates it from the Settlement, as we know it, is the fact that at present it has not been found practicable to supply that residence of the workers which with us is essential. 

That this is a weakness is fully acknowledged by its band of intelligent and devoted workers, and there is every hope that at no distant date a suitable building will be erected and residence secured. When visiting this "Settlement" a few months ago we were much impressed by the zeal and devotion of those who, with much selfsacrifice, have carried on this work during the last eight years. 

They have been struggling to reach an ideal, and in time will attain it. Though at present it stands alone in that great city, yet it acts as a Central Power-House, and gives valuable assistance to many other philanthropic agencies by visiting and reporting upon their cases. According to their last Report the total cost of carrying on the Settlement was £1,650 for the year.

A MUNICIPAL BAKERY (Budapest). 

pure 

THOUGH Hungary is one of the largest wheat-growing countries of Europe, the price of bread is much higher than it is in 

England. To remedy, or at least, to mitigate this grievance, and at the same time to ensure a supply of a and wholesome article, the Municipal Council of Budapest has recently established a Bread Factory, an account of which was given in the Municipal Journal, December 12th, 1909. This bakery is a large building of three storeys, built on municipal land at a cost of £25,458, and fully equipped with the most up-to-date machinery. The flour is hoisted by electrical power to the top storey, and in its progress downward is weighed, sifted, mixed, and leavened, and on reaching the ground floor it is baked by being

By courtesy of the Editor of the Municipal Journal. 

THE KNEADING MACHINE. 

By courtesy of the Editor of the Municipal Journal. 

THE OVENS.

PROGRESS 

pressed through steam-heated ovens. To ensure perfect cleanliness on the part of those who manipulate it, each workman-before beginning his work-is required, not only to wash, but even to take a shower bath, and then to don his clean working uniform. At present the municipality is supplying about 50,000 lbs. of bread daily, but its productive capacity is now being increased to 120,000, as, owing to the fact that the bread is not only superior in quality but lower in price than that produced by private enterprise, the demand is much greater than the supply. Naturally it encountered strong opposition from the master bakers, but, they have now learnt. to lower their prices and raise the quality. The public have always supported the action of the municipal authorities. 

SCHOOLS MUTUAL AID SOCIETY. 

THE work of this Society is well worth the attention of all Education Authorities and school teachers. Its object is to promote correspondence in connection with Nature Study between town and country schools, so that each may supply what the other lacks. For this purpose two schools—an urban and a rural are put into communication with each other. Each country school sends to its corresponding town school common objects suitable for Nature Study-the objects, or similar ones, having been previously used for a lesson by the senders; in return, the town school sends letters with details of town life, its Zoological Gardens, Museums, Picture Galleries, etc., also scrap albums, picture postcards, etc. In some cases the town teacher will send the syllabus of his Nature Study course to the teacher in the country, who endeavours to supply the needful specimens ; others prefer to receive such miscellaneous specimens as the children may happen to find. There is no hard and fast rule. To aid the seekers, the Society issues a detailed list of the plants, flowers, etc., that may be found each month of the year. The average cost of postage is 10s. per annum for each school.

The L.C.C. and some other Educational Authorities pay this out of their own funds ; in other cases it is paid out of a fund contributed by the teachers, or scholars, or both. It is an exceedingly happy idea, and one that carries with it large possibilities, not only quickening an interest in Nature Study, but awakening that sense of kinship and comradeship between town and country children which must have its influence on character.

TO SAVE THE BABIES. 

IN an address to the Summer School for the study of Social Questions, Mr. Broadbent the babies' friend-drew attention to the fact that "as civilisation advances, so will the maternal instinct decay, and its place must be taken by intelligence." Dr. Saleeby illustrates the truth of this by pointing out that while a mother cat always suckles her kittens and never thinks of giving them "anything that's going," a human mother often will, and needs to be taught that the mother's milk is the natural food for the babe. It is this fact that lends such importance to the Schools for Mothers, Babies' Welcomes, and similar institutions which endeavour, first and foremost, to impress upon the mothers the vital importance of breast-feeding. The value of these educational agencies is now becoming widely recognised and new ones are being constantly started in our own and other countries. 

They mostly follow much the same lines, but there is one in Vienna which the writer visited last November which has adopted a plan which, so far as we know, is quite unique. It is known as the 

Still und Milchkasse (Vienna). In its broad features it is a 

Savings bank into which the expectant mother pays a certain sum weekly, so that when her child is born she may have the wherewithal either to provide herself with sufficiently nourishing food to enable her to feed from the breast, or, if that be impossible, to secure for it a full supply of cow's milk properly prepared. So far it differs little, if at all, from many other similar institutions. 

But this is not all. The scheme of insurance- —or rather, as will be seen later, of assurance-which was originated by Dr. Siegfried 

Weiss, the celebrated children's doctor in Vienna, is designed not only to enable the expectant mother to obtain extra nourishment when her child is born and needs the breast, but to obtain such nourishment, both in kind and quantity, as the doctor recommends.

As in many other societies certain weekly sums are paid in to the savings bank during the months preceding confinement. These sums vary according to the number of weeks paid in, but altogether they are expected to amount to 15kr. (about 13s.), though allowance is made for those whose poverty makes full payment impossible. But the benefit received when the child is born is by no means measured by the 15kr. contribution. That benefit (as already stated) is determined by the doctor's orders, and it is continued for at least six months after confinement. This amounts to 1kr. weekly at the least, and in many cases to as much as 2kr., the difference being made up partly by a subsidy from city funds, partly by contributions

PROGRESS 

from the large employers of workwomen, and the remainder from the coffers of the Society. It should be added that should a mother be physically unable to feed her child from the breast she receives a full supply of other milk to take its place. In cases of miscarriage or stillbirth, or of removal from the district the whole amount contributed is repaid without abatement.

But the scheme of the Stillkasse is by no means limited to the mere payment and repayment of so much cash, however generous the repayments may be. It is a School, not only for mothers, but 

VEREIN lings Milchverteilung STILL MILCHKASSE. 

By courtesy of Dr. Siegfried Weiss. 

WAITING FOR THE MILK DISTRIBUTION. 

G.U also for expectant mothers, each one of whom becomes the care of the Society before as well as after confinement. She is visited constantly by those whose one aim it is to make her realise how largely the health and even the life of the unborn child depend upon her own conduct and diet before as much as after its birth. She is advised what to do, what to take and what to avoid, and above all it is impressed upon her that she should so live that when her child is born she may have sufficient stamina to enable her to supply its natural nourishment. When the child is born a premium of 3kr. is given to the midwife if she takes care that the mother begins to feed from

the breast at once and encourages her to continue for the first few days. Such care is taken to impress upon the expectant mother the vital importance of breast-feeding that no temptation or dissuasion may induce her to wilfully neglect it; only those members of this society physically unfit ever resort to artificial feeding. 

It is to this feature in the scheme that Dr. Weiss attributes, almost entirely, the success of the Stillkasse. In many parts of 

Germany money is given freely to encourage breast-feeding, and yet it is a universal complaint that the results are far from satisfactory, the reason being that money alone is not a sufficient incentive. 

Influences must be brought to bear when the hope of motherhood is first awakened, and these influences must be continued until that hope is realised.

The Leeds Babies' Welcome is very similar in both its aims and methods to the schools for mothers and babies' welcomes which have been described in our pages from time to time. It is as yet quite young, but during its first 5 months its success has been most encouraging. Thursday afternoons when the babies are brought to be weighed and examined by Dr. Mary Phillips-who is rendering splendid service—the rooms are filled with mothers and babies and lady helpers. Tuesday is the day for the expectant mothers when about 30 are brought together by the promise of a cup of tea and the assurance of a kind and friendly welcome. At these gatherings, which are held on both days, opportunity is taken to impart instruction-not however by formal talks, or lectures on hygiene from inexperienced professors—but by friendly and neighbourly discussion on points vitally affecting the baby's welfare. In these talks the subject of breast-feeding is always given a prominent place, but all subjects that concern the care and management of infants are brought within their range.

When washing is the topic a demonstration lesson follows and one or more babies are stripped and washed to teach the mothers how it should be done. For the expectant mothers a thrift society has been started and now about 35 of the poorest of the poor-for the "Welcome" is in one of the most poverty stricken parts of the city—are storing up their pence that when the baby comes they may have something wherewith to provide for its needs. 

Bedding is sometimes lent when the need is great, but the women are taught and encouraged to make their own babies' clothes at the weekly meetings, and they may buy, at a merely nominal cost, flannels and wearing apparel, etc., which have been sent as gifts by friends of the movement.

PROGRESS THE VILLAGE LIFE. 

THE dulness of village life compared with BRIGHTENING the attractiveness of the city accounts in no small measure for the rural exodus. It is only the few who have sufficient interests of their own to rise above the depressing atmosphere of their surroundings; for the majority, life is a weary monotony and those who cannot escape from it inevitably deteriorate. Anything that can add brightness and zest to this village life is to be heartily welcomed. The revival of the Morris dance and the old folk songs is doing something, yet there is need for something more—something that will give life a wider outlook and a more serious purpose. Two instances have been brought to our notice recently which, though totally different in character, are making for the same result. One of these is the starting of 

The Social Service Rooms at Bredon's Norton.—Those who have read our previous accounts of this "Village Utopia,” may wonder what the liveliest imagination of the most benevolent fairygodmother could find to add to what has already been done for these fortunate villagers. But it is evident that the enthusiasm of 

Miss Zula Woodhull knows no bounds. We have already described how she transformed the parish school into a children's paradise* and established her Agricultural Club† as an intellectual and artistic centre, but these did not content her. She has a strong desire that the women of the nation should take a deeper and more intelligent interest in the nation's affairs. To encourage this among her own neighbours—and with the hope that her example may be followed elsewhere-she has taken and tastefully furnished a pretty little cottage and placed it at their disposal as a place of meeting primarily for the discussion of the social conditions of the nation. Magazines, newspapers and other literature will be provided, but serve merely as the hors d'œuvres to the main repast. has been appointed to take over the management. subscription and all belonging to the favoured sex make a free use of its rooms. these are to

Our second instance is the uprising of an entire community and the creation of what is now becoming known as 

The Teetotal Village of Heckington.-This remarkable move ment had a remarkable beginning. During the last general election in the Sleaford division of Lincolnshire (in which Heckington is situated), some of the villagers were jeered at by the publicans for * Vide "PROGRESS," Vol. III., pp. 205-8. Ibid, Vol. IV., pp. 195-7.

wearing the colours of the candidate who was supposed to be inimical to their interests. Accordingly two of these decided to boycott the public-houses; they were soon joined by others and now from a population of only 1,600, more than 200 men have pledged themselves to abstain from all alcoholic beverages for twelve months. These have now enrolled themselves in the Heckington Working Men's Temperance League, a League which has entirely divested itself of any party character. The movement originated entirely among the working men themselves, but now a County Councillor and others of some social standing in the village have joined them. 

A difficulty however has arisen. Having shut themselves out from the public-house, which hitherto had been their rendezvous for social intercourse, they have nothing—at least nothing large enough -to take its place. Efforts are now being made to provide a hall, if possible of the character of Lady Bell's Winter Garden in Middlesbro', where they can meet for social entertainments and refreshments. Land can be had at a reasonable price, but money is required for the building. Some few subscriptions have been promised and the men themselves—many of whom are agricultural labourers with only 15/- a week-are giving 1d. weekly to this fund. There is in this movement a fine spirit which deserves encouragement; evidences of its reality are already seen in the better clothing and more comfortable homes of many of the villagers and in the payment of some old standing debts to local tradesmen. Help will be gladly received by the HON. SEC., Mr. J. Pockington.

SMALL 

HOLDERS' BANK (Norway). 

In an article which appeared in "PROGRESS" some years ago, * Mr. Yerburgh gave an interesting account of the movement for the establishment of Co-operative Banks especially in Germany and Ireland, and drew attention to the urgent need for their wider extension in this country. A report of H.M. Minister in Christiana, which appeared in the Labour 

Gazette for last December, describes a Bank established in Norway, having much the same object but on a different principle. 

Bank is not co-operative but State-supported. The amount of capital is fixed by Parliament and is provided by grants from the Exchequer. 

In the financial year 1907-8, this amounted to £278,000, but beyond this the Bank is empowered to contract loans (guaranteed by the State) up to six times the value of its capital. Interest is paid on the capital supplied by the State, but only after deducting the This

PROGRESS 

cost of administration and any deficit that may be occasioned by the Bank having to pay more for loans contracted than it received for loans granted.

The Bank has been established to grant loans to citizens of small means to enable them to acquire either small holdings or their own dwellings. In the former case the interest charged must not exceed 

3 per cent., nor in the latter 4 per cent. Loans are not granted to owners of property of greater value than £83 (country) or £11 (town). All applications for land must be approved, and all loans guaranteed by the local Council, which must also certify that the applicant is "competent to own, manage, and keep up a farm." 

Land holdings must not be less than 1 acre nor more than 5, and the loan value (its market price) must not exceed 197; in the case of dwellings, the land must not exceed 1 acre nor the loan value of land and buildings together, £278. Loans may be granted for dwellings up to nine-tenths the loan value with a guarantee, or up to one-half, without. The Bank holds the mortgages and all losses are made up by the Exchequer. At the end of the first four years loans had been granted amounting to £625,000. Applications came principally from labourers, factory workers, artisans, and fishermen. Out of a total of 2,500 applications only thirty-six were rejected as unfit.

SMALLHOLDER. 

Two difficulties stand in the way of keeping HOUSING THE our surplus rural population from increasing the congestion in the towns :-land and housing. THE WAYFORD TENANTS LTD. has just been started in 

N.E. Norfolk for the purpose of overcoming both. To provide land for smallholdings a farm of 98 acres has been purchased at a cost of £2,700; of this there are 10 acres of woodland and gravel pits—the latter bringing an income of about £50 per annum. The remainder will be divided into 21 holdings ranging from 1 to 21 acres, and let at an average rental of 30/- per acre, with fixity of rent, tenure, and compensation for all improvements. Up to last 

February six of these holdings had been allotted. Applications (numbering 200) came from various parts of England, but until more houses can be built only six could be entertained. 

To provide these houses with necessary outbuildings at a reasonable rent is one of the main objects of the society. Its scheme is that of the Co-partnership Tenants with which it is affiliated. The * Vide." PROGRESS," Vol. I., pp. 184-192.

shares are 10 each, and interest is limited to 5 per cent. It is expected that in course of time the tenant members will hold the whole of the shares and become, under this co-operative scheme, the owners of the entire estate with its land and buildings. One holding of 12 acres has been retained to serve as a model of a Danish smallholding. The services of a Danish smallholder have been secured and he will be available to help and instruct the others. 

Connected with the Wayford Tenants—though the funds are kept distinct-there is an Agricultural Trading Society (affiliated to the A.O.S.) which purchases for its members at wholesale prices all implements, seeds, manures, etc., and also arranges for the sale of their produce. For this purpose a Central Store is to be erected at which all products, such as eggs, vegetables, fruit, etc., can be graded and packed. There is also a Wayford Credit Society which is establishing a Credit Bank on the lines of the Raiffeisen banks in Germany. It is hoped that it will shortly be possible to arrange for the insurance of live stock. A Library and Reading Room are also to be added. Tenants share in all surplus profits. 

SOCIAL ASSETS. 

99 

AN article which appeared in "PROGRESS under this heading* some time ago excited so much attention that in two cases known to us complete surveys of the Social Assets of the town have been made on the lines suggested, and in several other cases the work has been done partially. That article urged the importance of an "exhaustive inquiry as to what agencies are available in any given community to cope with the needs of that element of the population which lives on the border line of poverty," without which no scientific solution of its problems can be reached. It is astonishing how little even intelligent people know about what is being donestill less about what is being left undone—in their own towns. The article which appeared in our last number under the head "Social Consulates" pointed out how much is being left undone which might be done if sufficient pressure were brought to bear upon the respective authorities to carry out existing Acts. 

But that pressure will only be applied after the facts have been ascertained. 

The February number of the "Association Monthly," the organ of the Y.W.C.A. movement in America, draws attention to the importance of ascertaining these facts, and at the same time it tells 

* Social Assets, by F. J. Matheson & Godfrey E. Reiss, ("PROGRESS" Vol. II., pp. 247-256).

PROGRESS 

of steps that are being taken by what is termed the Know your 

City" movement to attain this end.

The principle underlying the "Know your City" movement was a simple one. We must know our city before we can improve it. We must know what organisations we have with which we can co-operate, and whether any new kinds of work are needed. 

If a new Association is to be formed, we must know what work other societies are doing, for we wish to give just what is most needed and not to duplicate the endeavours of others. And further, we must know how much help and what kind of help we can get from the city authorities themselves. This is a possible source of power too much neglected by voluntary organisations. Many of the civic improvements which we desire can be secured, not by the vast labour and expense of voluntary contributions, but by appealing to the appropriate board of the city government. The average citizen has no idea of the vast extent to which he can use his city boards. 

This movement has already resulted in the formation of two "Know your City" Institutes in the towns of Seattle and Portland (Oregon).

Seattle was the first to start and it started in a very simple way. 

A father and daughter desiring to know more of a community of which they had just become members, gathered together a few friends into their own home to talk over the various activities of the city. A resolution was come to to make, if possible, a complete social survey in order to determine where work was most needed, and an effort was made to secure the co-operation of the city officials and the leaders of all the more prominent social movements. 

Gradually people of all classes became interested; addresses were given in the various clubs, societies, etc., and public opinion was roused. Having accomplished this, more regular meetings were arranged, in consultation with social workers representing various interests, at each of which some special topic was discussed. Previous to each session a visit was paid to the particular institution which was to come under discussion.* towns.

This American example is well worthy of imitation in all our It points a way to carry out our suggestion for the formation of Social Consulates, and it is a movement in which the British Institute would be both able and willing to render valuable assistance. 

EMPLOYING THE UNEMPLOYABLE. 

THE story of Pastor Bodelschwingh's wonderful Settlement at Bielefeld with its colony for epileptics, its Labour Colony, its Workmen's Homes and Workshops, has often been told. To "gather up the fragments that nothing be lost" is the purpose that determines all *Miss Strong (the daughter mentioned above) who writes this account, gives the following as the programme for the Portland Institute :-The City Council, Public Health, City Plans, Child Life, Courts and Jails, Charities, Welfare Work, Industrial Life, Standards of Life (including Amusements), Housing Conditions, etc., and lastly, Things we lack.

its activities. The good pastor believes that nothing should be wasted either of human energy or inanimate matter. We have a fine illustration of this ruling principle in that branch of his work known as "Brockensammlung," a word that finds its best translation in our phrase," gather up the fragments." An interesting account of this was given by Miss Sutter in her book, "The Colony of 

Mercy," some seventeen years ago, and a recent article in the Daily

Graphic has brought up-to-date the facts concerning this effort to utilise what otherwise would be "cast as rubbish to the void."

The Brockensammlung is not unknown in other parts of Germany; accounts of those in Berlin and Stuttgart have already been given in these pages, but here not only are cast-off garments, boots, furniture, etc., collected and repaired, that they may be sold at merely nominal prices to the neighbouring poor, but even such apparently worthless things as empty meat tins, capsules of bottles, etc., are so ingeniously handled by these so-called industrial wastrels. that they are turned out by the ton in the shape of toys-toy soldiers, toy trains, etc.—and are sold at a profit sufficient to provide for the maintenance of a large number of men, women, and children whose physical infirmities render them utterly unfit to enter the ordinary channels of industry. And it is wonderful how much ingenuity is displayed in utilising these fragments. "Japanese " lacquered ware is made out of the covers of old books; bronze ashtrays from old meat tins treated in a bronze bath ; even clock-work trains, which are sold at £1 a piece, are produced by using the mechanism taken from old watches. It is satisfactory to learn that after paying for the maintenance of the workers, who would otherwise have been a public burden, a fairly handsome profit remains for the support of the manifold benevolences of the Colony. But better still is the brightness brought into the lives of those who are being taught that so far from being good-for-nothings, there is a work that they can do and do well. * Vide "PROGRESS," Vol. II., pp. 198 f.

Municipal Free Legal Advice.-A Municipal Bureau has been established in Nüremberg at which free legal information is given to all citizens on certain matters connected with sick, accident, invalidity, and old-age insurance; also with regard to the respective rights of employer and employed. Citizens of the poorer class can receive free legal advice on all questions concerning the rights of citizenship, including all matters coming under the criminal code, if they are not themselves in a position to pay for an attorney. The officials connected with this Bureau, which is entirely supported by the Municipality, are strictly forbidden to receive any fees. There are as many as twenty of these Bureaux in other German cities. 

Incubators for Infants.-Dr. Moore, the Medical Officer of 

Health for Huddersfield, who has done so much to lower the rate of infant mortality in that town, has recently persuaded the Town Council to support him in another scheme for the same purpose. 

The Statistics of infant deaths revealed the fact that a large number were due to "debility at birth," "premature birth," and "cardial debility,' and Dr. Moore felt that if cases of this kind and others of a similar nature could be tided over their initial weaknesses and treated with exceptional care, many lives might be saved. To attain this, he has persuaded the Council to provide incubators-not to hatch the eggs, but to save the chickens, when hatched. The incubator is so simple that it can be used in the poorest homes. It consists of a boxabout as large as a moderate-sized soap box-with a glass top in which are holes for the pure air to come in and the bad air to go out. This box is laid on a couple of hot bricks; a thermometer hangs inside, by which the heat can be regulated from 65° to 85°,

according to the doctor's directions. This is the baby's home for such time as is necessary. Already they have done such good work in the homes of the poor under the supervision of the Lady Health Visitors that they have been introduced into the workhouse. Many lives have thus been spared.

Children and the Poor Law System. - A Blue Book recently issued contained a report on the condition of children in receipt of various forms of Poor-law relief in England and Wales by Miss Ethel M. N. Williams, M.D., assisted by Miss Mary 

Longman and Miss Marion Phillips, B.A. The total number of children (i.e., persons under sixteen) in receipt of Poor-law relief in 

England and Wales on January 1st, 1907, was 234,004, exclusive of a small number relieved in the casual wards. Of these children the insane numbered 2,086. They are a class which is steadily increasing. Of the 2,086, more than one-third belong to the

Metropolis. By far the largest class of Poor-law children is that on out-door relief. In January, 1907, these numbered 171,497 out of 231,918, or nearly 74 per cent. of the whole. The percentage of out-relief children classed as dull or mentally defective was as follows in the under-mentioned unions, viz. : Lambeth 32, Bradford 20, Liverpool 25, Merthyr Tydvil 18, Newcastle 24.5, York 33, Mitford and Launditch 43, Warwick 22, Bicester 23.

With regard to the low rate in Bradford, the investigations say: "The knowledge that out-relief is not given to applicants in undesirable surroundings undoubtedly prevents a certain number of persons in the Bradford district from applying."

The New Labour Exchanges.—At the beginning of last month 

Mr. Buxton, the President of the Board of Trade, was able to make the gratifying announcement that in the first 5 weeks during which these Exchanges have been opened "employers had notified 32,500 vacancies, and 19,907-or almost 20,000-places had been filled." 

It may be thought that this is a case for the new broom, but if the experience of our Exchanges is at all similar to that of those in Germany this is merely a fore-taste of far larger results. The initial difficulties are the greatest; the Labour Exchange can never reach its full strength until it has secured the entire confidence of both employer and employed, and this takes time. This has been the secret of its strength in Germany, but it was only attained after much struggle. It is satisfactory to learn that in our case the

PROGRESS 

beginnings are so full of promise, and what is perhaps the most satisfactory is the very large number of vacancies notified by employers. 

Hungry out-of-works will register rapidly enough, but employers often look shy at new schemes which they have not learned to understand. In 1908 Berlin alone filled more than 100,000 places, and if we are to rise to anything like the same proportion it must come through the hearty co-operation of the work-giver. 

A most valuable section of the work of these Exchanges is that which deals with Boy and Girl labour. If the plan adopted at the 

Leeds Exchange-which we have visited-is a fair sample of others, the work is being very thoroughly done. It is recognised that it is not sufficient to take merely such boys and girls as have determination enough to place their own names on the register, and then, having found them situations, leave them to look after themselves. 

Far more than this is needed if the problem is to be grappled with effectively. And far more is being done. The manager of the 

Exchange has put himself in close touch with the Education Authority, and through that with the Headmasters and Mistresses of the Elementary Schools. He is also enlisting a band of visitors who will help him especially on two points :—(1) to assist boys and girls to make a wise choice of a career, and (2) to see that those for whom places have been found make the best use of the opportunities which are afforded them when they commence. This last is of the utmost importance. Boys and girls need to be encouraged to stick to their places and overcome initial difficulties. They need also to be induced to use wisely their leisure time by attending Continuation Classes, and by associating themselves with some Club or Brigade. 

This opens a wide field for the personal service of the volunteer. 

Conditions of Female Labour in the Clothing Trades.— 

The returns of the Board of Trade enquiry into the earnings and hours of labour in the Dress, Millinery and Mantle Trades have just been published.* These returns affect 53,575 workpeople, of whom 44,978 were in workshops and 8,597 in factories. Though there is an increasing tendency for ready-made costumes to be made in factories, where power is used, yet more than 80 per cent. of the workers are still employed by the workshops, where wages are lower and hours longer. Of the total employed in the workshops 96 per cent. were women and girls (under 18 years), while in the factories *For fuller summary see Labour Gazette, February, 1910, pp. 41-44.

only 90.9 per cent. were women and girls and 9.1 per cent. men and boys.

The average weekly wage of the female workers, employed full time by the workshops, was found to be 13/10 for women and 3/9 for girls, but, excluding London-where the wages are nearly 25 per cent. higher than in the rest of the United 

Kingdom-the average stands at only a little over 12/- for women and a little less than 3/6 for girls; in factories the average for the 

United Kingdom is 15/5 for women and 6/4 for girls, or (excluding London) 14/8 and 6/2 respectively. As the average hours of labour are, in the workshops, 53.4 and in the factories 50.8 it will be seen that the rate of pay equals about 3d. an hour for the women and id. an hour for the girls. Returns have also been issued of wages paid in the Shirt, Blouse and Clothing Industry*. These affect a total of 35,624 workpeople, of whom 31 per cent. are employed in Ireland and 23 per cent. in London. Of this total, 7 per cent. were men and boys, 71 per cent. women, cent. girls. The average weekly wage for women (excluding 

London) is 12/2 and for girls 6/42. Here, however, the average is pulled down by the lower wages paid in Ireland, where the average stands at only 10/2 for women and 5/10 for girls. PURE MILK FOR THE 

UNITED 

STATES. 

Chairman of London Branch Council- THE LORD CHIEF JUSTICE. 

In view of the fact that the Joint Committee on Milk (composed of members of the League and of the National 

Health Society), is now preparing a series of leaflets-intended for the producer, retailer, and consumer respectively-to ensure that supply of pure milk so urgently needed in this and other countries, the following account of what is being done in the United States may interest our readers. 

THERE are at present 28 Medical Milk 

Commissions in the United States, which aim at ensuring that the milk sold under their auspices shall be both bacteriologically and chemically of the highest possible standard, and of a higher standard than the milk sold without their certificate. Their work has met with great success, and has appreciably raised the standard of the milk supplied.

Details as to the working of one of these Commissions have kindly been supplied by Dr. R. G. Perkins, bacteriologist to the

Commission in Cleveland, Ohio. The Commission was formed in 

1905, and is composed of seven of the leading medical and business. men in the town. A rigid contract was entered into with a farmer, giving absolute control to the Commission of every detail of the production, handling, transportation and delivery of the milk. The members of the Commission have absolutely no financial interest in the success of the undertaking, nor do they ever receive any recompense for their work. The staff consists of a bacteriologist, a chemist, and a veterinary inspector. The former makes tests at unexpected intervals, only such milk being approved as shows

a bacteriological standard of not more than 10,000 per cubic centimetre (about 16 drops), as compared with 500,000, which is the standard for dairymen outside the Commission. The bacteriological examination involves the farmer in no expense, unless it be a second or third test, necessitated by the first sample being below the standard set. The chemist has to satisfy himself, from time to time, that the milk contains not less than 4 per cent. of fat and is free from all contaminating foreign matter, and from the addition of chemical substances or colouring matter. The veterinary inspector deals with the management of the farm and the production and handling of the milk, and annually tests the herd with tuberculin, in addition to testing each new animal proposed to be added to the herd. The greatest safeguard for the purity of the milk is found to lie in the regulation adopted by the Commission that milk shall be sold in sterilised glass bottles only; such bottles being provided with a sealed metal cap supplied by the Commission, with an additional inner parchment cap, bearing the date of milking. The expenses of the Commission, including the experts' examinations, are covered by charging d. each for the caps-the certificate of the purity of the contents of each bottle.

The only means employed to attain this degree of perfection are absolute cleanliness, coolness and attention to detail. Neither sterilisation nor pasteurisation are ever resorted to, such treatment of the milk being considered prejudicial to the nutritive value as also to the digestion, and not sufficiently dependable. The cows are specially groomed and the stables kept scrupulously clean, the milkers sterilising their hands before milking and wearing special garments. The pails, too, are sterilised and the milk is cooled at once, being kept at a temperature of 45 degrees until delivery, by packing the sealed bottles in ice during transit. Experience has shown that the cans commonly used for large or small quantities of milk, both in the States (and far more in this country), are of a type which lends itself with fatal ease to contamination. It was found, for instance, that samples of milk taken from street dealers showed an increase of 50 per cent. in bacteria on windy, as compared with still days. 

All this care cannot be exercised without a considerable increase in cost to the producer. The Commission, therefore, allows him to recoup himself by charging 9d. instead of 4d. per quart, at which price there is a constantly increasing demand for "certified milk," as it is called, especially among the well-to-do classes and for those in ill health. Pure milk of this description, if kept at a low

NATIONAL LEAGUE 

temperature, remains perfectly sweet without the addition of chemicals for four or five days, and is therefore invaluable to travellers. 

HEALTH ASSOCIATION (BRADFORD). 

THIS Society is one outcome of the visit to 

England some years ago of Bernard Macfadden, of America, who, in his public lectures, satirized ill-health as being "a greater deviation from normalness' than immorality, and less excusable.” 

A movement was consequently formed in Bradford to raise the public ideal as to personal health, and to consider means of attaining greater physical efficiency. Without endorsing any particular views as to exercise, diet, etc., the Association opened its platform to those who had information or suggestions to offer as to the healthiest mode of living. Among those who have rendered valuable platform work are Dr. Rabagliati-whose reputation as a popular writer and speaker on hygiene is more than local-Dr. Crowleywho has recently been called to important work under the Board of 

Education and F. W. Richardson, Esq.-the City and County

Analyst. Popular lecturers from London, and elsewhere, have also given their views on subjects varying in scope from the experiences of record-breaking athletes to the views of those who emphasise the interactivity of human, physical, and psychic forces. Vegetarians and anti-vegetarians, physical culturists and spiritual healers, doctors and clergymen, food reformers and speakers on sex problems, etc., have all voiced the various phases of “ Physical Balance" which they represent, so that the members and friends of the Association might have a rational basis for forming their own conclusions. 

As was inevitable, the movement divided itself into sections; the 

Physical Culture Section has the use of the rooms for exercise classes two nights in the week (one night for each sex); the Outdoor Section arranges Saturday nature rambles and also holiday walking tours in the Yorkshire Dales, the Lake District, etc.; the Purity Section disseminates wholesome literature and information on the somewhat tabooed subject of sex; the Social Section seeks to add the desirable element of comradeship and has arranged many pleasant functions, including "Health Dances" with wide open windows. This section also arranges dancing classes for members of the Association. 

Each section has an organiser and a Committee. 

The public lectures of the Association are arranged by the Propaganda Section; the programme of the past winter included such lectures of public interest as "How to Beautify Bradford,"

"Unfriendly Microbes," "The Royal Poor Law Commission and Public Health," "Eugenics, the Breeding of Grander Humans.”

The Vegetarian Section (formerly the Bradford Food Reform 

Society) has also arranged public lectures and cookery demonstrations. 

A novel and very popular branch of the Association was started last summer by the formation of a Camping Section. A secluded field near the famous Rombalds Moors has been secured and two wooden pavilions (one for each sex) erected in addition to tents.

Each hut is fitted with a dozen berths (much like those on a steamship). This method has been found healthier and more comfortable than tents, as a freer circulation of air is ensured, but those who prefer tents may have them. The camp is sufficiently near Bradford for daily travelling backward and forward, and by providing healthy conditions and happy social intercourse is becoming popular as a week-end resort. For £1 per annum members may secure a sleeping bunk with use of cooking hut, etc. Each lives the simple life according to his own fancy. Athletic sports, picnics, etc., are arranged from time to time. The Camping Section (owing to the self-sacrificing work of its more active members) is self-supporting, while the expense to each member is only nominal. 

This enterprise of the Bradford Health Association might well be adopted in other parts of the country as an attractive means of getting young men and women into more vital touch with progressive social enterprise.

While the Association has received much encouragement, it is felt that it is as yet only entering upon the real work which is necessary to be done in large cities in the way of Health Education. 

Probably the Public Authorities can be helped and quickened by the development of public opinion. The Society has recently decided to form a Local Branch of the National League for Physical Educa tion and Improvement, and by this co-operation, hopes to render more efficient public service next winter. 

The membership subscription is 10/6 a year, but lower rates are available for membership of some of the sections. The public lectures are either free, or the charge very low. The rooms of the 

Association, where the Lectures, Drill Classes, Socials, etc., are held, are in Brearton Street, Manningham Lane. Mrs. Alfred

Illingworth, the honoured president, has rendered invaluable help and stimulus in many ways. HON. SEC., Rennie J. Foster, Exchange

Buildings, Bradford.

NATIONAL LEAGUE SCHOOL DENTISTRY. 

APART from general school clinics, school dentistry is making good progress,* largely due to the School Dentists' Society, recently affiliated with the National League. A pamphlet on the objects and aim of the Society (of which Mr. W. Fisk is Hon. Sec.) gives much valuable information on the present condition of school dentistry in this and several other countries. Germany still keeps to the fore; it has now more than 50 school dental surgeries, in which no fewer than 160,588 elementary school children were examined and treated during the year ending July, 1909, showing an average of 8 bad teeth for each child. A "Central Committee for the National Care of the Teeth" has now been formed and it is appealing to all public authorities for support and unity in this important movement. It proposes to make attendance at the clinics obligatory, and to make a compulsory charge of 1/- per annum for such services. 

In the United States, the Dental Hygiene Council of Massachusetts is doing much the same kind of work. It aims at educating the public as to the necessity for keeping the teeth and mouth clean and healthy; at inspiring general interest in saving the teeth; and at establishing dental hygiene as part of the medical examination of school children. With this end in view, it has published a variety of instructive leaflets and pamphlets for distribution among parents, educators, social workers and others likely to benefit from them. 

CHILD EMIGRATION. 

THE value of child emigration is rapidly gaining recognition. 

Child emigration is not open to the many objections that lie in the way of adult emigration; it opens up possibilities of health and success to the young emigrant, from which the older man is debarred; and above all it deals with humanity while its subject is still keenly sensitive to the influence of education and physical environment. 

A Society was founded last October by Colonials at Oxford University, called "The Society for the Furtherance of Child Emigration to the Colonies," which is under distinguished patronage and has just received the official recognition of the National League for Physical Education. This Society has brought forward, and is advocating, the "Farm-school" system of child emigration; it hopes to act in 

Mr. Councillor Arthur Black, of Liverpool, writes regarding an account of "School

Dental Clinics" given in "PROGRESS for last Oct. "It may interest you to know that 

2 years ago a room was fitted up, and a dentist engaged, to examine and attend to the teeth of the children in the Day Industrial School under the Liverpool Education Authority. This, I believe, has worked well." [ED.]

the Colonies as the one or main agency for taking over pauper children from the State and from associations and societies in Great Britain. The Society believes that the best results from child emigration are not to be obtained either by the boarding-out or the placing-out systems, but only by an upbringing in the Colonies based on the soundest principles of agricultural education and modern scientific knowledge of a child's requirements. The Government of Newfoundland has offered the Society 50,000 acres as a farm training-ground, and considerable other facilities. Mr. Kingsley 

Fairbridge, Exeter College, Oxford, is at present acting for the Society. 

THE LEAGUE'S PUBLICATIONS. 

It is satisfactory to record that there has been within the last six months a steady and continuous increase in the sale of our publications. 

Leaflet No. 3, on "HOW TO BRING UP A BABY," has still the largest circulation, nearly 50,000 having been disposed of, in the main to public bodies such as District Councils, Boards of Guardians, District Nursing Associations, etc. The recently issued revised edition of this leaflet, in card form for hanging on the wall, has already met with much favour, and over 5,000 copies have been circulated. The Welsh edition of these instructions on the feeding and care of infants has also just been published. Leaflet No. 4, on "Health Visiting," has been so much in demand that a second edition is now in course of preparation, in which the information is to be brought up to date and amplified. The "Report on Physical 

Education in Foreign Countries," which was issued a short time ago as the first of a new series of pamphlets, has been very well received and most favourably noticed in several journals. The 

Lancet in a long review says of it :-"We in this country have until recently, in our usual happy-go-lucky fashion, neglected the systematic teaching of physical exercises, except in the case of the Services, and the report before us may well give our educational authorities material for consideration. A sense of discipline is the crying need of the day, and this the foreign system of physical education inculcates more generally than our own systems, or rather want of system."

The League has also decided to issue English and Welsh editions of a leaflet on "Cleanliness and Ventilation in the Home," which should prove very useful. Publications on several other subjects germane to the League are also under consideration by the 

Executive Council.
