Books on Social Subjects. 

(For full particulars as to Publishers, Price, etc., see current, or former, Bibliography).

Trades for London Boys and Girls. (Longmans.)—In our notice of these two handbooks which appeared in the January number of "PROGRESS," the title was wrongly given as "Trade Schools, etc."

Those who took the trouble to read the notice would see the mistake at once, but those who went no further than the title would probably be misled. We take this opportunity of making the correction and apologising for the mistake.

How the Casual Labourer Lives. (Northern Publishing Co., 

Liverpool. is. and 2s.)—This is a most interesting Report, as much on account of the patience and skill with which it has been compiled as for the matter it contains. It gives the first-fruits of a small committee formed to promote "research into social conditions." The committee was composed of representatives of the Liverpool branches of the C.S.U., the N.U.W.W., the Fabian Society, the W.I.C., the Statistical Society, and the Victoria Settlement. The object of the present research was (1) to ascertain how family expenditure is regulated, in practice, to a violently fluctuating income, (2) to obtain figures showing the income and expenditure of individual labourer's families, (3) to study their diet on the lines laid down by Mr. Rowntree. 

The problem of the domestic condition and expenditure of the casual labourer is one of the most difficult in sociology, and especially so when these labourers are dockers. This will be readily understood when it is known that the wages of able-bodied men in the prime of life may varyas the Report states-from 50s. in one week to 15s. in the next; while those of others may range from 30s. to 4s. 6d. or nothing. The main object of this inquiry was to find out how the difficulty of adjusting such fluctuating incomes was met. To arrive at this an attempt was made to get a number of families to take books in which they were asked to keep a weekly record of the whole of the family's income and expenditure, together with all borrowings and repayments of debt. They were further asked to keep a weekly budget, entering everything spent each day; the bill of fare for each meal, with quantities, description and price; and the number of children and adults present at each meal. 

It must have required some courage even to ask for this from a dock labourer's wife, and it is no wonder that they had many refusals. Miss 

Augusta Jones acted as investigator, and by the exercise of admirable tact and patience, she managed to get 40 books taken, from which fairly trustworthy budgets were obtained. Twenty-two of these were for periods of six weeks or more. The 40 budgets were then collected and tabulated, and the results are here given. To these are appended a graphic representation showing in coloured columns the amounts spent by each family on various kinds of food, and indicating how far the total expenditure of each comes above or below Rowntree's minimum of 3s. 3d. per man per week, as well as other interesting matter. We heartily echo the

PROGRESS 

99 hope of the committee "that the results obtained may have some practical effect in strengthening the hands of those working for social reform,' and would express the further hope that this excellent example will incite others to pursue similar investigations. 

The Labour Exchange in Relation to Boy and Girl Labour. 

By F. KEELING. (King. 6d.)—Mr. Keeling is now the Manager of the 

Leeds Labour Exchange, and his evident grip of one of the most important branches of the work of these Exchanges, as revealed in this book, fully justifies his appointment. The problem of finding work for unemployed adults will be greatly relieved if suitable careers are found for the thousands of boys and girls who leave our elementary schools every year. It is with this phase of the subject that Mr. Keeling deals in his book, and he deals with it in a way which shows that he is not only fully alive to its gravity, but, what is equally important, is fully acquainted with the best methods that have been tried. In The plan of the book is excellent. It consists of four chapters. the first is a statement of the " general aspects of the problem and its relation to the Labour Exchange "; in the second, the various experiments now being tried in England and Scotland are discussed; the third gives an admirable account of German experiments in the same direction, revealing not only the writer's knowledge of the bare facts, but also a true appreciation of all that is best in these German Labour Bureaux and most worthy of adoption here. The final chapter, in which Mr. Keeling states the conclusions at which he has arrived, is an attempt to construct a definite scheme by first of all deciding which, if any, of the English schemes now in operation should be ruled out as "inadequate or unsatisfactory," and then, by discussing the rela-tion the Labour Exchange should bear to any that may remain. With regard to the most important of these-the Education Authorities- 

Mr. Keeling expresses his opinion that "all the branches of the machinery for the organisation of the labour market should be housed in one building, and that the Board of Trade should be the authority representing the State in the actual placing of juveniles as well as adults." In the former, each Exchange should have its separatedepartment. Mr. Keeling's book, with its intelligent appreciation of the many sides of this problem, will be a real help to many in their efforts to solve it.

Our National Drink Bill. JNO. NEWTON. (Nisbet. Is.) The Economy of Temperance. By J. J. BAKER. (C.E.T.S. 6d.)-In 

150 pp. Mr. Newton gives a telling array of facts and figures to show "the economic, industrial and social effects of our enormous expenditure: on intoxicating liquors." Its moral and physical evils have been repeatedly set forth, but hitherto, in this country at least, its economic side the damage to our commercial prosperity and lessening of productive efficiency-has not been sufficiently dealt with. Appeals to the pocket will often succeed where other arguments fail, and Mr. 

Newton has rendered good service by his treatment of the drink question

as a "business proposition, " and appealing on that ground both to employers and employed. Chapter XI., on "Inefficiency," contains a series of facts which should be seriously pondered by all who are concerned to maintain our commercial supremacy. So far as has been possible every fact has been tested by personal enquiry and every effort has been made to ensure accuracy. 

In the ECONOMY OF TEMPERANCE Mr. Baker deals with the same subject and from the same standpoint, but with a somewhat different purpose. 

His book is one of a series of text-books published by the C.E.T.S. for use in Bands of Hope. This is for the Seniors, and it is well adapted for its purpose. The facts and figures--which in this new edition have been carefully revised and brought up to date—are arranged for a course of twelve lessons, and divided under the two main headings, Domestic and National Economy. With each lesson there is given a Blackboard 

Leaders of Adult Societies and Senior Bands of Hope will find it a useful help. 

summary. 

The Hygiene of School Life, By RALPH H. CROWLEY, M.D. 

(Methuen. 3s. 6d.)—One of the most hopeful signs of the times is the enhanced value now put upon child life, and the increasing importance attached to all efforts to preserve and enrich it. This book deals with the child during its school life, and no one is better able to do this than Dr. Crowley, whose fine record as Medical Inspector of the Bradford 

Education Authority has won for him his present well-merited position in the Medical Department of the Board of Education. The object the author set before himself is thus briefly stated :--- 

To discuss the means by which the physical and mental health of school children can, in existing circumstances, be best promoted, and to offer some practical suggestions for dealing with the problems which confront Local Education Authorities, medical officers, managers, and parents in trying to make the School Medical Service of this country as effective and useful as possible.

These problems are by no means small, though they are often exaggerated by those who have no intelligent appreciation of their importance. The chief difficulty which deters many, even enlightened Education Authorities who are fully alive to the importance of making the School Medical Service effective, is that of cost. It is poverty, not will, that is responsible for much of the present ineffective administration. 

Dr. Crowley had to meet this difficulty at first in Bradford, but he showed so plainly his strong conviction that the life and welfare of the child are vastly more important than the exact figure of the education rate, and threw such enthusiasm into his advocacy, that he soon carried his Committee with him and placed Bradford quite in the forefront of other Education Authorities; in medical inspection, and treatment at its policlinic; in the establishment of an open air school; in school feeding; and now by the introduction of the shower-bath, so universal in Germany but scarcely known in this country. That which Dr. Crowley accomplished in his own city he now seeks to accomplish elsewhere by the publication of this book in which he urges the vital importance of these

PROGRESS 

among other provisions for the physical and mental welfare of our children during their school life. We cannot too highly recommend it; it should be carefully studied by all who have that welfare in their keeping, and by that not ungenerous public who are always ready to pay when convinced of necessity. 

Infancy. 

Edited by T. N. KELYNACK, M.D. (Robt. Culley. 1s.)- 

This book--the first of a series of National Health Manuals intended "to give an up-to-date scientific presentation of the principles which should guide the maintenance of personal, domestic, and national health "will be most helpful to all who are grappling with the problem of infant life preservation. This problem has attracted so much attention during recent years that there has been no lack of books, and many excellent books, dealing with its various phases, but we know of none which gives so concisely exactly what ought to be known and with the authority of so many experts. It contains twelve chapters, each of which is contributed by a recognised medical authority on the subject dealt with. 

Among these subjects are Anatomy; Hygiene and Feeding of Infants; their Common Disorders; Schools for Mothers; Créches and Infant 

Nurseries; Milk Depôt; Infant Life in relation to its existing laws; 

National and Municipal responsibility, and the Moral Aspects of Infant 

Life Protection. For the treatment of these subjects Dr. Kelynack has gathered together a fine band of contributors, contenting himself with a short, but very thoughtful introductory chapter, the inspiration for which may be found in Ruskin's dictum, "There is no Wealth but Life.” To each chapter an appendix is added, which gives a brief bibliography of books bearing on the subject in hand, but given with a wide outlook, so that it "may lead to specialised study and practical service."

By ARTHUR LATHAM and The Conquest of Consumption. 

C. H. GARLAND. (Unwin, 4s. 6d.)—The recognition of the fact that this conquest is possible is stimulating inquiry and effort in all directions,* and if the result is not speedily attained it will not be through lack of information and advice. The book before us is one of the more recent contributions to the armoury of this crusade. Here, however, the subject is treated on one of its side issues. It is dealt with not so much as a pathological, but as an economic study. It shows us the cost of the disease in life; in productive efficiency which implies wage-earning capacity; and in money-to the State, to the Insurance and Charitable 

Societies, as well as to individuals. That cost is worked out in the summary as amounting to (direct and indirect) a sum of over £7,000,000, and this, altogether apart from the cost of General Hospitals, personal expenditure or private and organised charity. The writers regard this enormous expenditure as a pure waste of money since consumption is due to causes which can, and ought to be removed. The theory of heredity (except in the rarest instances) is dismissed as altogether * Vid supra p. 115.

39 untenable, and "the two main sources of the bacilli which destroy the human race are stated to be (1)“ milk and butter " (2) " expectoration and the discharges of consumptives." From these premises the writers arrive at the conclusion that "with comprehensive co-operation and intelligent expenditure and organisation the disease can be eradicated in a generation.” But if this is to be done "the State must move in the matter, both for prevention and treatment," and this must be (1) by so improving the health conditions that it will be less easy for the disease to secure its victims; and (2) through some system of State 

Insurance by which all who are affected can obtain the necessary sana torium treatment. 

The Industrial System. By J. A. HOBSON. (Longmans, 

7s. 6d.)—The product of industry is commonly divided into Rent, Interest, and Wages. One school of economists, of whom Henry George is the most famous, argue that the labourer is paid his necessary wages, the capitalist his average interest and profits, and the surplus, the rest, goes to the landlord. General Walker and his followers (if he had any) maintained that the landlord took his differential rent, the capitalist his interest, and labour got what was left. The usual view is that the capitalist is the residuary legatee. Mr. Hobson rightly contends that the surplus actually goes to the agent which "has the pull," usually the capitalist sometimes the landlord: occasionally, in theory, if not in reality, the wage-worker. 

He analyses this surplus, and contends that it is useless to its recipient, in that it does not evoke any service in return. Rent, he says, always has this characteristic, and therefore unearned incomes, and that part of nominally earned incomes which surplus are the proper subject of taxation.

This is a very brief summary of a long and interesting volume. Mr. 

Hobson has set out to write a new economics, with hardly any reference to previous authorities. The exception is his attack on the marginal theory, which is effective up to a point, but inconclusive when he applies it to rent. But oddly enough, he himself uses the marginal theory in his explanation of interest, which he regards as determined by the marginal increment, the savings of the person just induced to save instead of spending, a view quite at variance with facts. 

Mr. Hobson's account of currency is most admirable, and is probably new, because it contains a remarkable blunder. He rightly argues that all property can be converted by the banks into currency (p. 264), and yet works out an elaborate calculation on p. 262 on the altogether false assumption that all the national annual income, and that only, is property which can be transmuted into currency. 

The merit of Mr. Hobson's book is its objective treatment of economics. He thinks in terms of things instead of manipulating economic abstractions. But he does not carry this method far enough. 

If he did he would perceive that his own favourite theory of underconsumption as an explanation of unemployment is based on an antithesis between saving and spending; and this antithesis, apparently

PROGRESS 

clear in terms of money, completely vanishes in terms of goods. new house to a duke is spending to a co-partnership tenant is saving. 

The new station at Victoria is either saving or spending as the Brighton " accountants determine. Which it ought to be called the ordinary man cannot tell. 66 All material goods are first saved and then spent. If they last for a short time are said to be "consumed." If they last for years or centuries, they are generally called savings. But there is no philosophic or economic distinction between the two, any more than there is between hot things and cold things.

No one could agree with all that this book contains, but nearly every page provokes the reader to vigorous thought. 

E. R. P. 

Laggards in our Schools. By LEONARD P. AYRES, M.A. 

(Charities Publication Committee, N.J., $1.50.)-The problem of the backward scholar has been the puzzle of teachers, Education Authorities, and-sometimes-of parents ever since educational results became a subject of scientific analysis. The old theory that backwardness is necessarily caused by stupidity or laziness has quite broken down, and with it we hope will pass away the stupidity or laziness of those in authority who have hitherto been content to leave the backward child to muddle through the various standards as best it could. Other methods have been tried with some considerable success by those who have learnt that backwardness results from removable causes, and that the success or failure of such children in after life depends upon their proper treatment during their earlier years. Among the most successful of these have been the Open Air School, and what is known as the "Mannheim System," which provides an intermediate grade. 

But hitherto all methods, however successful, have only touched the fringe of the problem, and have been able to offer only partial solutions, through not having a broad enough basis of fact. It was to supply this basis that this investigation was entered upon, the results of which are given in Mr. Ayres' Report. It was on a scale so wide that it was only made possible by means of the financial assistance of the Russell Sage Foundation. The problem which presented itself to the investigators was this:--They found that an average of 33 per cent. of the children in the public schools of the country (ranging from 7 per cent. in Massachussets to 75 per cent. in Memphis) were above the normal age for the grades (standards) they were in, and they felt that about this large group facts were needed. To obtain these, they began with an intensive study of the school records of one school in New York, and from that they passed to a wider investigation in order to obtain answers to the 

(1) Are these children in their present condition mainly because of such removable physical disabilities as hypertrophied tonsils or adenoids, defective vision or hearing, malnutrition? (2) Do they drop behind in their school life because of illness? (3) Are they behind because of late entrance into the schools?

(4) To what extent is irregularity of attendance a factor in delayed progress? (5) Is compulsory labour after school hours an important factor? (6) Do any schools succeed in educating an appreciably larger per cent. of these children than others? If so, how is it done?

The result of their inquiry led to the conclusion that "all the most important causes of retardation of school life can be removed," and "that relatively few children are so defective as to prevent success either in school or in after life." The reasons for these conclusions are set forth here and illustrated by no fewer than 106 statistical tables. 

Of course the conditions in America are very different from those in England, and their problems are largely complicated by the large number of non-English speaking children; but the problem is acute with us, and such a thorough investigation of the school records in our own country would be of the utmost value. It is just the work the British Institute would gladly undertake, but it is an undertaking far too clostly for any Society that is compelled to rely on voluntary contributions. In England we can get neither Government aid nor large endowments for research work of this kind, and we can only congratulate our American cousins on their happier lot and thank them for this valuable contribution towards the solution of a vexed problem. 

The Guild of Play Book. Parts I., II. By G. J. Kimmins. 

(Curwen. 5s. each.)-These are charming books, and should do much to give fresh impetus to that revival of old folk songs and dances now happily spreading through the country. Part I. was issued more than two years ago, and Part II., which has just been published, has a special reference to the part taken at the Pageant in Fulham Palace last summer by members of Mrs. Kimmins' far-famed Guild of Play. In each Part full directions are given as to dances, with words, music, and illustrations, the whole forming a complete guide to representation by any number of children. Both books will be found most helpful to that considerable number of teachers, leaders of girls' clubs, etc., who are interesting themselves in restoring these healthful exercises of a bygone age. Dances such as these, in which there is an entire absence of vulgarity or self-cor ceit, bring out all that is best in children, and do so much to brighten and uplift the lives of those who swarm in the poorest districts of our great cities, that we hope they have come to stay. These books should do much to ensure this. Not their least charm is the beautiful and copious illustrations representing the several movements in the various classes and games. 

Seven Good Annuals. A really reliable annual is an indispensable companion. We have seven of these now before us, any one of which may be fairly described as a “hardy perennial.' 

No student of affairs can afford to be without either HAZELL (3s. 6d.) or WHITAKER (2s. 6d.). Both have long been old and valued friends, and the present numbers fully sustain their well-established reputation. 

Their general features are too well known to need description, and like good wine " they need no "bush." Hazell's has some new features for the present year-a better classification of related subjects and signed 66

PROGRESS 

articles by experts on topics of the day-both of which add to its value. 

The new features in Whitaker are "A Review of Social Progress during the last 50 years," an article full of just those facts and figures most often needed for handy reference. There are also articles on The 

Break-up of the Poor Law" and "The Children's Charter." THE LOCAL GOVERNMENT ANNUAL (1s. 6d.) now reaches its nineteenth year, during which time it has seen many changes, and will probably see greater in the near future, but it may always be relied upon to keep up to date. As before, its main portion is the Directory, giving the names and addresses of the chief officials of such local authorities as are under the L.G.B. It contains also the most recent information relating to baths, washhouses, etc., with a list of parks and open spaces in 

London. The CO-OPERATIVE ANNUAL has its usual full tables giving the most recent statistics of the progress of the Co-operative movement, and, as in former years, there are special articles on some of the more prominent social movements of the day. In the present number an article by Mr. and Mrs. Sidney Webb on "A National Crusade against Destitution " naturally takes the first place, but others on "Pensions and the 

Poor Law," by Mr.W. M. J. Williams; on "French Market Gardening," 

by Mr. E. Gioskoy; and on "The Place of a Peasantry in Modern 

Civilisation,” are important contributions to the better understanding of subjects of present interest.

THE ENGLISH WOMAN'S YEAR BOOK (A. and C. Black. 

2s. 6d.) is a new and greatly improved edition of a well known and useful book of reference. It has not only been completely revised, but also in many parts re-arranged. The aim of the publishers has been to give greater simplicity—an important matter in a work containing such an enormous mass of information. This has been done by adding new headings and a wide system of cross references to facilitate research. 

Much new matter has been added to the Science and Art sections, and the section devoted to Music is altogether new. A Directory of the names and addresses of women who are experts in special subjects is a valuable 

THE SCOTTISH TEMPERANCE ANNUAL (IS. and Is. 6d.), now entering on its twelfth year, confines itself—as its name implies--to a record of Temperance work and statistics. These, however, are not confined to 

Scotland, but embrace also the countries of Europe and America. For all such information this is the best book of reference. 

We are glad to welcome the first number of the CATHOLIC SOCIAL YEAR 

BOOK (Catholic Truth Society. 6d.). Social work among the Catholics has developed so rapidly in recent years that it is quite time that its work should be recorded in a form convenient for reference. This is supplied in its new Year Book, in which are recorded the progress of Catholic Social Study in England and the various phases of Catholic social action, the relation of such action to national and municipal organisations, together with Bibliographies, etc. Catholic Social workers have long recognised the work done by the British Institute of Social Service, and we find in the first number of the Year Book a warm acknowledgment of our services.

Advertisements EIGHT REASONS WHY YOU SHOULD SUPPORT THE WOMEN'S INDUSTRIAL COUNCIL. 

I. 

It unites in common action men and women who are interested in the industrial welfare of women and children. 2. 

It is non-party and unsectarian, and can therefore, on industrial questions, help and claim help from all parties in the State. On the Executive, as on the Council, there are representatives of all political parties and of various denominations. 

It keeps its members informed of all events that concern the industrial life of women and children.

This is done through the quarterly organ, the Women's Industrial News, and the pamphlets on industrial subjects issued from time to time. 

3. It collects and publishes accurate information concerning the industrial life of women. 4. 

It has published two reports on the Home Industries of Women in London, 1897 and 1907; also reports on individual trades such as Laundry Work, Millinery, Tailoring, Jewellery, etc. It is now making an inquiry into the Industrial Employment of 

Married Women and Widows. 

It considers and frequently takes action on all such legislation as will further the industrial welfare of women and children, by promoting bills, giving evidence and sending deputations as occasion requires. 

E.G., the Home Industries Bill, the Factory and Workshop Amendment Act, the draft Order re Florists' Assistants, the formation of the Women's Work Committee (Central Unemployed Body). 

It also helps industrial women to secure their legal rights. 

E.G., the following reported cases: Davies v. Lipton, Butler v. Whiteley, Daigle v. I.G.O. Co., and others. 

5. It organises representative Conferences on industrial questions which concern women and children. 6. 7. 

E.G., The Conference on the Training of Girls as Children's Nurses, 1904; the 

Conference on Unemployment of Women dependent on their own earnings, 1907;

The Conference on the Industrial Training of Women and Girls, 1908; and the 

Conference on Underground Workrooms, 1909.

It suggests useful experiments in industrial education. 

E.G., Trade Schools for Girls, adopted by the Education Committee, L.C.C., and the scheme the Council is at present working at for Training Girls as Children's 

Nurses. 

It initiates organisations likely to increase the industrial welfare of women and children. 

E.G., the Club's Industrial Association; the Girls' Club Library; the A.T.C., an

Association for Trained Domestic Workers. 

The Committee on Wage Earning Children and the Apprenticeship and Skilled Employment Association have developed from the Council's activities in these directions. 

8. It provides lectures on industrial questions for any audience in any part of the kingdom.

A list of more than 150 lectures is published each year. 

A sum of four shillings per annum covers the minimum subscription of one shilling to the Council and entitles the subscriber to receive quarterly the "Women's Industrial News" and all other publications as they appear.

Specimen copies of the Annual Report, the "Women's Industrial News," the Lecture List, and an eight-page leaflet called "What the Council is and does," may be obtained on application to the Secre tarv, Miss WYATT PAPWORTH, M.A., 7, John Street, Adelphi, Strand, W.C.

Garden City is Not 

Ludgate Circus; Norton Way, Letchworth, is not The Strand; yet many printing commissions find their way to Garden City Press from London, and numerous other places. 

There are two main reasons.

First Reason: It Is So Easy to send Instructions by post. We desire YOUR 

particular attention to this point. Write your instructions briefly, succinctly-Drop them into the nearest post box. There is no further trouble. It is possible to misapprehend verbal instructions. If written they may be referred to again and again, and mistakes avoided. No matter where you live, the post may be trusted to bring your letter promptly. Send us orders by post for die-stamped notepaper, admirably executed on our own premises; for circulars, cards, catalogues, books, or whatever may be needed. If an interview is necessary a request by postcard will ensure an immediate call from our representative.

Second Reason: Our Productions are GOOD. It is not necessary to emphasise this point. It is already well known. 

Garden City Press Ld. Letchworth
